- Quad test: 15–20 weeks (second trimester)
- CVS: 10–13 weeks (diagnostic, not screening)
- Amniocentesis: 15–18 weeks (diagnostic)
- CRL: most accurate dating in first trimester
- Uterine artery Doppler at 11–14 weeks: predicts early-onset pre-eclampsia
- Additional 300 kcal in 2nd trimester (not 1st or "throughout")
- Anaemia: mild 10–10.9; moderate 7–9.9; severe < 7 g/dL
- Thalassaemia screening: MCV ≤ 80 fL first; then HPLC to confirm
- GCT for GDM: 24–28 weeks

**Fetal surveillance:**
- Reactive NST: ≥2 accelerations, ≥15 bpm, ≥15 sec, in 20 min
- Non-reactive NST in 20 min → extend to 40 min (fetal sleep)
- BPP parameters: NST, breathing, movements, tone, AFV (each 0 or 2; max 10)
- BPP parameter lost LAST in hypoxia: fetal tone
- BPP parameter lost FIRST: NST
- Investigation of choice for IUGR: umbilical artery Doppler
- Absent EDF: severe placental insufficiency → expedite delivery ≥ 32–34 wks
- Reversed EDF: immediate delivery regardless of gestation
- Most ominous Doppler in IUGR: reversed a-wave in ductus venosus
- Brain-sparing: CPR = MCA-PI / UA-PI < 1.08

**Preterm and tocolysis:**
- First-line tocolytic (WHO/RCOG): nifedipine (calcium channel blocker)
- Atosiban: oxytocin/V1a receptor antagonist; Europe preferred; IV
- Betamethasone for: fetal lung maturity + IVH + PVL (NOT PPH)
- Cervical insufficiency: recurrent painless mid-trimester loss → McDonald cerclage 12–14 wks
- Bishop ≥ 6: favourable cervix (ready for induction)
- Bishop < 6: ripen first (PGE1/PGE2/Foley)

**Rhesus:**
- Kleihauer-Betke: measures fetal RBCs in maternal blood (quantifies FMH)
- Indirect Coombs: measures maternal antibodies (for sensitised mothers)
- Anti-D RAADP single dose: 1500 IU at 28 weeks
- Anti-D two-dose: 500 IU at 28 + 34 weeks
- Postnatal anti-D: 500 IU within 72 hours

### Top Traps List
1. NST criteria: all THREE numbers (15 bpm × 15 sec × 2 times in 20 min)
2. Non-reactive NST → extend to 40 min FIRST (don't jump to C-section)
3. BPP tone lost LAST (not first)
4. Betamethasone does NOT prevent PPH
5. Kleihauer-Betke = counts fetal cells (not antibodies)
6. NT timing = 11–13 weeks (not 8–10, not 18–20)
7. Quad test = 15–20 weeks (second trimester)
8. CVS = diagnostic, not screening; earliest invasive test = 10–13 wks
9. Uterine artery Doppler at 14 wks → early-onset pre-eclampsia (not late-onset)
10. CPR < 1 = brain-sparing (MCA vasodilation + high UA resistance)
11. WHO ANC 2016 = 8 contacts (not 4)
12. Thalassaemia screening = MCV first, then HPLC (not HPLC first)

### High-Yield Comparison Table: Invasive vs. Screening Tests

| Test | Type | Timing | What it detects |
|------|------|--------|-----------------|
| NT scan | Screening (USG) | 11–13 wks | Down syndrome, aneuploidies |
| Double marker | Screening (serum+USG) | 11–13 wks | Down syndrome risk |
| Triple marker | Screening (serum) | 15–20 wks | Down, Edwards, NTDs |
| Quad test | Screening (serum) | 15–20 wks | Down syndrome (better sensitivity) |
| CVS | Diagnostic (invasive) | 10–13 wks | Karyotype, gene mutations |
| Amniocentesis | Diagnostic (invasive) | 15–18 wks | Karyotype, AFP, biochemistry |
| NIPT/cfDNA | Screening (blood) | ≥ 10 wks | Aneuploidies (high sensitivity) |

### High-Yield Table: Doppler Findings Severity in IUGR

| Doppler Finding | Significance | Management |
|----------------|-------------|-----------|
| Elevated UA S/D ratio | Mild-moderate insufficiency | Intensive surveillance |
| Absent EDF (AEDF) | Severe insufficiency | Deliver ≥ 34 wks; steroids if < 34 wks |
| Reversed EDF (REDF) | Extreme insufficiency | Immediate delivery regardless of gestation |
| MCA low PI (brain-sparing) | CNS redistribution | Alert; combined with UA Doppler (CPR) |
| CPR < 1 | Brain-sparing present | High-risk, plan delivery |
| Reversed DV a-wave | Right heart failure / imminent fetal death | IMMEDIATE delivery |

### Most Volatile Facts
- The two-dose anti-D protocol (500 IU at 28+34 weeks) vs. single-dose (1500 IU at 28 weeks) — both correct per different protocols; know both
- The exact BPP score threshold for delivery: ≥ 6 at term = deliver; at < 34 weeks, score of 6 = repeat in 24 hours (gestational age matters)
- Nifedipine vs. atosiban: NEET-PG answers depend on which guideline is cited; when no guideline is specified, nifedipine (WHO/global) is safer
- Uterine artery Doppler also predicts early IUGR — the question specifically says "pre-eclampsia" when asked at 14 weeks

---

## 🔗 Linked Clinical Topics from the ANC Question Bank

The following questions appeared in the ANC question bank but test adjacent gynae and obstetric medicine topics. They are integrated here because they frequently appear alongside ANC questions in NEET-PG papers.

---

### Ovarian Dermoid (Mature Cystic Teratoma) with Torsion

Mature cystic teratomas (dermoid cysts) contain all three germ layers. The *Rokitansky protuberance* (dermoid plug) is the ectodermal nodule visible on ultrasound — a pathognomonic finding. Dermoids are the most common ovarian tumour in reproductive-age women and the most commonly twisted ovarian cyst (due to their semi-solid composition making them heavier and prone to torsion).

**Q282 — Mature cystic teratoma (Rokitansky protuberance)**

A 30-year-old with Rokitansky's protuberance on imaging presents with acute abdomen. Diagnosis?

- A. Torsion of subserous fibroid
- B. Twisted ovarian cyst (dermoid)
- C. Ruptured ectopic pregnancy
- D. Ruptured ovarian cyst

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Twisted ovarian cyst (mature cystic teratoma)** — Rokitansky protuberance (dermoid plug) = ectodermal nodule in mature cystic teratoma. Torsion → acute abdomen; urgent laparoscopy.

</details>

**Key link to ANC:** Ovarian cysts discovered during pregnancy (often on the NT scan or anomaly scan) are frequently dermoids. Torsion risk increases in pregnancy due to displacement by the growing uterus. Management in pregnancy: laparoscopic cystectomy, ideally in second trimester.

---

### Endometrioma: The "Ground Glass" Cyst

Endometriomas are ovarian cysts containing old ("chocolate") blood from ectopic endometrial tissue. On ultrasound, they show a characteristic *homogeneous ground-glass* echogenicity (due to hemosiderin-laden fluid). They are relevant to ANC because endometriosis is a common cause of infertility and subfertility — patients who conceive with endometriosis require monitoring.

**Q1023 — Endometrioma**

A 35-year-old woman presents with chronic lower abdominal pain that worsens during menstruation. An ultrasound shows a cystic ovarian mass with a "ground glass" appearance. Which condition is most likely?

- A. Intramural fibroid
- B. Adenomyoma
- C. Endometrioma
- D. Myomatous polyp

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Endometrioma** — Ground-glass echogenicity on USG = endometrioma (chocolate cyst). Cyclic pain worsening with menstruation is the classic history.

</details>

---

### Genital Tuberculosis: A Mimic of PID

Genital tuberculosis (TB) is caused by *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* spreading haematogenously from a primary focus (usually lungs). It classically presents with *subacute/chronic* lower abdominal pain + intermenstrual bleeding + minimal or no discharge. The key diagnostic clue: despite a history of multiple sexual partners (which points toward PID), TB causes more indolent, chronic symptoms with minimal purulent discharge. Gonorrhoea and chlamydia cause acute PID with purulent discharge.

**Q995 — Genital TB vs. other lower abdominal infections**

A 28-year-old woman with a history of multiple sexual partners presents with lower abdominal pain for 1 month, minimal discharge, and intermenstrual bleeding. Most likely cause?

- A. Herpes simplex
- B. Genital tuberculosis
- C. Candida
- D. Neisseria gonorrhoea

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Genital tuberculosis** — Chronic (1 month) course + minimal discharge + intermenstrual bleeding in India = genital TB until proven otherwise. Gonorrhoea causes acute purulent PID; candida causes discharge without pain; herpes causes vesicular ulcers.

</details>

**Why D (gonorrhoea) is the trap:** Multiple sexual partners = STI risk = PID from gonorrhoea is the immediate thought. But gonorrhoea causes *acute* PID (days, not months) with *copious purulent discharge*. The chronicity (1 month) and minimal discharge point to TB.

---

### Anticoagulation in Pregnancy: Warfarin → LMWH Near Term

Warfarin crosses the placenta and is teratogenic in the first trimester (warfarin embryopathy) and causes fetal haemorrhage in the third trimester. For women with mechanical heart valves who require anticoagulation throughout pregnancy, the general approach is:

- **First trimester:** LMWH (to avoid warfarin embryopathy) or continue warfarin if dose < 5 mg/day
- **Second trimester:** Warfarin is acceptable (fetal risk lower)
- **After 36 weeks:** Switch to LMWH (to allow reversal before delivery and avoid fetal intracranial haemorrhage at delivery)

**Q1028 — Mechanical heart valve at 37 weeks on warfarin**

A 37-weeks pregnant woman with a mechanical prosthetic heart valve on warfarin presents for routine antenatal check-up. She is asymptomatic. Next step?

- A. Switch to LMWH
- B. Immediate induction of labour
- C. Immediate LSCS
- D. Continue the same

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Switch to LMWH** — At 36–37 weeks, warfarin should be switched to LMWH to allow safe neuraxial anaesthesia and to prevent fetal intracranial haemorrhage at the time of delivery. Delivery is planned, not emergent.

</details>

---

### Heavy Menstrual Bleeding: When to Biopsy

In any woman with abnormal uterine bleeding, endometrial thickness on ultrasound guides the need for biopsy. The threshold varies by menopausal status:
- **Postmenopausal:** > 4–5 mm → mandatory biopsy (to exclude endometrial cancer)
- **Perimenopausal/premenopausal with abnormal bleeding:** > 10–12 mm or persistent/recurrent bleeding → biopsy indicated

**Q1044 — Heavy menstrual bleeding with thick endometrium**

46-year-old with HMB, endometrial thickness 16 mm on USG. Next best step?

- A. Hysterectomy
- B. Endometrial biopsy
- C. HPV testing
- D. Progesterone therapy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Endometrial biopsy** — Endometrial thickness > 4–5 mm in postmenopausal or abnormal bleeding mandates biopsy to exclude endometrial carcinoma. Never treat (hysterectomy or hormones) before histological diagnosis.

</details>

**Clinical logic:** At 46 years (perimenopausal), with HMB and an endometrium of 16 mm, the priority is to *exclude endometrial cancer*. Jumping to hysterectomy (A) without a diagnosis is wrong — biopsy must come first. Progesterone therapy (D) without ruling out cancer is dangerous.

---

### Menopause: Vasomotor Symptoms and HRT

Menopausal hot flushes are caused by oestrogen withdrawal acting on hypothalamic thermoregulatory centres. FSH > 40 IU/L confirms ovarian failure.

**Q385 — Menopause: vasomotor symptoms**

A 51-year-old woman with hot flushes and night sweats for 8 months after her last menstrual period. FSH is 60 IU/L. No contraindications. Most effective treatment?

- A. Low-dose SSRIs (venlafaxine)
- B. Tibolone
- C. Combined oestrogen-progestogen HRT (systemic)
- D. Black cohosh supplements

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Combined oestrogen-progestogen HRT (systemic)** — Systemic HRT is the most effective treatment for menopausal vasomotor symptoms. All other options (SSRIs, tibolone, phytoestrogens) are second-line for women who cannot take HRT.

</details>

**Note:** Women with an intact uterus must receive *combined* oestrogen + progestogen HRT (progestogen protects the endometrium from unopposed oestrogen → endometrial cancer risk). Women post-hysterectomy can take oestrogen alone.




---

# Chapter 3: Hypertensive Disorders of Pregnancy, Medical Complications & Antepartum Haemorrhage

---

## Big Picture First

If there is one chapter that has been the examiner's favourite across every single NEET-PG / INI-CET paper from 2015 to 2025, this is it. Hypertensive disorders of pregnancy (HDP), gestational diabetes, hepatitis, cholestasis, cardiac disease, and antepartum haemorrhage collectively account for roughly **12–18 questions per paper** — sometimes more. That is not a guess; look at any year's paper and you will count them. This chapter is your single highest-return investment in OBG.

The examiner in this topic is not testing whether you can recite definitions. The examiner is testing whether you can **navigate nuance under pressure**: Is this pre-eclampsia or eclampsia? Is this HELLP or AFLP? Is this Mg toxicity or a seizure? Which antihypertensive is safe and which kills the fetus? Why does smoking *protect* against pre-eclampsia? These are the exact question stems you will face.

The most misunderstood areas, based on a decade of PYQ analysis: (1) The Mg sulfate toxicity sequence — students confuse the order of signs; (2) GDM diagnostic cut-offs — IADPSG vs DIPSI vs WHO; students learn one set and mix up the others; (3) The APH differential — vasa praevia is the dark horse that kills the fetus within minutes, and questions exploit the fact that students don't instinctively distinguish it from placenta praevia; (4) Abruptio — the concealed form is more dangerous but less visible, which is a perfect examiner trap.

The tricky differential that appears every year: **HELLP vs AFLP vs viral hepatitis vs obstetric cholestasis**. They all involve the liver, all occur in the third trimester, and the examiner knows you will panic and confuse them.

---

## Question Pattern Map

**What the examiner loves to ask:**
- "Drug of choice for eclampsia / chronic hypertension in pregnancy / acute severe hypertension"
- "First sign of Mg toxicity" (DTR loss — this appears almost every year)
- "Investigation of choice for placenta praevia" (TVUS — appears every year)
- "Comparison: concealed vs revealed abruption"
- "GDM diagnosis: which cut-off?" (IADPSG vs DIPSI)
- "Which cardiac condition is absolutely contraindicated in pregnancy?"
- "Which hepatitis has highest maternal mortality?"
- "Drug of choice for ICP (cholestasis)"
- "Smoking as a protective factor" (counterintuitive — classic trap)

**Common examiner traps:**
- Giving a vignette of concealed abruption with *no bleeding* and asking for the diagnosis — students panic because they expect blood
- Offering "emergency LSCS" for a patient with pre-eclampsia at 33 weeks who needs *medical stabilisation first*
- Mixing up GDM screening thresholds (DIPSI = 140, IADPSG FPG = 92)
- Asking about vasa praevia where the correct clue is bleeding *at the moment of membrane rupture* + immediate fetal distress
- "Smoking protects against pre-eclampsia" — this is NOT a fabrication; it is a real, reproducible epidemiological finding

---

## Core Concept Deep Dive

### Section 1: Pre-eclampsia — Diagnosis, Severity, Management

**The concept:** Pre-eclampsia = new-onset hypertension (BP ≥140/90 mmHg on two readings ≥4 hours apart) after 20 weeks, with either proteinuria (≥300 mg/24h or ≥2+ dipstick) OR severe features without proteinuria (AKI, thrombocytopenia <100,000, impaired liver function, pulmonary oedema, new severe headache, visual disturbance).

Pre-eclampsia with **severe features** = BP ≥160/110 mmHg persistently, OR the above organ-specific criteria regardless of BP.

**Eclampsia** = pre-eclampsia + seizures. No pre-existing history of epilepsy. The most important immediate management question is: **what drug do you give?**

---

> **MCQ Encounter — Q524 (PYQ)**
>
> *Drug of choice for management of eclamptic seizures is:*
> - A. Oral magnesium sulphate
> - B. Intravenous magnesium sulphate
> - C. Oral phenytoin
> - D. Intravenous phenytoin
>
> **Answer: B — IV MgSO4**
>
> **Why students get trapped:** "Magnesium" sounds nutritional and gentle. Phenytoin sounds like a "real" anticonvulsant. Students default to phenytoin out of habit from neurology.
>
> **Why IV MgSO4 wins:** The Magpie Trial (2002) showed MgSO4 reduces eclampsia risk by 58% vs placebo and is superior to both phenytoin and diazepam. Its mechanism is unique — it is an NMDA receptor antagonist, not a traditional anticonvulsant. It also causes cerebral vasodilation, reducing cerebrovascular spasm.
>
> **Loading dose:** 4 g IV over 5–10 min. Maintenance: 1–2 g/hr by infusion (Zuspan regimen) OR 10 g IM split dose + 5 g IM q4h (Pritchard regimen).
>
> **Key takeaway:** IV MgSO4 = drug of choice for both seizure treatment AND prophylaxis (Q560) in severe pre-eclampsia.

---

**Examiner Note: Mg Toxicity — The Sequence You Must Know**

This appears nearly every year, phrased as either "first sign" or "sequence of toxicity."

| Serum Mg Level | Clinical Sign |
|---|---|
| 4–7 mEq/L | Therapeutic range (seizure prophylaxis) |
| 7–10 mEq/L | **Loss of deep tendon reflexes (FIRST sign)** |
| 10–13 mEq/L | Respiratory depression |
| >13–15 mEq/L | Cardiac arrest |

**Monitor with the triad:** Patellar reflex (present = safe), RR ≥12/min, Urine output ≥25 mL/hr.

**Antidote:** Calcium gluconate 1 g IV (10 mL of 10% solution) — given SLOWLY over 10 min.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q268 & Q659 (PYQ)**
>
> *Pregnant woman on MgSO4 develops loss of DTRs and respiratory depression. Antidote?*
>
> **Answer: Calcium gluconate IV** — Calcium antagonises magnesium at the neuromuscular junction.
>
> **Why students get trapped:** They say "calcium chloride" — this is wrong. Calcium chloride is for cardiac arrest/ACLS, not for Mg reversal in obstetrics. The OBG answer is always **calcium gluconate**.

---

**Pre-eclampsia Management: Timing of Delivery**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q514 (PYQ)**
>
> *Primigravida with PIH at 32 weeks, BP 150/100 mmHg, controlled on medication, no complications. Pregnancy should be terminated at:*
> - A. 40 weeks  B. 37 weeks  C. 36 weeks  D. 34 weeks
>
> **Answer: B — 37 completed weeks**
>
> **The rule:** Gestational hypertension or pre-eclampsia *without* severe features → deliver at **37 weeks**. With severe features at ≥34 weeks → deliver after stabilisation. With severe features at <34 weeks → steroids + close monitoring, aim for brief prolongation if possible.

---

**Antihypertensive Therapy**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q630 (PYQ)**
>
> *Drug of choice for chronic hypertension in pregnancy:*
>
> **Answer: D — Methyldopa**
>
> Methyldopa has the longest safety record (decades of data). It is the first-line choice.

| Drug | Status in Pregnancy |
|---|---|
| **Methyldopa** | First-line (chronic hypertension) |
| **Labetalol** | First-line (acute severe hypertension, IV) |
| **Nifedipine** | Alternative (oral, acute and chronic) |
| **Hydralazine** | IV, acute severe hypertension |
| **ACE inhibitors** (enalapril, ramipril) | **ABSOLUTELY CONTRAINDICATED** — fetal renal dysplasia, oligohydramnios, death |
| Atenolol | Relatively contraindicated — IUGR |
| Sodium nitroprusside | Avoid if possible — cyanide toxicity in fetus |

**Examiner trap:** Enalapril is offered as an option in a drug-for-PIH question. Students recognise it as an antihypertensive and choose it. Wrong. ACE inhibitors = Category D/X in 2nd and 3rd trimesters.

---

**Not a Risk Factor for Pre-eclampsia:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q471 (PYQ 2015)**
>
> *Which is NOT a risk factor for gestational hypertension: Obesity / Smoking / Primigravida / Factor V Leiden?*
>
> **Answer: B — Smoking**
>
> This is the paradigm counterintuitive fact of HDP. Smoking reduces placental oxidative stress and endothelial dysfunction via nicotine-mediated vasoconstriction *and* reduced trophoblast invasion — paradoxically reducing pre-eclampsia risk. This does not justify smoking; it simply predicts the "NOT a risk factor" answer type.
>
> **Genuine risk factors:** Primigravida, multiple pregnancy, previous PE, chronic hypertension, diabetes, obesity, renal disease, SLE/APLA, extremes of age, Factor V Leiden (thrombophilia).

---

**Complications of Pre-eclampsia:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q482 (PYQ 2016)**
>
> *Complications of pre-eclampsia are all EXCEPT:*
> - A. Post-datism  B. DIC  C. Blindness  D. None of the above
>
> **Answer: A — Post-datism** (prolonged pregnancy is NOT a complication of pre-eclampsia).
>
> Genuine complications: abruption, DIC, HELLP, eclampsia, cortical blindness (hypertensive encephalopathy/occipital lobe oedema), renal failure, hepatic rupture, IUGR, IUFD.

---

### Section 2: HELLP Syndrome — Diagnosis and Pitfalls

**The concept:** HELLP = Haemolysis + Elevated Liver enzymes + Low Platelets. It is a severe variant of pre-eclampsia (though 15% occur without classical HDP features). The triad is what the examiner tests.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q266 & Q660 (PYQ)**
>
> *Pregnant woman with elevated LDH, low platelets, abnormal LFTs:*
>
> **Answer: A — HELLP syndrome**
>
> **Diagnostic criteria (Tennessee Classification):**
> - LDH >600 IU/L (haemolysis marker)
> - AST/ALT >70 IU/L (liver)
> - Platelets <100,000/µL (thrombocytopenia)

**The differentials:**

| Feature | HELLP | AFLP | ICP | Viral Hepatitis |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Trimester | 3rd | 3rd (late) | 3rd | Any |
| Jaundice | Mild/absent | Prominent | Mild/absent | Prominent |
| Pruritus | Absent | Absent | **Hallmark** | May be present |
| Platelets | **Low** | Low (late) | Normal | Normal |
| PT/APTT | Normal initially | **Prolonged** | Normal | Prolonged (severe) |
| Bile acids | Normal/slightly raised | Normal | **Very high** | Normal |
| Key fact | Pre-eclampsia context | LCHAD deficiency fetus | UDCA treatment | HEV = highest mortality |

---

> **MCQ Encounter — Q269 (PYQ)**
>
> *Acute fatty liver of pregnancy typically occurs in:*
>
> **Answer: C — Third trimester** (late, typically 35–37 weeks)
>
> AFLP pathophysiology: fetus carries LCHAD (long-chain 3-hydroxyacyl CoA dehydrogenase) deficiency → long-chain fatty acids accumulate in maternal liver → microvesicular steatosis → liver failure.
>
> **Treatment: IMMEDIATE DELIVERY** regardless of gestational age. Do not wait.

---

### Section 3: Gestational Diabetes Mellitus (GDM)

**Why students get confused:** There are THREE sets of diagnostic criteria taught in Indian exams — IADPSG, DIPSI, and WHO/overt diabetes cut-offs. These are NOT interchangeable.

**The framework:**

| Criterion | Test | Cut-off | Fasting? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **IADPSG** (gold standard, 75g OGTT) | FPG ≥92 mg/dL | Single abnormal value = GDM | Yes |
| | 1-hr ≥180 mg/dL | | |
| | 2-hr ≥153 mg/dL | | |
| **DIPSI** (India MoHFW) | 2-hr PG ≥140 mg/dL | After 75g glucose, non-fasting | **No fasting needed** |
| **Overt DM** | FPG ≥126 mg/dL or RBG ≥200 or HbA1c ≥6.5% | At any gestation | — |

> **MCQ Encounter — Q382 (PYQ)**
>
> *DIPSI test: A 26-year-old at 24–28 weeks given 75g glucose non-fasting. GDM if 2-hour PG ≥ ___?*
>
> **Answer: B — 140 mg/dL**
>
> **Why students get trapped:** They remember 153 from IADPSG (2-hr cut-off) and apply it to DIPSI. But DIPSI is a non-fasting test with a different threshold. Remember: **DIPSI = 140, IADPSG 2-hr = 153.**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q272 & Q545 (PYQ)**
>
> *Overt diabetes threshold in pregnancy (fasting PG):*
>
> **Answer: ≥126 mg/dL** — This is the WHO criteria for diagnosing pre-existing (overt) DM at any gestation, distinct from GDM cut-offs.

**Mechanism of GDM:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q562 (PYQ)**
>
> *Primary mechanism of gestational diabetes:*
>
> **Answer: B — Placental lactogen-induced peripheral insulin resistance**
>
> hPL, cortisol, progesterone, and oestrogen are all insulin antagonists that rise progressively from mid-pregnancy. This "diabetogenic stress" unmasks GDM in women with limited beta-cell reserve. Note: GDM is NOT autoimmune (that's Type 1 DM).

**Pre-gestational diabetes and fetal risk:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q633 (PYQ)**
>
> *Pre-gestational diabetes counselling about fetal anomaly risk:*
>
> **Answer: C — Four-fold higher risk** of congenital anomalies
>
> This is proportional to periconceptional HbA1c. The highest-risk anomalies: sacral agenesis (pathognomonic), VSD, neural tube defects. GDM (onset in pregnancy) does NOT increase structural anomaly risk — the anomalies form in 1st trimester, before GDM typically manifests.

---

### Section 4: Cardiac Disease in Pregnancy

**The framework:** The key question is always "which condition is most dangerous / absolutely contraindicated?"

> **MCQ Encounter — Q383 & Q699 (PYQ)**
>
> *Which cardiac condition is an ABSOLUTE contraindication to pregnancy? / Which has highest maternal mortality?*
>
> **Answer: Eisenmenger's syndrome** — Maternal mortality 30–50%
>
> **Why:** Pregnancy causes systemic vasodilation (progesterone effect), which in a right-to-left shunt patient worsens hypoxaemia and causes pulmonary vascular crisis. The shunt reverses further → refractory hypoxia → death. Advise **termination** if already pregnant.

**NYHA Classification and Risk:**

| NYHA Class | Description | Maternal Mortality |
|---|---|---|
| I–II | Asymptomatic or mild symptoms | <1% |
| III | Symptomatic on ordinary activity | 5–15% |
| IV | Symptomatic at rest | 25–50% |

**Peripartum cardiomyopathy:** New-onset heart failure in the last month of pregnancy or within 5 months postpartum. Treatment: diuretics, ACE inhibitors (postpartum only!), beta-blockers, anticoagulation if EF <35%. Can recur in subsequent pregnancies.

**The mitral stenosis trap:** Mitral stenosis is the most common rheumatic valvular lesion in pregnancy in India. The increased blood volume of pregnancy is particularly dangerous with MS (mitral stenosis) — the fixed cardiac output through a narrowed valve cannot accommodate the demand → acute pulmonary oedema.

---

### Section 5: Hepatitis in Pregnancy

> **MCQ Encounter — Q700 (PYQ)**
>
> *Which hepatitis has highest maternal mortality?*
>
> **Answer: D — Hepatitis E** — Maternal mortality 15–25% in 3rd trimester; fulminant hepatic failure is the mechanism.

| Hepatitis Type | Pregnancy Feature | Vertical Transmission | Key Fact |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hep E** | Highest maternal mortality (15–25%) | Yes | Faeco-oral, epidemic in India |
| **Hep B** | Usually no increased maternal morbidity | High (if HBeAg+) | HBsAg = first serological marker (Q270) |
| **Hep A** | No increased risk | Rare | Resolves; no chronicity |
| **Hep C** | Low risk | ~5% | No vaccine |

> **MCQ Encounter — Q270 (PYQ)**
>
> *Pregnant woman HBsAg positive at 14 weeks. True statement?*
>
> **Answer: C — HBsAg is the first serological marker**
>
> HBsAg appears 1–10 weeks post-exposure. Neonatal vaccination + HBIG at birth prevents 90% of vertical transmission. The confusion: students think "first marker = anti-HBc IgM" — that is the first marker of *acute infection* immune response; HBsAg is the first serological *antigen*.

---

### Section 6: Intrahepatic Cholestasis of Pregnancy (ICP)

**The concept:** ICP = pruritus in pregnancy (especially palms and soles), raised serum bile acids, elevated transaminases, NO rash, reverses postpartum.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q349 & Q547 (PYQ)**
>
> *Drug of choice for ICP:*
>
> **Answer: B — Ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA)**
>
> UDCA dose: 500 mg BD or 10–15 mg/kg/day. It reduces bile acid levels, relieves pruritus, and reduces fetal risk.
>
> **Why not cholestyramine?** Cholestyramine relieves pruritus but does NOT reduce bile acids or improve fetal outcomes. UDCA does both.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q536 (PYQ)**
>
> *Best diagnostic marker for ICP:*
>
> **Answer: C — Serum bile acids**
>
> ALP rises normally in pregnancy (placental ALP) and is NOT a reliable marker. Bile acids >40 µmol/L = significant risk; >100 µmol/L = very high risk of stillbirth.
>
> **Delivery timing:** 36–37 weeks when bile acids >40; some centres deliver at 35–36 if >100 µmol/L.

---

### Section 7: Antepartum Haemorrhage (APH) — The Big Three

APH = bleeding from the genital tract after 28 weeks (24 weeks in some guidelines) and before delivery of the baby.

**The three causes you must master:**

#### A. Placenta Praevia

**The concept:** Placenta implanted in the lower uterine segment, overlying or near the internal os.

**Classification (traditional):**
| Type | Relation to OS |
|---|---|
| Type I (lateral) | Encroaches lower segment |
| Type II (marginal) | Reaches edge of internal os |
| Type III (partial/incomplete) | Partially covers internal os |
| Type IV (complete/major) | Completely covers internal os |

**Classic presentation:** Painless, bright red, fresh bleeding, proportional to visible blood loss, soft non-tender uterus, abnormal lie/presentation common.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q345 (PYQ)**
>
> *Investigation of choice for suspected placenta praevia:*
>
> **Answer: C — Transvaginal ultrasound (TVUS)**
>
> TVUS is safe (probe stays >3 cm from cervix) and more accurate than transabdominal USG. **PV examination is absolutely contraindicated** until praevia is excluded — the "double set-up" examination (Q429) in OT is only for when you must examine but may need to proceed immediately to caesarean.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q477 & Q762 (PYQ 2015)**
>
> *Management of complete placenta praevia at 38 weeks without bleeding:*
>
> **Answer: C — Elective caesarean section**
>
> At ≥37 weeks, active delivery is mandatory. No bleeding = elective (planned) caesarean. Bleeding = emergency caesarean.
>
> The Macafee–Johnson regimen (expectant, hospitalise, transfuse, wait until 37 weeks) is appropriate for the patient with praevia diagnosed before 37 weeks without active bleeding.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q625 (PYQ)**
>
> *Placenta praevia found at 28 weeks — confirmatory scan at:*
>
> **Answer: A — 32 weeks** (then again at 36 weeks if persisting). Many cases "migrate" as the LUS develops.

**Placenta Accreta Spectrum:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q814 (PYQ 2016) & Q655 (PYQ)**
>
> *G3P2 with previous 2 LSCS + anterior placenta praevia — highest risk of:*
>
> **Answer: A — Placenta accreta**
>
> Prior LSCS scar + anterior praevia = Nitabuch's layer deficiency → villi invade myometrium. Risk with 2 previous scars ≈ 40%. This patient needs multidisciplinary planning, possible caesarean hysterectomy.

#### B. Abruptio Placentae

**The concept:** Premature separation of a normally situated placenta. Pain (unlike praevia), retroplacental haematoma.

**The classic trap — concealed abruption:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q404 (PYQ)**
>
> *32-year-old multigravida at 36 weeks, sudden severe abdominal pain, "woody hard" uterus, NO visible bleeding, fetal distress. Type:*
>
> **Answer: B — Type II (concealed/severe) — complete separation with retroplacental haematoma**
>
> This is the trap: "no bleeding" does NOT mean no abruption. In concealed abruption, blood is trapped behind the placenta → the apparent blood loss massively underestimates true loss → DIC. Woody-hard uterus = haematomyometrium (Couvelaire uterus).

**Abruptio vs Placenta Praevia:**

| Feature | Placenta Praevia | Abruptio Placentae |
|---|---|---|
| Pain | **Absent** | **Present** (often severe) |
| Bleeding | **Bright red, fresh** | Dark red ± concealed |
| Uterus | Soft, non-tender | Tender, board-like |
| Shock | Proportional to blood | Disproportionate (concealed) |
| Cause | Abnormal implantation | Vascular disease, trauma, cocaine |
| Recurrence | Low | High (10–15%) |
| Fetal presentation | Abnormal (floating) | Normal lie |

**Risk factors for abruption:** Hypertension (most common), smoking, cocaine, prior abruption, PPROM, trauma, rapid uterine decompression (polyhydramnios).

#### C. Vasa Praevia

**The concept:** Fetal vessels traverse the membranes over the internal os, unsupported by Wharton's jelly, associated with velamentous cord insertion.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q478 & Q768 (PYQ 2015)**
>
> *Vasa praevia is associated with:*
>
> **Answer: B — Velamentous placenta**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q403 & Q493 (PYQ)**
>
> *Classic scenario: painless bleeding AT the moment of membrane rupture + immediate fetal bradycardia. Diagnosis?*
>
> **Answer: Vasa praevia with fetal vessel rupture**
>
> The key distinguishing feature: bleeding is **FETAL blood** — the fetus exsanguinates within minutes. Apt test (Ogita test) confirms fetal blood in vaginal bleeding. Management: immediate emergency caesarean. Antenatal diagnosis by TVUS + colour Doppler → planned caesarean at 34–36 weeks with steroids.

---

### Section 8: Medical Complications — Drugs and Pregnancy

> **MCQ Encounter — Q408 & Q578 (PYQ)**
>
> *Which drug does NOT cross the placenta?*
>
> **Answer: A — Heparin** (large, charged molecule; cannot cross). Warfarin, morphine, naloxone = all cross freely.
>
> **LMWH advantage (Q384):** Does not cross placenta, predictable pharmacokinetics, less HIT, less osteoporosis vs UFH. Preferred for DVT prophylaxis in pregnancy.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q307 & Q701 (PYQ)**
>
> *Recurrent pregnancy loss + prolonged APTT / APLA. Treatment:*
>
> **Answer: Aspirin + LMWH** — Low-dose aspirin (75 mg) from pre-conception + prophylactic LMWH once intrauterine pregnancy confirmed.
>
> **Why the APTT is prolonged in APS:** Lupus anticoagulant interferes with phospholipid-dependent coagulation tests *in vitro* — paradoxically, patients are pro-thrombotic *in vivo*.

---

## Bias Removal Section

**Top traps in this chapter:**

1. **Mg toxicity sequence:** Students write "respiratory depression is first." Wrong. Loss of DTRs is first (7–10 mEq/L). Respiratory depression is second. Cardiac arrest is last.

2. **GDM thresholds:** DIPSI uses 140 (non-fasting), IADPSG 2-hr uses 153 (fasting OGTT). These are NOT the same test. Don't mix them.

3. **UDCA vs cholestyramine for ICP:** Both relieve itch but only UDCA reduces bile acids and fetal risk. UDCA = drug of choice.

4. **Enalapril/ACE inhibitors:** Students see "antihypertensive" and choose it. In pregnancy = absolutely contraindicated. The answer is always methyldopa (chronic) or labetalol/hydralazine (acute).

5. **Placenta praevia investigation:** Students write "transabdominal USG." Wrong. TVUS is more accurate and safe.

6. **Smoking = protective:** Students assume this is a trick question and choose "smoking is a risk factor." Smoking genuinely reduces pre-eclampsia risk (though it causes IUGR, abruption, and other harms).

---

## Variations and Twists

**How examiners modify HDP questions:**
- The scenario gives you a 33-week patient with BP 180/110, headache, visual disturbance, and oliguria — then asks "NEXT step." Students jump to "LSCS." Wrong. **Stabilise first** (MgSO4 + antihypertensive), THEN deliver. Q484 illustrates this perfectly.
- "A woman develops DIC after abruption at 34 weeks. Most likely underlying mechanism?" → Thromboplastin release from retroplacental clot activates extrinsic pathway.
- They may offer "phenytoin for seizure prophylaxis in severe pre-eclampsia" — this was the old practice and is WRONG by current guidelines.

**GDM vignette twists:**
- They give HbA1c 6.7% at first prenatal visit → this is OVERT DM, not GDM (HbA1c ≥6.5% = overt DM; GDM is diagnosed only 2nd trimester onwards and uses OGTT).
- "GDM patient, best indicator of fetal compromise" → Non-stress test (NST) is the first-line test (Q657).

---

## Memory Engineering

**HELLP mnemonic:** **H**aemolysis – **E**levated liver enzymes – **L**ow **P**latelets

**Mg toxicity sequence — "DTR before RR before Arrest":**
- D = DTRs lost (first warning)
- R = Respiration stops
- A = Arrest (cardiac)
- Antidote = **Ca gluconate** (Calcium Counters Magnesium)

**APH causes — "The 3 Ps":**
- **P**lacenta praevia (low-lying placenta)
- **P**lacental abruption (separation)
- vasa **P**raevia (fetal vessel over os) — "tiny bleed, fatal for fetus"

**Hepatitis mortality in pregnancy — "E for Emergency":** Hepatitis **E** = Emergency = highest mortality (15–25%)

**Antihypertensives — safe vs banned:**
- Safe: **M**ethyldopa, **L**abetalol, **N**ifedipine, **H**ydralazine → "M.L.N.H" = "My Labour Needs Help"
- Banned: **ACE inhibitors** (enalapril, ramipril), **ARBs** — all nephrotoxic to fetus

**GDM thresholds — "DIPSI is 140; IADPSG FPG is 92 (lowest)"**

---

## Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### What the Examiner REALLY Wants

The examiner wants you to know three things cold: (1) the Mg toxicity sequence + antidote, (2) the APH differential (especially the vasa praevia trap), and (3) GDM diagnostic cut-offs.

### 20-Second Revision Bullets

- MgSO4 = drug of choice for eclampsia (both treatment and prophylaxis)
- First sign of Mg toxicity = loss of deep tendon reflexes; antidote = calcium gluconate 1g IV
- Mg therapeutic range = 4–7 mEq/L; toxic >7; respiratory paralysis >10; cardiac arrest >13
- Methyldopa = drug of choice for chronic hypertension in pregnancy
- ACE inhibitors (enalapril) = absolutely contraindicated in pregnancy
- Smoking = protective against pre-eclampsia (NOT a risk factor)
- DIPSI GDM cut-off = 140 mg/dL (non-fasting 75g 2-hr PG)
- IADPSG: FPG ≥92 / 1-hr ≥180 / 2-hr ≥153 (any ONE = GDM)
- Overt DM in pregnancy: FPG ≥126 / RBG ≥200 / HbA1c ≥6.5%
- HELLP = Haemolysis + Elevated liver enzymes + Low platelets (<100,000)
- HELLP vs AFLP: AFLP = more liver failure, LCHAD deficiency fetus, treat by immediate delivery
- ICP treatment = UDCA (not cholestyramine); best marker = serum bile acids (not ALP)
- Hepatitis E = highest maternal mortality (15–25%); HBsAg = first serological marker
- Eisenmenger's = absolute contraindication to pregnancy (>50% mortality)
- TVUS = investigation of choice for placenta praevia (NOT per-vaginal exam)
- Complete praevia at term = elective LSCS; no bleeding = elective; bleeding = emergency
- Vasa praevia = bleeding at ROM + immediate fetal distress = fetal blood loss = emergency LSCS
- Concealed abruption = woody hard uterus + no PV bleeding + fetal distress + DIC
- Heparin does NOT cross placenta; warfarin DOES
- APS treatment = aspirin + LMWH (not steroids)
- Pre-gestational diabetes = 4-fold increased fetal anomaly risk
- GDM does NOT increase structural anomaly risk (anomalies form in 1st trimester, before GDM manifests)

### Top 5 Examiner Traps in Chapter 3

1. "First sign of Mg toxicity" → DTR loss (NOT respiratory depression)
2. "Vasa praevia" scenario → bleeding after ROM + immediate fetal distress (NOT placenta praevia)
3. "Drug for PIH" options include enalapril → always wrong (ACE inhibitors = contraindicated)
4. "Smoking and pre-eclampsia" → smoking is PROTECTIVE (counterintuitive but tested repeatedly)
5. "Investigation for praevia" → TVUS (NOT transabdominal USG and NEVER PV examination)

---




---

# Chapter 4: Normal Labour, Intrapartum Care, Operative Delivery & Complications

---

## Big Picture First

Labour and delivery is where obstetrics becomes life-or-death in real time. It is also where the NEET-PG examiner feels most at home. Roughly **10–15 questions per paper** come directly from this chapter — stages of labour, partogram interpretation, PPH management, shoulder dystocia, instrumental delivery, episiotomy, breech manoeuvres, cord prolapse, uterine inversion. No OBG preparation is complete without mastering this section.

What makes this chapter especially dangerous for the student is that it looks straightforward — "just know the stages, know the drugs, know the manoeuvres" — but the examiner deliberately writes questions that test the *order of steps*, the *exact criteria*, and the *exceptions to the rule*. A question on PPH does not test whether you know "uterine atony is the commonest cause" — you know that. It tests whether you know that carboprost is contraindicated in asthma, that its maximum dose is 2 mg (not 2000 µg, not 20 mg), and that ergometrine must NOT be given before the second stage.

The most misunderstood area in this chapter: **fetal presentations and their diameters**. Students know brow is bad and face can sometimes deliver vaginally, but they confuse the exact diameters, forget that mento-posterior face presentation = mandatory caesarean, and don't connect the examining-room findings (what landmarks are palpable) to the diagnosis.

The second most misunderstood area: the **partogram**. Specifically, the alert-action line gap (4 hours), the transition from latent to active phase (6 cm by ACOG 2014), and what crossing the action line means clinically.

---

## Question Pattern Map

**What the examiner loves to ask:**
- "Most common cause of primary PPH / secondary PPH" (atony vs retained tissue)
- "Maximum dose of carboprost / which drug is contraindicated in PPH with asthma"
- "Active management of third stage — which component is NOT included"
- "Partogram alert-to-action line distance" (4 hours — this recurs every year)
- "Drug contraindicated before second stage" (ergometrine)
- "First manoeuvre for shoulder dystocia" (McRoberts)
- "Last resort for shoulder dystocia" (Zavanelli)
- "Forceps vs vacuum — preterm preference / face presentation contraindication"
- "Johnson's formula for fetal weight calculation"
- "Stages of labour — duration in primigravida vs multigravida"
- "Mento-vertical diameter = which presentation?"
- "Frank breech = most common type"

**Common traps:**
- Carboprost dose: "2000 µg" looks like "2 mg" — students pick 2000 µg thinking it means 2 mg when written differently
- Ergometrine given "before second stage" in a question — recognising it as dangerous
- "McRoberts" described without name — "hyperflexion of maternal thighs onto abdomen"
- Vasa praevia appearing in a labour chapter question (it is both an APH and intrapartum emergency)
- Sheehan's syndrome presented as a labour complication consequence — severe PPH → pituitary necrosis → amenorrhoea (Q866, Q412)

---

## Core Concept Deep Dive

### Section 1: Stages of Labour — The Framework

Every question in this chapter is anchored to your understanding of the stages.

| Stage | Definition | Duration (Primigravida) | Duration (Multigravida) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **First stage — Latent phase** | Onset of regular contractions → 6 cm dilatation | Up to 20 hours | Up to 14 hours |
| **First stage — Active phase** | 6 cm → 10 cm (full dilatation) | ≥0.5–1 cm/hr (min rate) | Faster |
| **Second stage (propulsive)** | Full dilatation → delivery of baby | Up to 2 hours (3 with epidural) | ~20 minutes (Q736) |
| **Third stage** | Delivery of baby → delivery of placenta | Up to 30 minutes (active mgmt) | Same |
| **Fourth stage** | First 2 hours postpartum | — | — |

> **MCQ Encounter — Q410 (PYQ)**
>
> *Which is NOT true about the latent phase?*
> - A. Starts with onset of regular contractions
> - B. Begins at the end of the active phase
> - C. Ends at 6 cm dilatation (ACOG 2014)
> - D. Patient may present with mild crampy pain
>
> **Answer: B** — The latent phase PRECEDES the active phase; it does not begin at the end of it. The false statement is B.
>
> **Key update (ACOG 2014):** Active phase now defined as starting at **6 cm** (previously 4 cm). This raised the threshold for diagnosing "failure to progress" — do not diagnose arrest of active phase before 6 cm dilatation.

---

### Section 2: The Partogram — The Examiner's Favourite Tool

The partogram (WHO model) is a graphical record of labour progress. Three lines matter:

1. **Alert line:** Starts at 4 cm dilatation; based on the slowest 10th centile of normal labours (Friedman's curve).
2. **Action line:** Drawn **4 hours to the right** of the alert line. Crossing the action line = intervention warranted.
3. The gap between alert and action line = **4 hours** (not 2, not 1, not 5).

> **MCQ Encounter — Q492 & Q864 (PYQ 2016)**
>
> *Distance between alert and action lines in WHO partogram:*
>
> **Answer: C — 4 hours**
>
> This appears almost every single year. Four hours. Commit to memory and never hesitate.
>
> **What the action line means:** Labour has deviated significantly from expected progress; the patient needs assessment for CPD, malposition, inadequate contractions → consider ARM, oxytocin augmentation, or caesarean.

---

### Section 3: Fetal Presenting Diameters — The Anatomy You Must Know

This is the area where clinical vignettes are written. "On vaginal exam, the examiner palpates orbital ridges, nose, anterior fontanelle but NOT the mouth or chin — what is the presentation?"

**Key diameters:**

| Presentation | Degree of Flexion | Presenting Diameter | Size | Can Deliver Vaginally? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Vertex (well-flexed) | Maximum flexion | **Suboccipito-bregmatic** | 9.5 cm | Yes (ideal) |
| Vertex (partial deflexion) | Partial | Suboccipito-frontal | 10.0 cm | Yes |
| **Brow** | Partial deflexion | **Mento-vertical** | **13.5 cm** | **Almost never** |
| Face (mento-anterior) | Full extension | Submento-bregmatic | 9.5 cm | Yes |
| Face (mento-posterior) | Full extension | — | — | **NEVER** |
| Breech | — | — | After-coming head | With manoeuvres |

> **MCQ Encounter — Q294 (PYQ)**
>
> *Mento-vertical engaging diameter = which presentation?*
>
> **Answer: B — Brow presentation**
>
> The brow is the partially deflexed head. Mento-vertical is the largest AP diameter (13.5 cm). This is undeliverable in a term fetus — the pelvis (average 11–12 cm AP) cannot accommodate it.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q295 (PYQ)**
>
> *Presenting diameter of fully flexed (vertex) head:*
>
> **Answer: A — Suboccipito-bregmatic (9.5 cm)**
>
> This is the smallest presenting diameter and why good flexion matters. Full flexion = smallest diameter = easiest delivery.

---

### Section 4: Fetal Presentations — Recognition and Management

#### Brow Presentation

> **MCQ Encounter — Q370 (PYQ)**
>
> *Term primigravida in active labour: orbital ridges, nose, and anterior fontanelle palpable; mouth and chin NOT felt; station −1. Diagnosis:*
>
> **Answer: B — Brow presentation**
>
> **Landmarks palpated in brow:** Orbital ridges + anterior fontanelle (neither the occiput of vertex nor the chin/mouth of face). Management: almost always requires caesarean section in a term fetus. Small chance it converts to face (mento-anterior) and delivers vaginally, but never count on it.

#### Face Presentation

**Mento-anterior:** Can deliver vaginally (chin rotates to occiput anterior position; extension is maintained through the pelvis).
**Mento-posterior:** CANNOT deliver vaginally — the fully extended head cannot negotiate the sacral curve.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q369 (PYQ)**
>
> *Primigravida at term, active labour, face presentation with mentum directed posteriorly. Management:*
>
> **Answer: C — Caesarean section**
>
> Persistent mento-posterior = mandatory LSCS. Any attempt at vaginal delivery risks fetal and maternal injury.

#### Breech Presentation

> **MCQ Encounter — Q473 (PYQ 2015)**
>
> *Most common type of breech:*
>
> **Answer: B — Frank breech** (thighs flexed, knees extended = "pike position") — accounts for 65–70%. Complete breech (both hips and knees flexed) = next most common. Footling = least common but highest cord prolapse risk.

**Manoeuvres in breech delivery:**
- **Lovset manoeuvre** (Q297): For **extended/nuchal arms** — rotate baby 180° to deliver arms.
- **Mauriceau-Smellie-Veit (MSV) manoeuvre:** For the **after-coming head** — jaw flexion and occiput pressure with one hand, traction with the other.
- **Burns Marshall technique:** After-coming head, allows fetus to "hang" to allow head to flex, then swing up.
- **Piper's forceps** (Q706): Applied to the after-coming head when it is arrested.
- **Prague manoeuvre:** For delivery of head when occiput is posterior.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q297 (PYQ)**
>
> *Lovset manoeuvre is used for:*
>
> **Answer: D — Extended arms in breech delivery**
>
> Never confuse: MSV = head; Lovset = arms.

#### Transverse Lie / Shoulder Presentation

> **MCQ Encounter — Q624 (PYQ)**
>
> *Transverse lie, shoulder presentation, absent membranes in second stage. Management:*
>
> **Answer: D — Caesarean section** (neglected shoulder presentation — do NOT attempt ECV or internal podalic version with absent membranes at term; risk of uterine rupture is high).

---

### Section 5: Bishop Score — Cervical Favourability

The Bishop score determines whether the cervix is "ripe" for induction. Five parameters:

| Parameter | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Dilatation (cm) | Closed | 1–2 | 3–4 | ≥5 |
| Effacement (%) | 0–30 | 40–50 | 60–70 | ≥80 |
| Station | −3 | −2 | −1/0 | +1/+2 |
| Consistency | Firm | Medium | Soft | — |
| Position | Posterior | Mid | Anterior | — |

**Score ≥8 = favourable cervix** → induction likely successful with oxytocin alone.  
**Score <6 = unfavourable** → cervical ripening needed first.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q618 (PYQ)**
>
> *Cervix 3 cm dilated, soft, anterior, station −1, length <0.5 cm. Bishop score?*
>
> **Calculation:** Dilatation 3 cm (+2) + Effacement >80% (+3) + Station −1 (+1) + Soft (+1) + Anterior (+1) = **8**
>
> **Answer: B — 8** — Favourable cervix; induction can proceed with oxytocin.

---

### Section 6: Induction of Labour — Drugs and Contraindications

**Cervical ripening agents:**
- PGE2 (dinoprostone) gel/tablet — pre-induction cervical ripening
- PGE1 (misoprostol) — cervical ripening and induction
- Mechanical: Foley's catheter balloon (mechanical dilation), hygroscopic dilators

**Drug NOT used for induction:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q339 & Q921 (PYQ)**
>
> *41-week pregnancy, unfavourable cervix. Which should NOT be used for induction?*
>
> **Answer: D — PGF2α (carboprost)**
>
> PGF2α = carboprost = used for **PPH treatment** (uterotonic for atony). It causes bronchospasm and is too potent for induction. PGE1 (misoprostol), PGE2 (dinoprostone), and Foley's catheter are all appropriate.

**Misoprostol pharmacology:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q330 (PYQ)**
>
> *Misoprostol is an analogue of:*
>
> **Answer: A — PGE1 (prostaglandin E1)**
>
> Misoprostol is the methyl ester of PGE1. Used for: induction of labour, cervical ripening, medical TOP, missed abortion, and atonic PPH (off-label).

> **MCQ Encounter — Q515 (PYQ)**
>
> *Misoprostol NOT effective for:*
>
> **Answer: C — Menorrhagia** — Misoprostol is a PG agonist; menorrhagia is treated with PG inhibitors (NSAIDs). This is the perfect "opposite mechanism" trap.

**Ergometrine contraindication:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q298 (PYQ)**
>
> *Drug contraindicated before the second stage of labour:*
>
> **Answer: D — Ergometrine**
>
> Ergometrine causes sustained tonic contraction. If given before delivery of the fetus, it can cause placental entrapment, tetanic contraction, and fetal death. It is given only after delivery of the anterior shoulder or in the third stage.

**Contraindications to induction:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q280 & Q935 (PYQ)**
>
> *Which is NOT an absolute contraindication to induction?*
>
> **Q280 Answer: C — IUGR** (IUGR is actually an indication for induction, not a contraindication)
>
> **Q935 Answer: C — Heart disease** (vaginal delivery is preferred in cardiac patients; induction is acceptable with monitoring)
>
> **Absolute contraindications:** Complete placenta praevia, active genital herpes, pelvic obstruction (tumour), classical uterine scar, cord prolapse, transverse lie, fetal malpresentation preventing vaginal birth, multiple previous LSCS.

---

### Section 7: Caesarean Section — Classical vs LSCS

**Standard procedure:** Lower uterine segment caesarean section (LSCS), Kerr incision (transverse) — preferred because: less blood loss, easier repair, lower rupture risk in subsequent pregnancy.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q365 (PYQ)**
>
> *28 weeks, transverse lie with back-down, emergency caesarean required. Which incision?*
>
> **Answer: C — Classical (upper segment vertical)**
>
> Classical LSCS indications:
> - Extreme prematurity (<28 weeks) — lower segment not formed
> - Transverse lie with back-down (lower segment inaccessible)
> - Anterior low-lying placenta/praevia with extensive vasculature
> - Carcinoma cervix coexisting
>
> **Disadvantage:** Classical scar has much higher rupture risk in subsequent labour (5–10% vs 0.5% for LSCS scar) → future pregnancies must be LSCS.

**Scar rupture risk:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q300 (PYQ)**
>
> *Risk of scar rupture in next pregnancy after ONE LSCS:*
>
> **Answer: A — ~2%** (0.5–2% for lower transverse scar). Rises with classical scar and multiple LSCS.

---

### Section 8: Postpartum Haemorrhage (PPH) — The 4 Ts

**Definition:** Blood loss ≥500 mL after vaginal delivery or ≥1000 mL after caesarean, within 24 hours. Primary PPH = within 24 hours; secondary PPH = 24 hours to 12 weeks.

**The 4 Ts framework:**

| Cause | Frequency | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Tone** (uterine atony) | **70–80%** | Most common cause of PRIMARY PPH |
| **Trauma** | 10–20% | Cervical/vaginal tears, uterine rupture, episiotomy |
| **Tissue** (retained) | 5–10% | Retained placenta, cotyledon, membranes — most common cause of SECONDARY PPH |
| **Thrombin** (coagulopathy) | 1–5% | DIC, HELLP, AFLP, AFE |

> **MCQ Encounter — Q276 & Q906 (PYQ)**
>
> *Which is NOT a cause of PPH?*
>
> **Answer: A — Placenta praevia**
>
> Placenta praevia causes antepartum haemorrhage, NOT PPH. Students trap themselves by thinking "placenta = postpartum."

> **MCQ Encounter — Q299 & Q476 (PYQ 2015)**
>
> *Most common cause of secondary PPH:*
>
> **Answer: B — Retained placental tissue** (± endometritis)
>
> Secondary PPH = 24 hours to 12 weeks; most commonly 8–14 days. Cause: retained cotyledon/membrane fragment → failure of uterus to involute normally.

**PPH-increasing risk factors:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q654 (PYQ)**
>
> *Which complication does NOT increase PPH risk?*
>
> **Answer: A — Hypertension (pre-eclampsia)**
>
> Uterine overdistension = macrosomia, twins, polyhydramnios → all predispose to atony. Hypertension does not cause uterine distension; it is not in the 4 Ts.

**Uterotonic Drugs — The Hierarchy:**

**Step 1:** Oxytocin 10 IU IM or IV infusion (first-line; universal)
**Step 2:** Ergometrine 0.5 mg IM (or Syntometrine = oxytocin + ergometrine combined)
**Step 3:** Carboprost 250 µg IM (if above fail)
**Step 4:** Misoprostol 800 µg sublingual/rectal (second-line PG)
**Step 5:** Tranexamic acid 1 g IV (antifibrinolytic; add early in all cases of PPH)

> **MCQ Encounter — Q334 & Q561 & Q944 (PYQ)**
>
> *Maximum cumulative dose of carboprost (15-methyl PGF2α) in PPH:*
>
> **Answer: 2 mg** (250 µg IM every 15–90 min × maximum 8 doses = 2000 µg = **2 mg**)
>
> **The trap:** Options offer 200 µg, 2 mg, 20 mg, 2000 µg. Candidates confuse 2000 µg and 2 mg — they are the SAME. But "200 µg" is wrong (one dose is 250 µg) and "20 mg" is way too high.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q557 (PYQ)**
>
> *Carboprost is contraindicated in:*
>
> **Answer: B — Asthma** — Carboprost (PGF2α analogue) causes bronchoconstriction. Absolutely contraindicated in asthma. Safe alternatives: oxytocin, ergometrine, misoprostol, tranexamic acid.

**Active Management of Third Stage (AMTSL):**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q606 (PYQ)**
>
> *Active management of third stage includes all EXCEPT:*
>
> **Answer: D — Ergometrine**
>
> Current WHO AMTSL components:
> 1. Oxytocin 10 IU IM (within 1 minute of delivery)
> 2. Uterine massage after placenta delivery
> 3. Controlled cord traction
>
> Ergometrine has been removed from routine AMTSL in current WHO guidelines (though it can be used as a second uterotonic if oxytocin fails).

---

### Section 9: Instrumental Delivery

#### Vacuum vs Forceps — When to Use Which

> **MCQ Encounter — Q363 (PYQ)**
>
> *Which statement about vacuum vs forceps is correct?*
>
> **Answer: A — Forceps preferred when fetal scalp must be protected (preterm <34 weeks)**
>
> **Rules:**
> - Vacuum contraindicated below **34 weeks** (risk of cephalhaematoma, intracranial haemorrhage in preterm neonates)
> - Vacuum contraindicated in **face presentation**
> - Vacuum contraindicated if **fetal bleeding disorder**
> - Forceps causes more **maternal perineal trauma** than vacuum
> - Vacuum requires more **maternal effort** (active pushing) than forceps

> **MCQ Encounter — Q364 (PYQ)**
>
> *Absolute contraindication to outlet forceps:*
>
> **Answer: B — Unengaged fetal head**
>
> For ANY instrumental delivery (forceps or vacuum): head must be **engaged** (at or below ischial spines = station 0 or positive). Applying forceps to an unengaged head is one of the most dangerous obstetric errors.

**Forceps types (examiner likes to test recognition):**
- **Wrigley's:** Outlet forceps, low cavity, short blades
- **Neville-Barnes:** Mid-cavity, long handles
- **Kielland's:** Rotational forceps (for OP positions) — NO pelvic curve
- **Piper's:** After-coming head in breech

---

### Section 10: Shoulder Dystocia

**The concept:** After delivery of the fetal head, anterior shoulder is impacted behind the pubic symphysis. "Turtle sign" = head retracts back against perineum.

**Management — HELPERR Mnemonic:**
- **H** = Call for Help
- **E** = Evaluate for Episiotomy
- **L** = Legs (McRoberts manoeuvre)
- **P** = Suprapubic Pressure
- **E** = Enter (internal rotational manoeuvres: Rubin II, Woods screw)
- **R** = Remove the posterior arm
- **R** = Roll the patient (Gaskin all-fours)

> **MCQ Encounter — Q358 (PYQ)**
>
> *Turtle sign is noted, obstetrician flexes maternal thighs sharply onto abdomen. Manoeuvre:*
>
> **Answer: B — McRoberts manoeuvre** — FIRST step in shoulder dystocia. Hyperflexion of thighs flattens lumbar lordosis, rotates symphysis superiorly, increases functional AP diameter. Success rate alone: ~40%.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q359 (PYQ)**
>
> *All other manoeuvres have failed. Obstetrician pushes head back into uterus and proceeds to caesarean. This is:*
>
> **Answer: C — Zavanelli manoeuvre** (cephalic replacement) — Absolute LAST RESORT. Rotate head to OA, push back into vagina under tocolysis, perform emergency LSCS.

---

### Section 11: Cord Complications

#### Cord Prolapse

> **MCQ Encounter — Q360 (PYQ)**
>
> *Cord visible at introitus, fetal bradycardia. Most important IMMEDIATE step:*
>
> **Answer: C — Digital elevation of presenting part** (manually push presenting part upward to relieve cord compression)
>
> This buys time while preparing for emergency LSCS (the definitive treatment). The sequence: call for help → manually relieve compression → Trendelenburg/knee-chest position → fill bladder if possible (lifts head) → emergency LSCS.
>
> **Do NOT** try to replace the cord into the uterus — this is futile and dangerous.

#### Vasa Praevia (Intrapartum Scenario)

This is covered in Chapter 3 but revisited here because it presents during labour. Bleeding at ROM + immediate fetal distress = vasa praevia = fetal exsanguination = emergency LSCS immediately.

---

### Section 12: Uterine Inversion

**Degrees:**
- 1st: Dimpling, fundus remains above cervix
- 2nd (complete): Fundus passes through cervical os but not beyond introitus
- **3rd (prolapsed):** Fundus protrudes beyond the introitus
- 4th (total): Vaginal walls also inverted

> **MCQ Encounter — Q361 (PYQ)**
>
> *Fundus not palpable abdominally, dark mass through introitus. Degree:*
>
> **Answer: C — Third degree (prolapsed)**

**Management — critical sequence:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q362 (PYQ)**
>
> *Manual reposition fails. Before Johnson's hydrostatic method, which drug to give?*
>
> **Answer: C — Terbutaline (tocolytic)**
>
> **The rule:** To reposition the inverted uterus, you must RELAX it first. Tocolytics (terbutaline, nitroglycerin, MgSO4) relax the cervical ring.
>
> **The trap:** Students give oxytocin or ergometrine (uterotonics). This contracts the uterus further, trapping the inversion and making it impossible to replace. Uterotonics are given ONLY AFTER successful repositioning.

---

### Section 13: Amniotic Fluid Embolism (AFE)

> **MCQ Encounter — Q367 (PYQ)**
>
> *Multiparous woman: sudden respiratory distress + hypotension + cyanosis within minutes of delivery + DIC + cardiac arrest. Most likely diagnosis:*
>
> **Answer: B — Amniotic fluid embolism**
>
> **Classic triad:** Hypoxia + Hypotension + Coagulopathy (DIC) in peripartum period. Mortality >80%. Mechanism: amniotic fluid (containing fetal debris, prostaglandins, thromboplastin) enters maternal circulation via uterine/cervical veins → anaphylactoid reaction → cardiac collapse + DIC.

---

### Section 14: Twin Pregnancy Complications

> **MCQ Encounter — Q371 (PYQ)**
>
> *MCDA twins at 22 weeks: one sac polyhydramnios (DVP >8 cm), other sac oligohydramnios (DVP <2 cm), "stuck" twin. First-line treatment for stage II–III TTTS:*
>
> **Answer: C — Fetoscopic laser photocoagulation of anastomotic vessels**
>
> TTTS (Twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome) = monochorionic placenta with arteriovenous anastomoses → donor twin (anaemic, IUGR, oligohydramnios) + recipient twin (polycythaemic, polyhydramnios).
>
> Laser ablation addresses the root cause (pathological vascular anastomoses) and is superior to amnioreduction for stage II–IV TTTS before 26 weeks. Amnioreduction = palliation only.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q372 (PYQ)**
>
> *What complication is UNIQUE to monoamniotic (MA) twins?*
>
> **Answer: B — Cord entanglement** — Monoamniotic twins share one sac → cords inevitably entangle → risk of cord compression/knotting → fetal death. TTTS requires monochorionic placenta but can occur in MCDA; cord entanglement is MA-only.

> **MCQ Encounter — Q338 (PYQ)**
>
> *36-week pregnant, one previous delivery of twins. Gravida-parity:*
>
> **Answer: A — G2P1** — Gravida = number of pregnancies. Parity = number of deliveries ≥20 weeks, regardless of number of fetuses. Twin delivery = ONE parity event.

---

### Section 15: Perineal Tears — Classification and Repair

| Degree | Structures Involved |
|---|---|
| **1st** | Skin only |
| **2nd** | Skin + perineal muscles (not sphincter) |
| **3rd** | Perineum + **external anal sphincter (EAS)** |
| — 3a | <50% EAS thickness |
| — 3b | >50% EAS thickness |
| — 3c | Complete EAS + internal anal sphincter (IAS) |
| **4th** | Perineum + EAS + IAS + **anal/rectal mucosa** |

> **MCQ Encounter — Q772 (PYQ)**
>
> *Tear involves perineum and external anal sphincter, intact mucosa. Grade:*
>
> **Answer: C — Third degree** (mucosa intact = not 4th degree)

**Episiotomy comparison:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q274 (PYQ)**
>
> *Advantage of mediolateral over midline episiotomy:*
>
> **Answer: D — Less extension to 3rd/4th degree tears**
>
> | Feature | Midline | Mediolateral |
> |---|---|---|
> | Blood loss | Less | More |
> | Pain | Less | More |
> | Healing | Faster | Slower |
> | Extension to 3rd/4th degree | **Higher risk** | **Lower risk** |
> | Common in | USA | UK/India |

**Delayed perineal tear repair:**

> **MCQ Encounter — Q617 & Q964 (PYQ)**
>
> *Complete perineal tear, home delivery 2 weeks ago. Management:*
>
> **Answer: D — Repair after 3 months** — Primary repair window is within 24 hours. Presenting at 2 weeks = oedema, infection, fibrosis are present. Wait 3 months for infection/oedema to fully resolve, then do secondary repair.

---

### Section 16: Johnson's Formula and Fetal Weight Estimation

> **MCQ Encounter — Q301 (PYQ)**
>
> *SFH = 40 cm, vertex at −1 station. Estimated fetal weight by Johnson's formula:*
>
> **Formula:** EFW (g) = (SFH in cm − n) × 155
> - n = 12 if vertex is ABOVE the ischial spines
> - n = 11 if vertex is AT or BELOW the ischial spines
>
> **Calculation:** Station −1 = above ischial spines → n = 12
> (40 − 12) × 155 = 28 × 155 = **4,340 g ≈ 4.3 kg**
>
> **Answer: D — ~4.3 kg**

---

### Section 17: Sheehan's Syndrome — A Labour Complication Consequence

> **MCQ Encounter — Q412 & Q866 (PYQ)**
>
> *35-year-old, amenorrhoea + failure of lactation after second delivery + empty sella. Diagnosis:*
>
> **Answer: C — Sheehan's syndrome**
>
> **Mechanism:** Massive postpartum haemorrhage → pituitary ischaemia (the pituitary is enlarged and hypervascular in pregnancy → sensitive to hypoperfusion) → anterior pituitary necrosis → panhypopituitarism.
>
> **Classic triad:** Agalactia (failure of lactation — first sign, within days), secondary amenorrhoea, empty sella on imaging.
>
> **Why students confuse with pituitary adenoma:** Adenoma = prolactinoma typically = galactorrhoea + amenorrhoea. Sheehan's = agalactia (no milk) + amenorrhoea. Opposite milk finding.

---

## Bias Removal Section

**Top traps in this chapter:**

1. **Carboprost dose:** 2 mg = 2000 µg. Both are correct representations of the same maximum dose. "200 µg" (too low) and "20 mg" (way too high) are the wrong options.

2. **Ergometrine timing:** "Given before delivery of baby" = wrong and dangerous. Only after anterior shoulder delivery or in 3rd stage.

3. **Zavanelli vs McRoberts:** McRoberts = FIRST step (thigh flexion); Zavanelli = LAST resort (push head back).

4. **Active management of third stage:** Current WHO guidelines no longer include ergometrine as a routine component. Oxytocin + cord traction + uterine massage (after placenta delivery) = the three components.

5. **Mento-posterior face = always LSCS:** Students think "face presentations can deliver vaginally" → TRUE for mento-anterior. NOT for mento-posterior. Never confuse them.

6. **Partogram:** Students say "2 hours" for alert-action line gap. The answer is ALWAYS 4 hours for WHO partogram.

7. **Sheehan's vs prolactinoma:** Sheehan's = no milk (agalactia). Prolactinoma = too much milk (galactorrhoea). Key differentiator.

---

## Variations and Twists

**How examiners vary these questions:**

- "A multiparous woman's placenta does not deliver for 40 minutes" → Manual removal under anaesthesia (Q366). The trap answer is "wait longer" or "give more oxytocin."

- "Bakri balloon inserted after LSCS for praevia" — examiner asks mechanism. Answer (Q368): direct intrauterine tamponade pressure exceeding arterial perfusion pressure. Not "stimulates contraction" and not "compresses uterine arteries."

- Sheehan's presented as a question about a patient who had severe PPH from placenta praevia: "Most likely outcome post delivery?" Answer = absence of menstrual cycle (Q866). The trap is "galactorrhoea" — but Sheehan's causes agalactia.

- Forceps type image questions (Q706): Piper's forceps = long perineal curve, used for after-coming head in breech. Kielland's = no pelvic curve, used for rotational delivery.

---

## Memory Engineering

**Stages of labour — "FADS":**
- **F**irst stage = Fully dilate (latent + active)
- **A**ctive phase starts at **6** cm (ACOG 2014)
- **D**elivery = Second stage
- **S**eperation of placenta = Third stage

**PPH 4 Ts — "Tone Trauma Tissue Thrombin":**
- Most common = **Tone** (atony, 70–80%)
- Secondary PPH = **Tissue** (retained)
- Coagulopathy = **Thrombin**

**Uterotonic hierarchy — "O-E-C-M-T":**
- **O**xytocin first
- **E**rgometrine second
- **C**arboprost third (not in asthma)
- **M**isoprostol fourth
- **T**ranexamic acid (add throughout)

**Shoulder dystocia — HELPERR:** Help, Episiotomy, Legs (McRoberts), Pressure (suprapubic), Enter (Rubin II/Woods), Remove posterior arm, Roll (Gaskin)

**Presenting diameters — "The best position is minimum deflexion":**
- Vertex well-flexed = suboccipito-bregmatic = **9.5 cm** (smallest = best)
- Brow = mento-vertical = **13.5 cm** (largest = worst)
- Face mento-anterior = submento-bregmatic = **9.5 cm** (can deliver)

**Breech manoeuvres — "Arms = Lovset, Head = MSV":**
- Extended **A**rms → Lovset (**A** for Arms)
- After-coming **H**ead → Mauriceau-Smellie-Veit (**H** for Head)

**Episiotomy — "Midline = More extension; Mediolateral = More blood":**
Midline easier to repair but risky for sphincter. Mediolateral safer for sphincter but more blood/pain.

---

## Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### What the Examiner REALLY Wants

The examiner wants you to sequence manoeuvres correctly (McRoberts before Zavanelli), dose drugs correctly (carboprost = 2 mg max), and diagnose by landmarks (mento-vertical diameter = brow presentation). These are precision questions, not recall questions.

### 20-Second Revision Bullets

- Latent phase ends at 6 cm (ACOG 2014); active phase minimum rate = 0.5 cm/hr
- WHO partogram alert-to-action line distance = **4 hours** (tested every year)
- Second stage = full dilatation to delivery; duration in multipara = ~20 minutes
- Suboccipito-bregmatic diameter = 9.5 cm = well-flexed vertex (smallest, best)
- Mento-vertical diameter = 13.5 cm = brow presentation (largest, worst)
- Brow presentation at term → almost always LSCS
- Face presentation: mento-anterior = can deliver vaginally; mento-posterior = always LSCS
- Frank breech = most common type (65–70%)
- Lovset manoeuvre = extended arms in breech; MSV = after-coming head
- Piper's forceps = after-coming head in breech
- Bishop score ≥8 = favourable cervix
- PGF2α (carboprost) NOT used for induction; used for PPH only
- Misoprostol = PGE1 analogue; NOT for menorrhagia (pro-PG; menorrhagia needs anti-PG)
- Ergometrine = contraindicated before second stage
- PPH first-line drug = oxytocin; carboprost max = **2 mg** (8 doses × 250 µg); carboprost contraindicated in asthma
- Active management of third stage = oxytocin + cord traction + uterine massage (ergometrine NOT routine in current WHO)
- Primary PPH = within 24 hours; most common cause = **uterine atony** (Tone)
- Secondary PPH = 24h to 12 weeks; most common cause = **retained placenta/tissue** (Tissue)
- Hypertension does NOT increase PPH risk (not a uterine distension factor)
- Shoulder dystocia: first step = McRoberts; last resort = Zavanelli (cephalic replacement)
- Cord prolapse: first step = digitally elevate presenting part; definitive = emergency LSCS
- Uterine inversion: TOCOLYTIC (terbutaline) BEFORE repositioning; uterotonic only AFTER replacement
- 3rd degree tear = EAS involved, mucosa intact; 4th degree = mucosa involved
- Delayed perineal tear at 2 weeks → repair after 3 months
- Sheehan's syndrome = massive PPH → agalactia + amenorrhoea + empty sella
- Johnson's formula: EFW = (SFH − 12) × 155 g (if above spines)
- Frank breech = G2P1 (parity counts deliveries, not fetuses)
- AMTSL does NOT include early cord clamping or fundal pressure; ergometrine removed from routine WHO protocol

### Top 5 Examiner Traps in Chapter 4

1. "Partogram alert-to-action line gap" → **4 hours** (not 2, not 1, not 5)
2. "Carboprost in a patient with asthma" → **contraindicated** (causes bronchospasm); choose misoprostol/oxytocin instead
3. "Uterine inversion + manual replacement fails — which drug?" → **tocolytic** (terbutaline), NOT oxytocin/ergometrine (those make it worse)
4. "McRoberts vs Zavanelli" → McRoberts = FIRST (described as 'flexing thighs onto abdomen'); Zavanelli = LAST (described as 'pushing head back')
5. "Mento-posterior face presentation — can it deliver vaginally?" → **No, always caesarean** (mento-anterior = yes; mento-posterior = never)

---




---

# PART II — GYNAECOLOGY

---



---

# Chapter 5 — Contraception

> *"The mark of a good exam candidate is knowing not just which method works, but WHY it works, when it fails, and what to do in the edge cases."*

---

## Big Picture First

### Exam Weightage

Contraception consistently delivers 4–7 marks per NEET-PG paper. The questions cluster around four poles:

1. **Failure rates / Pearl Index** — pure number recall, but with sneaky option ordering
2. **Mechanism of action** — OCPs, emergency contraception, IUCDs: subtle "EXCEPT" traps
3. **Contraindications** — absolute vs. relative; situation-based (cardiac disease, lactation, postpartum)
4. **Surgical sterilisation** — named techniques for tubectomy/vasectomy, timing, complications

### The Pearl Index: Get This Right First

The Pearl Index (PI) = number of pregnancies per 100 woman-years of use. Higher number = WORSE method.

**The Golden Rule:** When a question asks "most effective" or "lowest failure rate," you want the LOWEST Pearl Index.

### The Reversibility Spectrum

```
PERMANENT ←————————————————————————————→ TEMPORARY
Tubectomy   Vasectomy   IUCD/Implant   DMPA   OCP/POP   Barrier   LAM
(irreversible)  (irreversible)  (LARC)     (6-9mo delay)  (immediate)
```

---

## Question Pattern Map

Recurring PYQ themes from 2015–2024:

| Theme | Frequency |
|---|---|
| Emergency contraception — mechanism, drug of choice, timing | Very High |
| Progestin-only pill — mechanism, NOT a mechanism | High |
| Ideal contraceptive in lactation / cardiac disease | High |
| IUCD — absolute contraindications, side effects, generations | High |
| Vasectomy — false statement about sterility | High |
| Pearl Index / lowest failure rate | Medium |
| Tubectomy techniques (Pomeroy, Kroener, Uchida, Irving) | Medium |
| LAM criteria | Medium |

---

## Core Concept Deep Dive

### 1. Combined Oral Contraceptive Pill (OCP)

#### Mechanism (and the "EXCEPT" traps)

The combined OCP works through **four mechanisms**. Examiners love asking which is NOT a mechanism:

1. **Inhibition of LH surge → anovulation** (primary mechanism)
2. **Thickening of cervical mucus** → sperm cannot penetrate
3. **Endometrial atrophy** → impaired implantation
4. **Altered tubal motility**

---

**PYQ 1 (Q540)**

> All of the following are mechanisms of action of combined OCPs EXCEPT:
> - A. Inhibition of ovulation
> - B. Spermicidal effect
> - C. Interference with implantation
> - D. Interference with placental function

**Answer: D. Interference with placental function**

The OCP has no effect once placentation has occurred. Option B (spermicidal effect) is technically not a classic mechanism either, but option D is definitively wrong. Options A, B, and C are accepted as partial mechanisms. Once the embryo has implanted and the placenta is forming, the OCP is completely ineffective — this is the conceptual boundary between contraception and abortion.

---

#### Non-Contraceptive Benefits of OCP

Examiners test this indirectly through "OCP is NOT indicated for ___" questions. OCPs are beneficial in:
- Dysmenorrhoea (reduces PGF2α)
- Endometriosis (decidualisation → atrophy)
- Menorrhagia (thins endometrium)
- PCOS (suppresses androgens)
- PMS
- Reduces risk of ovarian and endometrial cancer

#### OCP Failure Rate

**PYQ 2 (Q313)**

> Which contraceptive has the LEAST pregnancy failure rate?
> - A. OCP
> - B. Cu-IUCD
> - C. Diaphragm
> - D. Condom

**Answer: A. OCP** *(per source key — among these four options)*

However, in reality, **subdermal implants** have a lower failure rate than OCPs (Pearl Index 0.05 vs. 0.3). When implants are in the options, implants win. When the question restricts to these four, OCP wins.

---

**PYQ 3 (Q456/Q634)**

> A young woman wants the most effective reversible long-acting contraception with the lowest Pearl Index. The method with the LOWEST failure rate is:
> - A. Subdermal implants (etonogestrel)
> - B. Combined oral contraceptive pill
> - C. Condom
> - D. Copper IUD

**Answer: A. Subdermal implants**

Subdermal etonogestrel implants (Implanon/Nexplanon): Pearl Index 0.05 per 100 woman-years — the lowest of ALL reversible methods. Effective for 3 years, no user compliance needed, immediately reversible on removal.

---

#### OCP in Lactation — A Crucial Distinction

**PYQ 4 (Q458)**

> A woman who is lactating comes for contraception 6 weeks after delivery. Which contraceptive is NOT advisable?
> - A. Combined oral contraceptive pill
> - B. Progestogen-only pill (mini-pill)
> - C. Copper IUD
> - D. DMPA (depot medroxyprogesterone acetate)

**Answer: A. Combined oral contraceptive pill**

The oestrogen component suppresses prolactin → reduces milk volume. This is a direct pharmacological effect, not just theoretical. All progestogen-only methods (POP, DMPA, LNG-IUS, implants) and IUDs are safe during breastfeeding.

---

### 2. Progestogen-Only Pill (Mini-Pill, POP)

#### Mechanisms

**PYQ 5 (Q904)**

> Which of the following is NOT a mechanism of action of progesterone-only pills?
> - A. Thicken the cervical mucus
> - B. Prevent ovulation
> - C. Kill the sperms in the cervical canal
> - D. Inhibit penetration of sperm

**Answer: C. Kill the sperms in the cervical canal**

POPs are NOT spermicidal. Their three mechanisms are: (1) cervical mucus thickening, (2) suppression of ovulation (inconsistent — only ~50% with traditional POP; higher with desogestrel-POP), (3) endometrial atrophy reducing implantation.

---

**PYQ 6 (Q918)** *(from 2018)*

> What is the effect of progesterone-only pills?
> - A. Completely suppresses ovulation
> - B. Thin lining of uterus
> - C. Thick cervical mucus
> - D. All of the above

**Answer: D. All of the above**

Note the apparent contradiction with the previous question — the 2018 paper accepted "all of the above." The nuance: traditional POPs suppress ovulation in ~50% of cycles; desogestrel-containing POPs (Cerazette) suppress in ~97%. For exam purposes, "all of the above" when all three are correct mechanisms is also valid.

#### POP Side Effects — What's NOT a Side Effect

**PYQ 7 (Q757)**

> Which is not a side effect of POP (Progestin-only pill)?
> - A. Ovarian cysts
> - B. Venous thromboembolism
> - C. Increased risk of diabetes mellitus
> - D. Ectopic pregnancy

**Answer: B. Venous thromboembolism**

VTE is the side effect of OESTROGEN, not progestogen. POPs are the safe choice in women with:
- Prior VTE / thrombophilia
- Lactation
- Cardiovascular disease risk factors
- Smokers over 35 years

POPs can cause: ovarian cysts (follicles accumulate without rupturing), functional ectopic pregnancy risk (if pill fails, altered tubal motility increases ectopic risk), irregular bleeding, and mild carbohydrate intolerance.

#### Missed POP Dose

**PYQ 8 (Q620)**

> A 26-year-old on a progestogen-only pill usually takes it at 10 am but took it yesterday at 5 pm. Advice:
> - A. No need to worry; continue same contraceptive
> - B. Use additional contraception for the next 48 hours
> - C. Change over to combined oral contraceptive pill
> - D. Take double dose next morning

**Answer: B. Use additional contraception for the next 48 hours**

The window of tolerance: Traditional POP (norethisterone) = 3-hour window. Desogestrel POP = 12-hour window. If taken late beyond these windows, the cervical mucus effect is lost — additional barrier contraception for 48 hours is needed. Never double dose.

---

### 3. Emergency Contraception

This is a perennial high-yield area with multiple PYQs testing mechanism and drug choice.

#### Drug of Choice

**PYQ 9 (Q314/Q469)**

> Progestogen of choice for emergency contraception?
> - A. Norethisterone
> - B. Medroxyprogesterone
> - C. Oxytocin
> - D. Levonorgestrel

**Answer: D. Levonorgestrel**

LNG 1.5 mg as a single dose (or 0.75 mg × 2 doses 12 hours apart) within 72 hours. Effective up to 120 hours (5 days), but efficacy decreases significantly after 72 hours.

---

#### Ulipristal Acetate — The Better Option

**PYQ 10 (Q315)**

> Dose of ulipristal acetate for emergency contraception?
> - A. 300 mg
> - B. 30 mg
> - C. 300 µg
> - D. 30 µg

**Answer: B. 30 mg**

Ulipristal acetate (UPA) is a selective progesterone receptor modulator (SPRM). Single 30 mg oral dose, effective up to 120 hours. It is MORE effective than LNG beyond 72 hours because it can delay ovulation even after the LH surge has begun (LNG cannot).

---

#### Mechanism of Emergency Contraception — The "NOT a Mechanism" Trap

**PYQ 11 (Q7/Q505)**

> Emergency contraception prevents pregnancy by all mechanisms EXCEPT:
> - A. Delaying or inhibiting ovulation
> - B. Inhibiting fertilisation
> - C. Preventing implantation of the fertilised egg
> - D. Interrupting an early established pregnancy

**Answer: D. Interrupting an early established pregnancy**

This is a conceptual boundary question. Emergency contraception is CONTRACEPTION — it prevents pregnancy. Once implantation is complete and pregnancy is established, EC has zero efficacy. Any intervention at that stage constitutes termination. This question has appeared multiple times (Q7, Q505) with identical answer.

---

#### Emergency Contraception Comparison

| Feature | LNG (Levonorgestrel) | UPA (Ulipristal) | Cu-T 380A |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dose | 1.5 mg single | 30 mg single | Device insertion |
