| Window | Up to 72h (up to 120h) | Up to 120h | Up to 5 days (120h) |
| Mechanism | Delay ovulation; prevent fertilisation | Delay ovulation (even post-LH surge) | Spermicidal + anti-implantation |
| Efficacy | ~85% | ~98% | >99% |
| Contraindication | Pregnancy | UPA: avoid if on regular hormonal contraception | Active PID, STI risk |
| Most effective option | — | — | YES (most effective EC) |

**Key exam trap:** Cu-T 380A is the most effective single EC method (>99%), can be left in for ongoing contraception for up to 10 years, AND is the only EC method that also provides future contraception.

---

### 4. IUCD Types — Exact Specifications Examiners Ask

#### Generations of Copper IUCDs

| Generation | Device | Surface area |
|---|---|---|
| 1st | Lippes Loop | None (inert) |
| 2nd | Cu-T 200, Multiload 250 | 200–250 mm² |
| 3rd | Cu-T 380A, Multiload 375 | 375–380 mm² |
| Frameless | GyneFix | Frameless, anchored |

**PYQ 12 (Q798)**

> Which of the following is false about intra-uterine devices (IUDs)?
> - A. Multiload Cu-375 is a third generation intra-uterine device
> - B. Copper devices are effective as post-coital contraceptives
> - C. LNG-20 (Mirena) has an effective life of 5 years
> - D. Pregnancy rates of Lippes Loop and T Cu-200 are similar

**Answer: A. Multiload Cu-375 is a third generation intra-uterine device**

Multiload Cu-375 is SECOND generation. Third-generation devices include Cu-T 380A and the frameless GyneFix. Option C (LNG-IUS/Mirena effective for 5 years) is technically true for original labelling, though regulatory approvals now extend to 8 years in some countries — the answer as set remains A.

---

#### Hormonal IUDs — Precise Data

| Device | Hormone | Release rate | Duration | Primary use |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Progestasert | Progesterone | **65 µg/day** | 1 year | Historical |
| LNG-IUS (Mirena) | Levonorgestrel | **20 µg/day** | 5–8 years | Contraception + menorrhagia |
| Kyleena | LNG | 17.5 µg/day | 5 years | Smaller device, nullipara |
| Jaydess/Skyla | LNG | 14 µg/day | 3 years | Nullipara |

**PYQ 13 (Q6)**

> The quantity of hormone released by the Progestasert IUCD per day is:
> - A. 25 µg
> - B. 45 µg
> - C. 65 µg
> - D. 85 µg

**Answer: C. 65 µg**

Progestasert contains 38 mg progesterone in a silicone oil reservoir. Released at 65 µg/day. Valid for only 1 year. Compare with Mirena (20 µg LNG/day, 5 years) — the numbers are frequently confused in MCQs. Mechanism: thickens cervical mucus, suppresses endometrium.

---

#### IUCD Mechanism — Copper Devices

The copper IUD works by:
1. **Spermicidal effect** — copper ions are directly toxic to sperm
2. **Inhibition of fertilisation** — copper alters tubal fluid
3. **Impaired implantation** — endometrial inflammatory reaction
4. **Increased tubal peristalsis** — faster egg transit

Copper IUDs do NOT suppress ovulation (unlike LNG-IUS which partially does).

---

#### IUCD Side Effects — Most Common

**PYQ 14 (Q528)**

> The most common side effect of IUCD insertion is:
> - A. Bleeding (menorrhagia)
> - B. Pain/dysmenorrhoea
> - C. Pelvic infection
> - D. Ectopic pregnancy

**Answer: A. Bleeding (menorrhagia)**

Copper IUCDs increase menstrual blood loss by **30–50%** due to prostaglandin-mediated endometrial reaction. This is the most common reason for discontinuation. Compare: LNG-IUS (Mirena) reduces blood loss by 90% — it is used to TREAT menorrhagia.

---

#### IUCD Absolute Contraindications

**PYQ 15 (Q475/Q750)**

> Absolute contraindications to IUCD are all EXCEPT:
> - A. Pregnancy
> - B. Undiagnosed vaginal bleeding
> - C. Pelvic inflammatory disease
> - D. Uterine malformation

**Answer: D. Uterine malformation**

Uterine malformation (distorted cavity) is only a RELATIVE contraindication. Absolute contraindications include:
- Current pregnancy
- Active PID / current STI
- Undiagnosed abnormal uterine bleeding
- Gestational trophoblastic disease *(Q969)*
- Known/suspected genital malignancy
- Wilsons disease (copper IUD only — contraindicated due to copper accumulation)

---

#### IUCD and Ectopic Pregnancy — A Conceptual Trap

**PYQ 16 (Q682)**

> A P2L2 patient using an IUCD presents to the emergency with vaginal bleeding and recurrent syncope. Most likely diagnosis?
> - A. Ectopic pregnancy
> - B. Recurrent abortion
> - C. Septicaemia
> - D. Coagulation disorder

**Answer: A. Ectopic pregnancy**

The IUCD prevents INTRAUTERINE pregnancy very effectively, but provides LESS protection against ectopic pregnancy. If a woman conceives with an IUCD in situ, the probability that pregnancy is ectopic is higher than in the general population. The IUCD does NOT increase the absolute risk of ectopic pregnancy in users compared with no contraception — but if breakthrough pregnancy occurs, the relative chance of it being ectopic increases.

---

#### LNG-IUS — What It Treats vs. What It Doesn't

**PYQ 17 (Q422/Q594)**

> Levonorgestrel-releasing IUD (LNG-IUS) is helpful in all of the following EXCEPT:
> - A. Menorrhagia
> - B. Dysmenorrhoea
> - C. Endometriosis
> - D. Pelvic inflammatory disease

**Answer: D. Pelvic inflammatory disease**

LNG-IUS is therapeutic for menorrhagia (reduces blood loss ~90%), primary and secondary dysmenorrhoea, and endometriosis (induces endometrial decidualisation/atrophy). It is CONTRAINDICATED in active PID — it is not protective against ascending infection and it should not be inserted in the setting of infection.

---

#### IUCD Displacement — Management

**PYQ 18 (Q413/Q1063)**

> A woman with a copper IUD inserted 2 years ago has absent threads on speculum examination; pelvic ultrasound shows the device partially displaced into the abdominal cavity. Management:
> - A. Hysteroscopy
> - B. No removal needed
> - C. IUCD hook retrieval
> - D. Laparoscopy

**Answer: D. Laparoscopy**

A displaced IUD in the abdominal/peritoneal cavity MUST be removed — it causes bowel adhesions, visceral perforation, and injury if left. Laparoscopic removal is standard. Hysteroscopy is for intracavitary devices with missing threads where the device is still uterine.

---

#### IUCD in Pregnancy — Visible Threads

**PYQ 19 (Q1025)**

> A 28-year-old woman with a 10-week intrauterine gestation presents to the OPD with an IUCD in place. The IUCD thread is clearly visible. She expresses her desire to continue the pregnancy. What is the next best step?
> - A. Leave IUCD inside and continue pregnancy with prophylactic antibiotics
> - B. Leave IUCD inside and continue pregnancy
> - C. Remove IUCD immediately and continue with pregnancy
> - D. Termination of pregnancy due to risk of infection

**Answer: C. Remove IUCD immediately and continue with pregnancy**

If the strings are visible and the woman wants to continue the pregnancy, remove the IUCD to prevent septic abortion (risk ~50% if left), preterm labour, and PROM. If strings are not visible, do NOT remove blindly — proceed with ultrasound-guided management or leave it and counsel about risks.

---

### 5. Barrier Methods

#### Female Condom

**PYQ 20 (Q1 — Q5)**

> A contraceptive image shows a polyurethane sheath with two flexible rings (one inserted, one external). Identify:
> - A. Male condom
> - B. Female condom
> - C. Diaphragm
> - D. Cervical cap

**Answer: B. Female condom**

Female condom: polyurethane sheath inserted into the vagina; the outer ring remains external. Unlike the male condom (latex), it is polyurethane. It provides STI protection (including HIV). Pearl Index ~5% with perfect use, ~21% typical use.

---

#### Contraceptive Sponge

**PYQ 21 (Q604)**

> "Today" contraceptive sponge contains:
> - A. Prostaglandin F2α
> - B. Norethisterone
> - C. Nonoxynol-9
> - D. Copper-releasing mesh

**Answer: C. Nonoxynol-9**

The "Today" sponge is a polyurethane disc impregnated with Nonoxynol-9 (a surfactant-spermicide). It provides both physical barrier and spermicidal action. Nonoxynol-9 also appears in spermicidal gels/creams. It is NOT recommended alone (high failure rate) but acceptable as adjunct.

---

#### Ideal Contraceptive in Cardiac Disease

**PYQ 22 (Q529)**

> Ideal contraceptive for a patient with cardiac disease is:
> - A. Cu-IUCD
> - B. Depot medroxyprogesterone acetate (DMPA)
> - C. Barrier method (diaphragm/condom)
> - D. Combined oral contraceptive pill

**Answer: C. Barrier method (diaphragm/condom)**

Combined OCPs → oestrogen → VTE risk (absolutely contraindicated in Eisenmenger's, severe MS, etc.)
DMPA → fluid retention → can worsen cardiac failure
Cu-IUCD → vasovagal at insertion → haemodynamic risk, prostaglandin-mediated effects
Barrier methods → no systemic effects, no haemodynamic consequences

---

### 6. DMPA (Depot Medroxyprogesterone Acetate)

**PYQ 23 (Q316)**

> DMPA is given once every:
> - A. 3 months
> - B. 6 months
> - C. 9 months
> - D. 45 days

**Answer: A. 3 months (12 weeks)**

150 mg IM every 12 weeks. Key facts:
- Delayed return to fertility: **6–12 months** after last injection
- Causes amenorrhoea (50% after 1 year)
- Safe in lactation (progestin only)
- NOT ideal for women desiring future fertility soon (due to delay in return)

---

### 7. Sterilisation

#### Tubectomy — Named Techniques (High Yield)

| Technique | Method |
|---|---|
| Pomeroy | Loop of tube ligated, segment excised — most common laparoscopic method |
| Modified Pomeroy | Same but with delayed absorbable suture |
| Uchida | Subserosal saline injection, tube elevated and excised |
| Irving | Both ends buried into myometrium and broad ligament — lowest failure rate |
| Madlener | Knuckle of tube crushed and ligated — NOT excised (higher failure rate, rarely used) |
| **Kroener (Fimbriectomy)** | **Fimbrial end removed** |
| Filshie clips | Titanium clips — increasingly used, most reversible |

**PYQ 24 (Q889)**

> Fimbriectomy procedure is known as:
> - A. Uchida method
> - B. Irving method
> - C. Madlener technique
> - D. Kroener method

**Answer: D. Kroener method**

Kroener = Fimbriectomy. Remember: "Kroener cuts the corner (fimbrial end)." The other three techniques all involve a mid-tubal approach. Irving buries both ends and has the LOWEST failure rate.

---

#### Site of Tubal Ligation

**PYQ 25 (Q1019)**

> A woman is undergoing elective tubal ligation for permanent contraception. Which is the most common site selected for ligation, and what is the reason?
> - A. Ampulla – As it has a uniform thickness of muscle
> - B. Ampulla – As it is the widest part
> - C. Ampulla–Isthmus junction – As it is the site of fertilization
> - D. Isthmus – As it is the narrowest part

**Answer: D. Isthmus – As it is the narrowest part**

The isthmus is the narrowest segment, with the most uniform muscle wall thickness and the least blood supply — easiest to ligate, divide, and achieve effective occlusion. Fertilisation actually occurs at the ampullary-isthmic junction, but this is not the reason for choosing the isthmus for ligation.

---

#### Vasectomy — The "Sterility Is Immediate" Trap

**PYQ 26 (Q459/Q638)**

> All of the following about non-scalpel vasectomy are correct EXCEPT:
> - A. Haematoma occurs in up to 5% of subjects
> - B. Sexual function following healing is rarely affected
> - C. Sterility is immediate after vasectomy
> - D. Recanalization is possible

**Answer: C. Sterility is immediate after vasectomy**

This has appeared TWICE (Q459, Q638) — it is a classic NEET-PG trap. After vasectomy, residual sperm remain in the vas deferens distal to the ligation. The patient remains fertile until these sperm are cleared (typically after 20 ejaculations or 12 weeks). Backup contraception is mandatory until azoospermia is confirmed on semen analysis.

---

### 8. Lactational Amenorrhoea Method (LAM)

#### Mechanism

**PYQ 27 (Q697)**

> Lactational amenorrhoea is caused by:
> - A. Prolactin-induced inhibition of GnRH
> - B. Prolactin-induced inhibition of FSH
> - C. Oxytocin-induced inhibition of GnRH
> - D. Oxytocin-induced inhibition of FSH

**Answer: A. Prolactin-induced inhibition of GnRH**

The suckling reflex → pituitary prolactin surge → prolactin directly suppresses hypothalamic GnRH pulsatility → reduced FSH and LH → follicular development fails → anovulation and amenorrhoea. Note: Oxytocin is released by suckling too, but its role is milk let-down, NOT contraception.

---

#### LAM Criteria (ALL THREE Must Be Present)

**The Triple Lock:**

| Criterion | Specification |
|---|---|
| Feeding | Exclusive breastfeeding (no supplemental feeds, bottles, or water) |
| Duration | Infant < 6 months old |
| Menstrual status | Postpartum amenorrhoea (no period since delivery) |

**If any ONE criterion fails, LAM is unreliable and another method should be added.**

**Pearl Index of LAM:** 0.5–2% when all three criteria are met. Rises to 5–10% if criteria are not all met.

---

**PYQ 28 (Q418/Q587)**

> Ideal contraceptive for a woman who is exclusively breastfeeding is:
> - A. Barrier method alone
> - B. Combined oral contraceptive pill
> - C. Lactational amenorrhoea alone
> - D. Progestogen-only pill

**Answer: D. Progestogen-only pill**

LAM alone fails when: infant >6 months, supplemental feeding starts, or periods return. At that point, the ideal additional contraceptive is the progestogen-only pill because it does not suppress prolactin or reduce milk supply.

---

## Bias Removal Section

### Common Misconceptions That Cost Marks

**1. "Higher Pearl Index = Better method"**
WRONG. Pearl Index = pregnancies per 100 woman-years. Lower = better. If you see "Pearl Index 0.05 vs 9," the 0.05 method (implant) is vastly superior to the 9 method (condom).

**2. "Emergency contraception terminates early pregnancy"**
WRONG. EC prevents pregnancy. Once the fertilised egg has implanted, EC has zero effect. This is not a legal or ethical nuance — it is a pharmacological fact. Q7 and Q505 both test this.

**3. "Vasectomy gives immediate sterility"**
WRONG — Q459 and Q638 specifically test this. Backup contraception is needed for 12 weeks / 20 ejaculations.

**4. "Combined OCP is safe in breastfeeding"**
WRONG — oestrogen reduces milk supply. Progestogen-only methods are safe.

**5. "Uterine malformation is an absolute contraindication to IUCD"**
WRONG — it is RELATIVE, not absolute.

**6. "Multiload Cu-375 is a third-generation IUD"**
WRONG — it is second-generation. Third = Cu-T 380A, GyneFix.

---

## Variations and Twists

### Scenario: Postpartum Contraception

| Situation | Best method |
|---|---|
| Exclusively breastfeeding, < 6 months | LAM + POP (belt-and-suspenders) |
| Not breastfeeding | Any method from 3 weeks postpartum |
| Lactating at 6 months | Add POP/Cu-IUCD |
| Wants permanent contraception | Tubectomy (day 2–3 postpartum or interval) |
| Wants reversible long-term | Cu-IUCD (can insert immediately postpartum) |
| Cardiac disease, lactating | Barrier + POP |

### Scenario: Missed Periods While on OCP

If a woman on combined OCP misses her period, first step = urine pregnancy test. Do NOT assume OCP side effect without ruling out pregnancy.

### Scenario: IUCD and Pregnancy (2022 actual PYQ Q988)

> A 24-year-old lactating female with an 18-month-old child with irregular, heavy bleeding seeking contraceptive advice:

Answer: "Mala" — In the Indian National Family Planning Programme, "Mala-N" and "Mala-D" are the official brand names of combined OCPs distributed through government health system. With heavy irregular bleeding, a combined OCP helps regulate cycles and reduce blood loss. At 18 months, LAM criteria are no longer met (infant too old).

---

## Memory Engineering

### Pearl Index Table (Memorise This Sequence)

| Method | Pearl Index (typical use) |
|---|---|
| **Implants** | **0.05** ← LOWEST |
| LNG-IUS (Mirena) | 0.1–0.2 |
| Male sterilisation (vasectomy) | 0.1 |
| Female sterilisation (tubectomy) | 0.5 |
| Combined OCP | 0.3–9 |
| DMPA (injectable) | 0.3–3 |
| Copper IUD | 0.6–0.8 |
| Progestogen-only pill | 0.3–9 |
| Male condom | 2–15 |
| Female condom | 5–21 |
| Diaphragm + spermicide | 6–12 |
| LAM (all 3 criteria met) | 0.5–2 |
| Spermicide alone | 18–29 |
| No contraception | 85 |

**Memory hook for the bottom of the table:** "SDNF" — Spermicide, Diaphragm, No method, Female condom — Pearl Indices go from bad to catastrophic.

### LAM Criteria Anchor: "EAM"
- **E**xclusive breastfeeding
- **A**menorrhoea (no period)
- **M**onths < 6 (infant age)
All three = effective. Miss one = add another method.

### Emergency Contraception Window Mnemonic: "3-5-5"
- LNG: effective to **3** days (72h), works to **5** days with reduced efficacy
- UPA: works for full **5** days (120h), more effective than LNG
- Cu-T: works for **5** days, **most** effective option overall

---

## Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### Emergency Contraception Decision Table

| Presentation | Preferred Choice |
|---|---|
| Within 72h, routine | LNG 1.5 mg single dose |
| 72–120h post-coitus | UPA 30 mg (more effective) |
| Any time within 120h, all circumstances | Cu-T 380A (+ future contraception bonus) |
| On enzyme-inducing drugs (rifampicin, phenytoin) | Cu-T 380A (hormonal EC reduced efficacy) |
| BMI > 26, within 72h | Cu-T 380A preferred (LNG efficacy reduced) |
| Rape / sexual assault | Cu-T 380A or UPA (+ STI prophylaxis) |

### Top 5 Traps in Contraception MCQs

1. **"Sterility immediate after vasectomy" = FALSE** — the most-tested vasectomy fact
2. **Pearl Index confusion** — higher number = worse; subdermal implant = lowest
3. **Combined OCP in lactation** — contraindicated; not progestogen-only
4. **Multiload Cu-375 = 2nd generation** (not 3rd)
5. **IUCD and ectopic** — IUCD prevents IUP, if breakthrough pregnancy occurs, higher relative chance it is ectopic

---

## All Chapter 5 PYQs Integrated

### Q-Bank Practice Block

---

### Q1 — Female condom identification

A contraceptive image shows a polyurethane sheath with two flexible rings (one inserted, one external). Identify:

- A. Male condom
- B. Female condom
- C. Diaphragm
- D. Cervical cap

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Female condom** — Polyurethane sheath; inserted into vagina; provides STI protection. Diaphragm covers cervix; cervical cap is smaller.

</details>

---

### Q2 — Progestasert IUCD: daily hormone release

The quantity of hormone released by the Progestasert IUCD per day is:

- A. 25 µg
- B. 45 µg
- C. 65 µg
- D. 85 µg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. 65 µg** — Progestasert contains 38 mg progesterone in a silicone oil reservoir; releases 65 µg/day. Compare LNG-IUS/Mirena: 20 µg/day, valid 5 years.

</details>

---

### Q3 — Emergency contraception: NOT a mechanism

Emergency contraception prevents pregnancy by all mechanisms EXCEPT:

- A. Delaying or inhibiting ovulation
- B. Inhibiting fertilisation
- C. Preventing implantation of the fertilised egg
- D. Interrupting an early established pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Interrupting an early established pregnancy** — Once implantation is complete, EC is ineffective and any intervention constitutes termination, not contraception.

</details>

---

### Q4 — Lowest pregnancy failure rate

Which contraceptive has the LEAST pregnancy failure rate?

- A. OCP
- B. Cu-IUCD
- C. Diaphragm
- D. Condom

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. OCP** — Among these four options, OCP has the lowest Pearl Index. (Note: implants are even lower if that option is present.)

</details>

---

### Q5 — Emergency contraception progestogen of choice

Progestogen of choice for emergency contraception?

- A. Norethisterone
- B. Medroxyprogesterone
- C. Oxytocin
- D. Levonorgestrel

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Levonorgestrel** — 1.5 mg single dose within 72h (up to 120h). Ulipristal 30 mg is more effective at 72–120h.

</details>

---

### Q6 — Ulipristal acetate dose

Dose of ulipristal acetate for emergency contraception?

- A. 300 mg
- B. 30 mg
- C. 300 µg
- D. 30 µg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 30 mg** — Selective progesterone receptor modulator; single 30 mg PO within 120h.

</details>

---

### Q7 — DMPA dosing interval

DMPA is given once every:

- A. 3 months
- B. 6 months
- C. 9 months
- D. 45 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. 3 months (12 weeks)** — 150 mg IM every 12 weeks. Delays return to fertility ~6–9 months.

</details>

---

### Q8 — Subdermal implants: failure rate

A young woman wants the most effective reversible long-acting contraception with the lowest Pearl Index:

- A. Subdermal implants (etonogestrel)
- B. Combined oral contraceptive pill
- C. Condom
- D. Copper IUD

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Subdermal implants** — Pearl Index 0.05 per 100 woman-years — the lowest failure rate among all reversible methods. Effective for 3 years, no user compliance.

</details>

---

### Q9 — Combined OCP: contraindicated in lactation

A lactating woman 6 weeks postpartum. Which contraceptive is NOT advisable?

- A. Combined oral contraceptive pill
- B. Progestogen-only pill
- C. Copper IUD
- D. DMPA

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Combined oral contraceptive pill** — Oestrogen suppresses prolactin → reduces breast milk volume. All progestogen-only methods and IUDs are safe during breastfeeding.

</details>

---

### Q10 — Non-scalpel vasectomy: false statement

All of the following about non-scalpel vasectomy are correct EXCEPT:

- A. Haematoma occurs in up to 5% of subjects
- B. Sexual function following healing is rarely affected
- C. Sterility is immediate after vasectomy
- D. Recanalization is possible

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Sterility is immediate after vasectomy** — NOT immediate; residual sperm remain. Backup contraception until azoospermia confirmed (~12 weeks or 20 ejaculations).

</details>

---

### Q11 — IUD absolute contraindication

Absolute contraindications to IUCD are all EXCEPT:

- A. Pregnancy
- B. Undiagnosed vaginal bleeding
- C. Pelvic inflammatory disease
- D. Uterine malformation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Uterine malformation** — Relative, not absolute contraindication. The others (pregnancy, unexplained bleeding, active PID) are absolute.

</details>

---

### Q12 — Most common side effect of IUCD

The most common side effect of IUCD insertion is:

- A. Bleeding (menorrhagia)
- B. Pain/dysmenorrhoea
- C. Pelvic infection
- D. Ectopic pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Bleeding (menorrhagia)** — Copper IUCDs increase menstrual blood loss ~30–50%; most common reason for discontinuation. LNG-IUS reduces blood loss.

</details>

---

### Q13 — Ideal contraceptive for cardiac disease

Ideal contraceptive for a patient with cardiac disease:

- A. Cu-IUCD
- B. DMPA
- C. Barrier method
- D. Combined OCP

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Barrier method** — No systemic haemodynamic or thrombotic effects. Combined OCP → VTE risk; DMPA → fluid retention; Cu-IUCD → vasovagal risk.

</details>

---

### Q14 — LNG-IUS: NOT indicated for

Levonorgestrel-releasing IUD is helpful in all EXCEPT:

- A. Menorrhagia
- B. Dysmenorrhoea
- C. Endometriosis
- D. Pelvic inflammatory disease

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Pelvic inflammatory disease** — LNG-IUS is contraindicated in active PID; it does not treat or prevent PID.

</details>

---

### Q15 — Lactational amenorrhoea mechanism

Lactational amenorrhoea is caused by:

- A. Prolactin-induced inhibition of GnRH
- B. Prolactin-induced inhibition of FSH
- C. Oxytocin-induced inhibition of GnRH
- D. Oxytocin-induced inhibition of FSH

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Prolactin-induced inhibition of GnRH** — Suckling → prolactin → suppresses hypothalamic GnRH → reduced FSH/LH → anovulation. Effective only with exclusive breastfeeding, < 6 months, and amenorrhoea.

</details>

---

### Q16 — Ideal contraceptive for lactating mother

Ideal contraceptive for a lactating mother:

- A. Barrier method
- B. Combined OCP
- C. Lactational amenorrhoea alone
- D. Progestogen-only pill

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Progestogen-only pill** — Does not suppress prolactin or milk production. LAM alone is unreliable beyond 6 months.

</details>

---

### Q17 — OCP mechanism: FALSE

All of the following are mechanisms of action of combined OCPs EXCEPT:

- A. Inhibition of ovulation
- B. Spermicidal effect
- C. Interference with implantation
- D. Interference with placental function

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Interference with placental function** — Once placentation occurs, OCPs have no effect.

</details>

---

### Q18 — POP: not a side effect

Which is not a side effect of POP (progestogen-only pill)?

- A. Ovarian cysts
- B. Venous thromboembolism
- C. Increased risk of diabetes mellitus
- D. Ectopic pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Venous thromboembolism** — VTE is an oestrogen effect. POPs are safe in women with VTE risk.

</details>

---

### Q19 — Progestogen-only pill: NOT a mechanism

Which of the following is NOT a mechanism of action of progesterone-only pills?

- A. Thicken the cervical mucus
- B. Prevent ovulation
- C. Kill the sperms in the cervical canal
- D. Inhibit penetration of sperm

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Kill the sperms in the cervical canal** — POPs are not spermicidal.

</details>

---

### Q20 — Progesterone effect (2018)

What is the effect of progesterone-only pills?

- A. Completely suppresses ovulation
- B. Thin lining of uterus
- C. Thick cervical mucus
- D. All of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. All of the above** — All three are accepted effects.

</details>

---

### Q21 — Progestogen-only pill — missed dose advice

A 26-year-old on a POP usually takes it at 10 am but took it yesterday at 5 pm. Advice:

- A. No need to worry; continue same contraceptive
- B. Use additional contraception for the next 48 hours
- C. Change over to combined OCP
- D. Take double dose next morning

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Use additional contraception for the next 48 hours** — Traditional POP: 3-hour window; desogestrel: 12-hour window. If taken outside window, add barrier for 48 hours.

</details>

---

### Q22 — Fimbriectomy method

Fimbriectomy procedure is known as:

- A. Uchida method
- B. Irving method
- C. Madlener technique
- D. Kroener method

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Kroener method** — Removes fimbrial end. Uchida = saline injection; Irving = bury both ends; Madlener = crush a loop.

</details>

---

### Q23 — Site of tubal ligation

Most common site for tubal ligation:

- A. Ampulla – uniform muscle thickness
- B. Ampulla – widest part
- C. Ampulla–Isthmus junction – site of fertilization
- D. Isthmus – narrowest part

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Isthmus – As it is the narrowest part** — Easiest to ligate, least blood supply, uniform muscle wall.

</details>

---

### Q24 — IUCD and ectopic pregnancy

A P2L2 patient using an IUCD presents with vaginal bleeding and recurrent syncope. Most likely diagnosis?

- A. Ectopic pregnancy
- B. Recurrent abortion
- C. Septicaemia
- D. Coagulation disorder

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Ectopic pregnancy** — IUCD protects against intrauterine pregnancy but not ectopic. Syncope + vaginal bleeding in reproductive-age woman = ectopic until proven otherwise.

</details>

---

### Q25 — IUCD: false statement (2016)

Which of the following is false about IUDs?

- A. Multiload Cu-375 is a third-generation IUD
- B. Copper devices are effective as post-coital contraceptives
- C. LNG-20 (Mirena) has an effective life of 5 years
- D. Pregnancy rates of Lippes Loop and T Cu-200 are similar

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Multiload Cu-375 is a third-generation IUD** — It is second-generation. Third-generation = Cu-T 380A and frameless GyneFix.

</details>

---

### Q26 — IUCD absolute contraindication (2022)

Which is an absolute contraindication for insertion of the IUCD shown?

- A. Menstruation
- B. Trophoblastic disease
- C. Immediately after delivery
- D. Ruptured condom during intercourse

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Trophoblastic disease** — GTD is absolute contraindication. IUCD can be inserted during menstruation, immediately postpartum, and as emergency contraception after unprotected sex.

</details>

---

### Q27 — IUCD displaced into abdomen

Copper IUD with absent threads; ultrasound shows partial displacement into abdominal cavity. Management:

- A. Hysteroscopy
- B. No removal needed
- C. IUCD hook retrieval
- D. Laparoscopy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Laparoscopy** — Abdominal/peritoneal displacement must be removed laparoscopically to prevent bowel adhesions and visceral injury.

</details>

---

### Q28 — Progestin IUCD and upper genital tract infection

All explain reduced risk of upper genital tract infection with progestin IUDs EXCEPT:

- A. Reduced retrograde menstruation
- B. Decreased ovulation
- C. Thickened cervical mucus
- D. Decidual changes in the endometrium

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Decreased ovulation** — The protective mechanisms against ascending infection are: thickened cervical mucus, reduced retrograde menstruation, decidualisation. Ovulation suppression is not the mechanism.

</details>

---

### Q29 — "Today" contraceptive sponge

"Today" contraceptive sponge contains:

- A. Prostaglandin F2α
- B. Norethisterone
- C. Nonoxynol-9
- D. Copper-releasing mesh

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Nonoxynol-9** — Polyurethane sponge impregnated with Nonoxynol-9 spermicide; provides barrier + spermicidal action.

</details>

---

### Q30 — IUCD in pregnancy, visible threads (2024)

A 28-year-old at 10 weeks with IUCD in place, thread visible, wants to continue pregnancy. Next step?

- A. Leave IUCD inside with prophylactic antibiotics
- B. Leave IUCD inside and continue pregnancy
- C. Remove IUCD immediately and continue pregnancy
- D. Termination of pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Remove IUCD immediately and continue with pregnancy** — Removing IUCD (when threads visible) reduces risk of septic abortion (~50% risk if left in), preterm labour, and PROM.

</details>

---

### Q31 — Lactating female with heavy bleeding seeking contraception (2022)

A 24-year-old lactating female with 18-month-old child, irregular heavy bleeding, seeks contraception:

- A. Progestasert
- B. Copper-T 380A
- C. Mala
- D. Norethisterone enanthate depot injection

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Mala** — "Mala-D" and "Mala-N" are combined OCP brands in India's National Family Planning Programme. With heavy irregular bleeding, combined OCP regulates cycles and reduces blood loss. At 18 months, LAM criteria are not met (infant too old).

</details>

---




---

# Chapter 6 — Infertility and Assisted Reproductive Technology (ART)

> *"Infertility questions reward the candidate who understands sequence — what to investigate first, what each test tells you, and when to escalate to ART. Follow the logic and the marks follow you."*

---

## Big Picture First

### WHO Definitions (Current, Exam-Ready)

- **Infertility:** Failure to achieve a clinical pregnancy after **12 months** of regular, unprotected sexual intercourse in women **< 35 years**
- **Infertility in women ≥ 35:** Evaluate after **6 months** (accelerated timeline due to declining ovarian reserve)
- **Primary infertility:** No previous pregnancy ever
- **Secondary infertility:** Previous pregnancy (including ectopic, miscarriage), currently unable to conceive
- **Subfertility:** Reduced capacity to conceive — used interchangeably with infertility in most exams

### Prevalence and Causes

In India: ~10–15% of couples affected.

Distribution of causes:
| Cause | Approximate proportion |
|---|---|
| Male factor | 30–40% |
| Female factor (ovulatory) | 25–30% |
| Female factor (tubal/pelvic) | 25–35% |
| Unexplained | 10–15% |
| Combined (both partners) | 20–30% |

**Exam insight:** Questions frequently test male factor first because it is simple, non-invasive, and cheap to investigate (semen analysis). Always investigate BOTH partners simultaneously.

### Investigation Sequence — The Logical Order

```
Step 1:  Semen analysis (male) — cheap, non-invasive, rules in/out male factor
Step 2:  Document ovulation (female)
            - Day 21 serum progesterone (> 5 ng/mL = ovulatory)
            - LH urinary kit (LH surge)
            - BBT charting (biphasic)
            - TVS follicle monitoring
Step 3:  Tubal patency (female)
            - HSG first-line
            - Laparoscopy + chromopertubation if HSG abnormal
Step 4:  Uterine cavity assessment
            - HSG (basic)
            - Hysteroscopy (definitive)
            - Saline sonohysterography
Step 5:  Ovarian reserve
            - AMH (best single marker)
            - Day 3 FSH + estradiol
            - Antral follicle count (AFC) on TVS
```

---

## Question Pattern Map

Recurring PYQ themes from 2015–2024:

| Theme | Frequency |
|---|---|
| Bilateral cornual block on HSG — management | Very High |
| WHO 2021 semen analysis reference values | High |
| Semen terminology (aspermia vs azoospermia vs asthenospermia) | High |
| Hydrosalpinx on HSG — diagnosis and IVF implication | High |
| OHSS — diagnosis, severity, management | High |
| Asherman syndrome — post-curettage amenorrhoea | High |
| Ovulation induction: clomiphene vs letrozole | High |
| Obstructive azoospermia — clinical features | Medium |
| Genital TB — tubal involvement, HSG features | Medium |
| IVF sequence, PGT (preimplantation genetic testing) | Medium |
| Endometriosis and infertility | Medium |

---

## Core Concept Deep Dive

### 1. Semen Analysis — WHO 2021 Reference Values

This is the single most frequently tested ART topic. The WHO 6th Edition (2021) significantly revised normal values downward from previous editions.

#### WHO 2021 Lower Reference Limits (5th percentile)

| Parameter | WHO 2021 (6th edition) | WHO 2010 (5th edition — OLD) |
|---|---|---|
| Volume | ≥ **1.4 mL** | ≥ 1.5 mL |
| Sperm concentration | ≥ **16 million/mL** | ≥ 15 million/mL |
| Total sperm count | ≥ **39 million/ejaculate** | ≥ 39 million |
| Total motility (PR + NP) | ≥ **42%** | ≥ 40% |
| Progressive motility | ≥ **30%** | ≥ 32% |
| Morphology (strict Kruger) | ≥ **4%** | ≥ 4% |
| Vitality | ≥ **54%** | ≥ 58% |

**PYQ 1 (Q350)**

> WHO 2021 lower reference limit for sperm concentration:
> - A. 5 million/mL
> - B. 10 million/mL
> - C. 16 million/mL
> - D. 20 million/mL

**Answer: C. 16 million/mL**

The 2021 WHO 6th edition number is 16 million/mL. Option A (5 million) = severe oligospermia. Option D (20 million) = the pre-2010 threshold. This is a direct recall question — the number must be committed to memory.

---

### 2. Semen Terminology — Extremely Exam Friendly

These definitions are tested repeatedly and frequently confused:

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Aspermia** | COMPLETE absence of ejaculate (no semen volume at all) |
| **Azoospermia** | No sperm in the ejaculate (but semen present) |
| **Oligospermia** | Sperm count < 16 million/mL (WHO 2021) |
| **Asthenozoospermia** | Reduced sperm motility |
| **Teratozoospermia** | Abnormal sperm morphology (< 4% normal forms) |
| **Oligoasthenoteratozoospermia (OAT)** | All three abnormalities combined |
| **Hypospermia** | Low ejaculate volume (< 1.4 mL) |
| **Hyperspermia** | High ejaculate volume (> 6 mL) |

**PYQ 2 (Q532)**

> Aspermia refers to:
> - A. Complete absence of semen (ejaculate)
> - B. Absence of sperm in the ejaculate (azoospermia)
> - C. Absence of sperm motility (asthenozoospermia)
> - D. Occurrence of abnormal sperm morphology (teratozoospermia)

**Answer: A. Complete absence of semen**

Aspermia = no ejaculate at all (retrograde ejaculation into the bladder, or ejaculatory duct obstruction). The ejaculate volume is zero. Contrast with azoospermia where ejaculate is present but contains no sperm. This distinction is a classic NEET trap.

---

### 3. Male Factor Infertility — Clinical Investigation Logic

#### Obstructive vs. Non-Obstructive Azoospermia

**PYQ 3 (Q671)**

> A 25-year-old man presents with azoospermia. Testes are normal sized; FSH and testosterone are normal. Most likely cause?
> - A. Obstructed vas deferens
> - B. Kallmann syndrome
> - C. Klinefelter syndrome
> - D. Noonan syndrome

**Answer: A. Obstructed vas deferens**

This clinical vignette demonstrates the diagnostic algorithm:
- **Normal FSH** = pituitary is working normally = testes are receiving gonadotropin signal
- **Normal testicular size** = testes are producing sperm normally
- **Azoospermia** = sperm are produced but cannot reach the ejaculate

This is **obstructive azoospermia** (OA). The obstruction is in the vas, epididymis, or ejaculatory ducts. Treatment: surgical sperm retrieval (PESA, TESA, MESA) + ICSI.

Compare:
- **Klinefelter (47,XXY)** = small testes + elevated FSH + azoospermia (non-obstructive)
- **Kallmann syndrome** = small testes + LOW FSH/LH + anosmia (hypogonadotropic hypogonadism)
- **Noonan syndrome** = elevated FSH + variable testicular function

---

#### Key Differentiator: FSH in Azoospermia

| FSH level | Testes size | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| Normal/Low | Normal | Obstructive azoospermia (OA) |
| Elevated | Small | Non-obstructive (spermatogenic failure) |
| Very Low | Small | Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (Kallmann, pituitary failure) |

---

### 4. Female Factor — Ovulation Assessment

#### When to Measure Progesterone

**PYQ 4 (Q607)**

> Best day for serum progesterone evaluation in a 28-day cycle to confirm ovulation:
> - A. Day 3
> - B. Day 8
> - C. Day 14
> - D. Day 21

**Answer: D. Day 21**

Mid-luteal progesterone is measured 7 days before the next expected period. In a 28-day cycle, that is day 21 (midluteal = approximately day 14 + 7). A level > 5 ng/mL (some sources say > 10 ng/mL) confirms ovulation. Day 3 is for FSH/estradiol (ovarian reserve). Day 14 is for LH surge detection.

---

#### Indicators of Anovulation (and Their MCQ Relevance)

Anovulatory infertility classically presents as:
- Irregular/oligomenorrhoea + difficulty conceiving
- Low mid-luteal progesterone (< 3 ng/mL)
- No biphasic BBT chart
- No dominant follicle on TVS monitoring

**PYQ 5 (Q388)**

> A 35-year-old obese woman (BMI 34) presents with irregular, heavy, unpredictable periods. Endometrial biopsy shows simple hyperplasia without atypia; serum progesterone in luteal phase < 3 ng/mL. The mechanism of her AUB is:
> - A. AUB-L (leiomyoma)
> - B. AUB-O (anovulatory cycles with unopposed oestrogen)
> - C. AUB-C (coagulopathy)
> - D. AUB-P (endometrial polyp)

**Answer: B. AUB-O**

Obesity → peripheral aromatisation of androgens to oestrone → unopposed oestrogen stimulates endometrium → no progesterone opposition (low luteal progesterone confirms anovulation) → irregular heavy bleeding + endometrial hyperplasia. This patient also has infertility potential (PCOS likely).

---

### 5. Ovarian Reserve

#### Markers of Ovarian Reserve

**PYQ 6 (Q322)**

> All are markers of ovarian reserve EXCEPT:
> - A. Inhibin A
> - B. Estradiol (day 3)
> - C. Inhibin B
> - D. Antral follicle count / ovarian volume

**Answer: A. Inhibin A**

Ovarian reserve markers: AMH (best), Inhibin B, Day-3 FSH, Day-3 estradiol, Antral Follicle Count (AFC), ovarian volume.

Inhibin A is produced mainly by: (1) dominant follicle in follicular phase, and (2) corpus luteum in luteal phase → most importantly, by the PLACENTA during pregnancy. It is used in the Down syndrome screening "quad test," NOT as an ovarian reserve marker.

---

#### Premature Ovarian Insufficiency (POI)

**PYQ 7 (Q321)**

> Which hormone is elevated in premature ovarian failure?
> - A. Serum inhibin
> - B. Serum FSH
> - C. Serum estradiol
> - D. Both A and B

**Answer: B. Serum FSH**

POI (formerly POF): FSH > 25–40 IU/L on two occasions (at least 1 month apart), age < 40 years. Pattern: elevated FSH, elevated LH, low estradiol, very low AMH, low inhibin B. Inhibin DECREASES in POI (not increases) because the granulosa cells are depleted.

**PYQ 8 (Q11 — clinical vignette)**

> 36-year-old P2L2 with 9 months amenorrhoea, FSH 36, LH 56, AMH 0.5. Diagnosis?

**Answer: B. Premature ovarian failure (POI)**

The combination of elevated FSH/LH + very low AMH + amenorrhoea in a woman < 40 years = POI. Normal range for FSH is 2–10 IU/L in the follicular phase; FSH > 25 on two separate occasions = diagnostic of POI.

---

### 6. Tubal Factor Infertility — HSG and Laparoscopy

#### HSG: When to Perform

**PYQ 9 (Q351)**

> Best timing for HSG in the menstrual cycle:
> - A. Day 2–5
> - B. Day 6–12
> - C. Day 14–16
> - D. Day 21–28

**Answer: B. Day 6–12**

Reasons: Post-menstrual (avoids pregnant state if cycle fails), pre-ovulatory (endometrium thin → better visualisation), before ovulation occurs (no risk of exposing a fertilised egg to radiation or contrast). Days 2–5 = menses may be ongoing. Days 14+ = risk of disrupting ovulation or early pregnancy.

---

#### PID and Infertility — First Investigation

**PYQ 10 (Q614)**

> A 22-year-old with history of PID presents with 12 months of infertility, normal menses, normal examination. Next investigation:
> - A. Hysterosalpingography (HSG)
> - B. AMH
> - C. Hysteroscopy
> - D. CA-125

**Answer: A. HSG**

History of PID specifically raises concern for tubal damage (salpingitis → peritubal adhesions → hydrosalpinx → blocked tubes). HSG is the first-line test for tubal patency after PID. AMH assesses ovarian reserve (not the priority here). Hysteroscopy is for intrauterine pathology. CA-125 is a tumour marker for ovarian cancer (not useful for routine infertility).

---

#### Bilateral Cornual Block — Management Decision

This question has appeared in multiple years (Q664, Q704, Q784, Q1059) with consistent answer. Master it.

**PYQ 11 (Q664/Q784/Q1059)**

> A woman presents with infertility and bilateral tubal block at the cornua on HSG. Best management?
> - A. Laparoscopy and hysteroscopy
> - B. Hydrotubation
> - C. IVF
> - D. Tuboplasty

**Answer: A. Laparoscopy and hysteroscopy**

**The Clinical Reasoning:**
Cornual (proximal) block on HSG is very frequently a FALSE POSITIVE — due to:
1. Tubal spasm at the time of contrast injection
2. Mucous plugs at the cornua
3. Endometrial debris occluding the ostia

Laparoscopy + chromopertubation (methylene blue dye) confirms true tubal status under direct vision. Hysteroscopy simultaneously allows cannulation of the cornual ostia.

If laparoscopy confirms TRUE bilateral cornual block → then IVF is the treatment of choice (bypass the tubes entirely).

**Hydrotubation** (injection of fluid under pressure) is obsolete and may cause iatrogenic infection.

**Why NOT IVF first?** Because up to 40–60% of cornual blocks on HSG are spasm artefacts — no point committing to expensive ART without confirming true occlusion.

---

#### Hydrosalpinx and IVF

**PYQ 12 (Q668)**

> An HSG of a 26-year-old infertile woman shows blocked fallopian tubes with fluid-filled dilatation at the fimbrial end. Diagnosis?
> - A. Hydrosalpinx
> - B. Retroverted fixed uterus
> - C. Uterine fibroids
> - D. Cervical fibroid

**Answer: A. Hydrosalpinx**

Hydrosalpinx = distal tubal occlusion (fimbrial end) + accumulation of serous fluid → clubs the tube into a sausage-shaped structure. HSG shows a clubbed, blind-ending tube.

**Critical ART implication:** Hydrosalpinx fluid contains inflammatory cytokines, endotoxins, and free radicals that are TOXIC to IVF embryos if they reflux into the uterine cavity. Hydrosalpinx reduces IVF success rates by ~50%. Before IVF, the hydrosalpinx must be dealt with — either:
- Laparoscopic **salpingectomy** (removes the tube entirely) — preferred
- Laparoscopic **proximal occlusion** (if salpingectomy not feasible)

**PYQ 13 (Q1026) — 2024 actual paper**

> A 29-year-old woman with infertility and chronic pelvic pain. TVS reveals adnexal mass with incomplete septations and a cogwheel appearance. Most likely diagnosis?
> - A. Hydrosalpinx
> - B. Follicular ovarian cyst
> - C. Endometriosis
> - D. Haemorrhagic ovarian cyst

**Answer: A. Hydrosalpinx**

The "cogwheel sign" on TVS (cross-sectional view showing mural nodules/mucosal folds) is PATHOGNOMONIC of hydrosalpinx. Incomplete septations (actually tubal folds appearing as septa) are another feature.

---

### 7. Genital Tuberculosis and Infertility

**PYQ 14 (Q323/Q621)**

> Most common site of female genital TB?
> - A. Fallopian tubes
> - B. Endometrium
> - C. Ovaries
> - D. Vulva/vagina

**Answer: A. Fallopian tubes (~100%)**

The hierarchy of genital TB involvement:
1. Fallopian tubes — virtually 100% (hallmark organ)
2. Endometrium — 60–80%
3. Ovaries — 20–30%
4. Cervix — 5%
5. Vagina/vulva — <1%

Genital TB is a common cause of tubal factor infertility in India. Always consider in a young woman presenting with:
- Infertility + amenorrhoea + constitutional features (fever, weight loss)
- Bilateral tubal block on HSG (especially pipe-stem or beaded pattern)
- Asherman syndrome-like picture

**PYQ 15 (Q686)**

> 32-year-old with 6-month secondary amenorrhoea, chronic pelvic pain, intermittent fever, weight loss, and adnexal mass. Most likely diagnosis?
> - A. PCOS
> - B. Endometriosis
> - C. Genital tuberculosis
> - D. Ectopic pregnancy

**Answer: C. Genital tuberculosis**

Constitutional features (fever, weight loss) + secondary amenorrhoea + adnexal mass in an endemic region = genital TB. Diagnostic gold standard: endometrial biopsy during menstruation (culture, PCR, histology for caseating granulomas with Langhans giant cells).

---

#### HSG Features of Genital TB

**PYQ 16 (Q844)**

> HSG findings suggestive of genital TB?
> - A. Beaded tubes
> - B. Honeycomb uterus
> - C. Golf-club tube
> - D. All of the above

**Answer: D. All of the above**

Genital TB on HSG shows:
- **Beaded/pipestem tubes** (TB salpingitis with fibrosis)
- **Golf-club appearance** (irregular narrowing at ampullary end)
- **Honeycomb uterus** (multiple filling defects = intrauterine synechiae)
- Feathering of the mucosal pattern
- Calcification in healed lesions

---

### 8. Asherman Syndrome

**PYQ 17 (Q667)**

> A woman presents with secondary amenorrhoea following curettage for abortion. FSH is 7 IU/L. Most likely diagnosis?
> - A. Pituitary failure
> - B. Ovarian failure
> - C. Uterine synechiae (Asherman syndrome)
> - D. Pregnancy

**Answer: C. Uterine synechiae (Asherman syndrome)**

The diagnostic key is **normal FSH** (7 IU/L is normal). This excludes:
- Pituitary failure (FSH would be low)
- Ovarian failure (FSH would be high, >25)
- Pregnancy (would need beta-hCG, but FSH being normal + curettage history → Asherman is the answer)

Asherman syndrome: Intrauterine adhesions (synechiae) destroy the functional endometrium post-curettage. Diagnosis by **hysteroscopy** (gold standard) or HSG (filling defects). Treatment: hysteroscopic adhesiolysis + high-dose oestrogen (2–4 mg/day for 4–6 weeks) to re-epithelialise.

**PYQ 18 (Q992) — 2023 actual paper**

> For which procedure can hysteroscopy preferably be done?
> - A. Tubal ligation
> - B. Asherman syndrome
> - C. Endocervical polyp
> - D. Subserosal fibroid

**Answer: B. Asherman syndrome**

Tubal ligation requires laparoscopy. Subserosal fibroids are outside the uterine cavity — laparoscopic myomectomy. Endocervical polyp may be managed with simple avulsion or hysteroscopic resection (both possible, but hysteroscopy is more definitive). Asherman syndrome: hysteroscopic adhesiolysis is the treatment — definitive and diagnostic at the same time.

---

### 9. Endometriosis and Infertility

**PYQ 19 (Q672)**

> 23-year-old with lower abdominal pain and dyspareunia; retroverted uterus with adnexal and cervical motion tenderness. Most important long-term sequela?
> - A. Recurrent pregnancy loss
> - B. Infertility
> - C. Pelvic organ prolapse
> - D. Amenorrhoea

**Answer: B. Infertility**

Endometriosis causes infertility through multiple mechanisms:
1. **Peritubal/periovarian adhesions** → mechanical obstruction
2. **Peritoneal toxins** (prostaglandins, cytokines) → hostile peritoneal environment for gametes
3. **Endometriomas** → compresses ovarian reserve, surgical cystectomy further reduces AMH
4. **Impaired folliculogenesis** and oocyte quality
5. **Uterine receptivity** — altered endometrial gene expression

~30–50% of infertile women have endometriosis. ~50% of women with endometriosis have some degree of infertility.

---

**PYQ 20 (Q833) — 2016 actual paper**

> Examination of a 26-year-old obese infertile female: Fixed retroverted uterus and nodularity of the uterosacral ligaments. Most likely diagnosis?
> - A. PCOS
> - B. Endometriosis
> - C. Adenomyosis
> - D. TB

**Answer: B. Endometriosis**

Fixed retroverted uterus (adhesions pulling uterus posteriorly) + nodular uterosacral ligaments = pathognomonic combination for endometriosis. The posterior cul-de-sac (Pouch of Douglas) becomes obliterated by adhesions and endometriotic deposits. Gold standard diagnosis: laparoscopy with histological confirmation.

---

**PYQ 21 (Q673) — Severe endometriosis: management**

> 29-year-old nulliparous woman with endometriosis and infertility: laparoscopy shows severe pelvic adhesions, "kissing ovaries" (each 6–8 cm cystic), bowel stuck to fundus. Best next management?
> - A. GnRH analogues
> - B. Combined OCP
> - C. Hysterectomy with bilateral oophorectomy
> - D. Bilateral cystectomy, adhesiolysis, and prepare for IVF

**Answer: D. Bilateral cystectomy, adhesiolysis, and prepare for IVF**

In a young nulliparous woman desiring fertility with severe endometriosis:
- GnRH analogues alone → reduce pain but do NOT restore fertility; 50% recurrence off treatment
- Combined OCP → for pain management, not fertility
- Hysterectomy/oophorectomy → radical surgery, destroys fertility (last resort in non-fertility-desiring patients)
- Conservative surgery → maximises natural and IVF-assisted fertility

Standard approach: conservative laparoscopic surgery (cystectomy, adhesiolysis, excision of deposits) → attempt natural conception for 6 months → proceed to IVF.

---

### 10. Ovulation Induction

#### Clomiphene Citrate

Clomiphene citrate (CC) = selective oestrogen receptor modulator (SERM).

**Mechanism:**
- Blocks hypothalamic oestrogen receptors → hypothalamus "thinks" oestrogen is low → increases GnRH → increases FSH/LH → stimulates follicular development

**Anti-oestrogenic effects (side effects from mechanism):**
- **Thin endometrium** (oestrogen receptors blocked in uterus)
- **Thick cervical mucus** (less favourable for sperm)
- **Hot flushes**
- Ovarian cysts
- Multiple pregnancy risk (5–10%)

**Dose:** 50 mg/day, days 2–6 or 3–7 of cycle. Can increase to 100–150 mg if no response.

**Limitation:** Despite increasing ovulation rate to ~70%, pregnancy rate is only ~15–40% per cycle — partly due to anti-oestrogenic endometrial and cervical effects.

---

#### Letrozole — Current First-Line in PCOS

**PYQ 22 (Q352)**

> First-line ovulation induction agent in PCOS infertility (2018 ESHRE / 2023 international guideline):
> - A. Clomiphene citrate
> - B. Letrozole
> - C. Gonadotropins
> - D. Metformin

**Answer: B. Letrozole**

Letrozole is an **aromatase inhibitor** — blocks conversion of androgens to oestrogen. This causes:
- Oestrogen ↓ → FSH ↑ via negative feedback → follicular stimulation
- No peripheral anti-oestrogenic effects (no endometrial thinning, no mucus thickening)
- Result: Better endometrial receptivity, higher live-birth rate in PCOS vs. clomiphene

The 2014 NEJM landmark trial (Legro et al.) confirmed letrozole superiority. Current guidelines (ESHRE 2018, WHO 2023): Letrozole is FIRST-LINE for anovulatory infertility in PCOS.

Note: Letrozole is technically **off-label** for ovulation induction in many countries (licensed for breast cancer) but widely used and guideline-recommended.

---

#### Clomiphene vs. Letrozole Comparison

| Feature | Clomiphene | Letrozole |
|---|---|---|
| Class | SERM | Aromatase inhibitor |
| Mechanism | Blocks oestrogen receptors → ↑ FSH | Blocks aromatase → ↓ oestrogen → ↑ FSH |
| Endometrial effect | THIN (anti-oestrogenic) | NORMAL (no anti-oestrogenic effect) |
| Cervical mucus | Thick (sperm hostile) | Normal |
| Multiple pregnancy risk | 5–10% | ~2–3% |
| Current guideline status | Second-line for PCOS | FIRST-LINE for PCOS |
| Ovulation induction rate | ~70% | ~70–80% |
| Live birth rate (PCOS) | ~19% | ~27% |

---

### 11. PCOS and Infertility

**PYQ 23 (Q696)**

> The cysts in PCOS are formed by:
> - A. Failure of atretic follicles to undergo apoptosis
> - B. Oocyte proliferation
> - C. Multiple corpora lutea
> - D. Cystic degeneration of the ovarian cortex

**Answer: A. Failure of atretic follicles to undergo apoptosis**

In PCOS: elevated androgens (from the theca cells, driven by excess LH and insulin resistance) inhibit follicular atresia. Multiple small follicles (2–9 mm) accumulate in a "pearl necklace" pattern at the ovarian periphery without one achieving dominance or ovulating. They are "arrested" antral follicles, not true cysts with epithelial linings.

**Imaging criteria for PCOS (Rotterdam 2003):** ≥12 follicles of 2–9 mm per ovary OR ovarian volume > 10 mL on TVS.

---

### 12. The IVF Process — Step by Step

Understanding the IVF sequence is essential for both process questions and complication questions.

#### IVF Protocol Steps

```
1. CONTROLLED OVARIAN STIMULATION (COS)
   - GnRH agonist (long protocol) or GnRH antagonist (short protocol)
   - Gonadotropins (FSH/LH) to stimulate multiple follicle growth
   - Monitoring: Serial TVS + serum estradiol every 1–2 days
   
2. TRIGGER SHOT
   - hCG 5000–10,000 IU when leading follicle ≥ 18 mm
   - Initiates final oocyte maturation
   - Or GnRH agonist trigger (in high OHSS-risk patients)
   
3. OOCYTE RETRIEVAL
   - 36 hours after trigger
   - Trans-vaginal ultrasound-guided needle aspiration
   - Follicular fluid collected → oocytes identified in lab
   
4. FERTILISATION
   - Conventional IVF: oocytes + sperm co-incubated
   - ICSI: Single sperm injected directly into oocyte
   
5. EMBRYO CULTURE
   - Day 3 (8-cell cleavage stage)
   - Day 5 (blastocyst stage — higher implantation rates)
   
6. PREIMPLANTATION GENETIC TESTING (PGT) — if indicated
   - Biopsy of blastomere (day 3) or trophectoderm (day 5)
   
7. EMBRYO TRANSFER
   - Fresh cycle (day 3 or day 5) OR
   - Frozen embryo transfer (FET)
```

**PYQ 24 (Q873) — 2016 actual paper**

> Test used to detect genetic abnormality in embryo before transferring it to the uterus in IVF is:
> - A. Embryo cell biopsy
> - B. CVS
> - C. ICSI
> - D. All of the above

**Answer: A. Embryo cell biopsy**

Preimplantation Genetic Testing (PGT) = biopsy of embryonic cells before transfer. Types:
- **PGT-A (aneuploidy)** — screens for chromosomal number abnormalities
- **PGT-M (monogenic)** — for single-gene disorders (CFTR, BRCA, etc.)
- **PGT-SR (structural rearrangements)** — for translocations

CVS (chorionic villus sampling) is an invasive prenatal diagnostic test AFTER implantation — not preimplantation. ICSI is a fertilisation technique, not a genetic test.

---

### 13. OHSS — Ovarian Hyperstimulation Syndrome

OHSS is the most serious iatrogenic complication of ART. High yield for both NEET-PG and INI-CET.

#### Pathophysiology

Exogenous hCG trigger → massive VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor) release from stimulated follicles → increased vascular permeability → fluid shifts from intravascular to third space (ascites, pleural effusion) → hypovolaemia + haemoconcentration → VTE risk.

#### Classification (Golan / RCOG)

| Grade | Features |
|---|---|
| Mild | Abdominal bloating, mild discomfort, ovaries 5–10 cm |
| Moderate | Nausea, vomiting, ascites on USG, ovaries 5–12 cm |
| Severe | Massive ascites, hydrothorax, Hct > 45%, oliguria, ovaries > 12 cm |
| Critical | Arterial thromboembolism, ARF, ARDS, torsion |

**Risk factors for OHSS:**
- PCOS (most important)
- Young age
- Low BMI
- Previous OHSS
- High AFC / AMH
- Large number of follicles recruited
- High serum estradiol (> 2500–3000 pg/mL at trigger)

---

**PYQ 25 (Q400)**

> A 29-year-old woman with PCOS undergoing IVF develops severe abdominal distension, nausea, shortness of breath 5 days after oocyte retrieval. Ultrasound shows bilateral enlarged ovaries (each > 10 cm) with massive ascites. Haematocrit 52%. This complication is:
> - A. Ruptured ovarian cyst
> - B. Severe ovarian hyperstimulation syndrome
> - C. Ovarian torsion
> - D. PID post-retrieval

**Answer: B. Severe OHSS**

Features of severe OHSS: ovaries > 10 cm, massive ascites, Hct > 45% (52% here = haemoconcentration), respiratory distress, oliguria. The PCOS background + IVF context + timing (5 days post-retrieval) = classic late-onset OHSS (triggered by endogenous hCG from early pregnancy if an embryo was transferred, or by exogenous trigger).

---

**PYQ 26 (Q336)**

> Patient on infertility treatment with gonadotropins develops abdominal pain, ascites, dyspnoea; USG shows enlarged multicystic ovaries. Diagnosis?
> - A. PCOS
> - B. OHSS
> - C. Mucinous cystadenoma
> - D. Theca lutein cysts

**Answer: B. OHSS**

The iatrogenic context (currently on gonadotropins for infertility treatment) + bilateral enlarged multicystic ovaries + ascites = OHSS. Theca lutein cysts are also bilateral and multicystic but occur in gestational trophoblastic disease context (very high hCG). Mucinous cystadenoma is typically unilateral and slowly growing.

---

**PYQ 27 (Q666)**

> A 25-year-old nulliparous woman undergoing controlled ovarian stimulation has 8 growing follicles and serum estradiol 800 pg/mL. Best management?
> - A. Retrieve all follicles immediately
> - B. Give cabergoline
> - C. Cancel the cycle
> - D. Withhold HCG trigger

**Answer: D. Withhold HCG trigger**

8 follicles growing = high OHSS risk. Withholding or switching the trigger is a risk-reduction strategy. Options include:
- **Withhold hCG** ("coasting" — continue stimulation but hold trigger until estradiol drops)
- **Switch to GnRH agonist trigger** (instead of hCG) — dramatically reduces OHSS risk
- **Freeze all embryos** (freeze-all strategy — prevents early pregnancy hCG from worsening OHSS)

Cabergoline (dopamine agonist) can reduce OHSS severity by blocking VEGF-mediated permeability, but it is not first-line here.

---

### 14. OHSS Prevention Strategy

| Strategy | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| GnRH antagonist protocol | Allows GnRH agonist trigger (not hCG) |
| GnRH agonist trigger (instead of hCG) | Short LH surge, less sustained stimulus |
| Freeze all embryos | No endogenous hCG from early pregnancy |
| Cabergoline | Reduces VEGF receptor signalling |
| Albumin infusion | Expands intravascular volume |
| Coasting | Hold gonadotropins until estradiol stabilises |

**Memory hook for OHSS classification: "M-M-S-C"**
Mild → Moderate → Severe → Critical

**Severe OHSS numbers to remember:**
- Ovaries > 10 cm
- Hct > 45%
- WBC > 25,000 (infection risk/haemoconcentration marker)

---

### 15. ICSI — When to Use

ICSI (Intracytoplasmic Sperm Injection) injects a single sperm directly into the oocyte cytoplasm. It bypasses:
- Sperm swimming/motility
- Zona pellucida penetration
- Acrosome reaction

**Indications for ICSI (over conventional IVF):**
- Severe oligospermia
- Asthenozoospermia (poor motility)
- Teratozoospermia
- Obstructive azoospermia (surgically retrieved sperm)
- Non-obstructive azoospermia (testicular sperm extraction)
- Previous failed fertilisation on conventional IVF
- Low oocyte numbers
- Frozen-thawed sperm

---

### 16. Infertility Workup — Initial Investigations

**PYQ 28 (Q542)**

> A 29-year-old couple presents with 3 years of infertility, regular cycles, normal coitus. What initial investigations are indicated?
> - A. Semen analysis, CXR, Mantoux
> - B. Semen analysis, tubal patency test, ovulation assessment
> - C. Ovulation assessment, tubal patency test, Mantoux
> - D. Testicular biopsy, scrotal USG, sperm penetration assay

**Answer: B. Semen analysis, tubal patency test, ovulation assessment**

The basic infertility workup triad:
1. **Semen analysis** — investigate male factor first (cheap, non-invasive)
2. **Ovulation documentation** — Day 21 progesterone/TVS
3. **Tubal patency** — HSG

Testicular biopsy and sperm penetration assay come only if initial semen analysis shows azoospermia or IVF failure. Mantoux/CXR would be indicated if genital TB is suspected (constitutional features, tubal disease pattern).

---

### 17. Uterine Causes of Infertility and Miscarriage

#### Submucosal Fibroid

**PYQ 29 (Q665)**

> 28-year-old with menorrhagia, primary infertility, and Hb 8 g/dL; hysteroscopy shows a round firm mass on a stalk in the uterine cavity. Diagnosis?
> - A. Pedunculated subserosal fibroid
> - B. Submucosal fibroid
> - C. Carcinoma endometrium
> - D. Gestational sac

**Answer: B. Submucosal fibroid**

Submucosal fibroids protrude into the uterine cavity → distort endometrium → cause menorrhagia + infertility + recurrent pregnancy loss. FIGO classification: Type 0 (entirely intracavitary, on a stalk), Type 1 (< 50% intramural), Type 2 (≥ 50% intramural). Types 0 and 1 are treated hysteroscopically.

---

#### Incompetent Cervix and Recurrent Miscarriage

**PYQ 30 (Q669)**

> A woman with two previous abortions; the Hegar dilator test shows size-8 dilator passes easily. Diagnosis?
> - A. Septate uterus
> - B. Unicornuate uterus
> - C. Uterus didelphys
> - D. Incompetent cervix

**Answer: D. Incompetent cervix**

Cervical insufficiency: painless dilatation of the cervix in the second trimester → pregnancy loss. Classical feature: Hegar dilator No. 8 passes without resistance outside pregnancy. Management in subsequent pregnancy: **McDonald cerclage** at 12–14 weeks gestation.

---

### 18. Tubal Anatomy and Function

#### Kartagener Syndrome and Tubal Motility

**PYQ 31 (Q535)**

> Decreased motility/ciliary dysfunction of the fallopian tube is associated with:
> - A. Noonan syndrome
> - B. PCOS
> - C. Churg-Strauss syndrome
> - D. Kartagener's syndrome

**Answer: D. Kartagener's syndrome**

Primary ciliary dyskinesia (PCD): absent dynein arms → immotile cilia. Features: bronchiectasis, recurrent sinusitis, situs inversus (50%), male infertility (immotile sperm), and female subfertility (impaired tubal egg transport). The egg cannot be transported toward the uterus without ciliary action.

---

### 19. Embryology Concepts Relevant to ART

#### Implantation Timing

**PYQ 32 (Q687/Q411/Q580)**

> After ovulation, embryo implantation occurs after approximately:
> - A. 3–5 days
> - B. 7–9 days
> - C. 10–12 days
> - D. 13–15 days

**Answer: B. 7–9 days**

Timeline:
- Day 0: Ovulation / fertilisation
- Day 3–4: 8-cell cleavage → morula → enters uterine cavity
- Day 4–5: Blastocyst formation (cavitation of morula)
- Day 5–6: Hatching from zona pellucida
- Day 7–9: Implantation into secretory endometrium

This question appears THREE TIMES in the database — the answer is consistently B (7–9 days).

---

#### Blastocyst Formation Timing

**PYQ 33 (Q548)**

> Blastocyst formation (cavitation of morula) occurs after fertilisation on approximately:
> - A. Day 4–7
> - B. Day 10–12
> - C. Day 12–15
> - D. Day 15–20

**Answer: A. Day 4–7**

Morula (Day 3–4) → blastocyst (Day 4–5 by cavitation) → hatching from zona pellucida → implantation Day 6–7. The uterine cavity is reached by Day 4 as a morula.

---

#### Secondary Oocyte — Meiotic Arrest

**PYQ 34 (Q1013) — 2024 actual paper**

> After ovulation, the egg is released from the ovary. At what stage of meiosis is the released egg arrested?
> - A. Secondary oocyte arrested in meiosis I
> - B. Secondary oocyte arrested in meiosis II
> - C. Primary oocyte arrested in meiosis I
> - D. Primary oocyte arrested in meiosis II

**Answer: B. Secondary oocyte arrested in meiosis II**

The story of meiotic arrests:
- **Primary oocyte**: arrested in Prophase of Meiosis I → from fetal life until puberty (sometimes 12–50 years)
- **At ovulation**: LH surge → meiosis resumes → first polar body extruded → becomes secondary oocyte → arrested in Metaphase II
- **After fertilisation**: sperm entry triggers completion of Meiosis II → second polar body extruded → female pronucleus formed

So at the moment of ovulation and release, the egg is a **secondary oocyte in Metaphase II arrest**.

---

## Bias Removal Section

### Common Traps in Infertility MCQs

**1. "Cornual block on HSG = IVF immediately"**
WRONG. Cornual block is often spasm. First step = laparoscopy + hysteroscopy to confirm. IVF is the answer only if true blockage is confirmed.

**2. "Hydrosalpinx: proceed directly to IVF"**
Partially right, but INCOMPLETE. Hydrosalpinx MUST be dealt with (salpingectomy or proximal occlusion) BEFORE IVF, otherwise IVF success rates are halved.

**3. "FSH elevated = ovarian failure"**
Correct direction, but context matters. Elevated FSH is also seen in Klinefelter syndrome (in males). In females < 40 years: POI.

**4. "Inhibin A is an ovarian reserve marker"**
WRONG. Inhibin B is the reserve marker. Inhibin A is placental.

**5. "Sterility after vasectomy is immediate"**
WRONG (also tested in Chapter 5). Azoospermia confirmed ~12 weeks later.

**6. "WHO 2010 values are current"**
WRONG. WHO 2021 (6th edition) is current. Key change: concentration is 16 million/mL (was 15 million). Volume is 1.4 mL (was 1.5 mL).

---

## Variations and Twists

### Scenario: Normal HSG but Still Infertile

If semen analysis is normal, HSG is normal, and ovulation is confirmed → **unexplained infertility** OR consider:
- Mild endometriosis (not visible on HSG)
- Sperm-cervical mucus incompatibility
- Immunological factors
- Luteinised unruptured follicle (LUF) syndrome
- Poor oocyte quality

Next step: **Diagnostic laparoscopy** — can find endometriosis invisible on HSG.

### Scenario: Male with Bilateral Absence of Vas Deferens

CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of vas deferens): associated with **cystic fibrosis** (CFTR gene mutation). Semen analysis shows azoospermia + very low volume + acidic semen (seminal vesicle also absent). Treatment: testicular sperm extraction + ICSI + genetic counselling.

### Scenario: Woman with Elevated Prolactin + Infertility

Hyperprolactinaemia → suppresses GnRH → anovulation → infertility. Treatment: **cabergoline** (dopamine agonist) → normalises prolactin → restores ovulation. First exclude pituitary macroadenoma (MRI).

---

## Memory Engineering

### WHO 2021 Semen Analysis — Memory Hook: "16-42-30-4-54"

**"16 men, 42 total, 30 progressive, 4 shape, 54 alive"**
- Concentration: **16** million/mL
- Total motility: **42%**
- Progressive motility: **30%**
- Morphology: **4%** (strict Kruger)
- Vitality: **54%**
- Volume: **1.4** mL

### OHSS Severity — Memory Hook: "5-10-45"
- Mild/moderate: ovaries 5–10 cm
- Severe: ovaries **> 10 cm**, Hct **> 45%**

### Semen Terminology — Memory Hook: "AZOAT"
- **A**spermia = absent ejaculate
- **Az**oospermia = absent sperm (but ejaculate present)
- **O**ligospermia = low count
- **A**sthenospermia = poor motility
- **T**eratospermia = abnormal morphology

### IVF Indications Mnemonic: "TUBES MALE UNEXPLAINED"
- **T** — Tubal disease (bilateral block, hydrosalpinx)
- **U** — Unexplained infertility (failed other treatments)
- **B** — Both partners contributing (mixed factor)
- **E** — Endometriosis (severe, surgery failed)
- **S** — Semen abnormality (severe OAT)
- **MALE** — Male factor (azoospermia requiring ICSI)

---

## Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### Semen Analysis Normal Values (WHO 2021) — Direct Recall

| Parameter | Normal Lower Limit |
|---|---|
| Volume | ≥ 1.4 mL |
| Concentration | ≥ 16 million/mL |
| Total motility | ≥ 42% |
| Progressive motility | ≥ 30% |
| Morphology (strict) | ≥ 4% |
| Vitality | ≥ 54% |

### Investigation Sequence Summary

| Suspected cause | First-line investigation |
|---|---|
| Male factor | Semen analysis |
| Anovulation | Day 21 progesterone (cycle day 21 in 28-day cycle) |
| Tubal factor (history of PID/TB) | HSG (days 6–12) |
| Cornual block on HSG | Laparoscopy + hysteroscopy |
| Uterine adhesions (post-curettage) | Hysteroscopy |
| Ovarian reserve | AMH |
| PCOS | TVS + LH:FSH ratio + androgen levels |

### Top 5 Traps in Infertility/ART MCQs

1. **Cornual block on HSG → lap + hysteroscopy first** (not straight to IVF)
2. **Aspermia (no semen) ≠ azoospermia (no sperm in semen)** — definitions swapped in distractors
3. **Inhibin A ≠ ovarian reserve** — Inhibin B is the reserve marker
4. **Hydrosalpinx must be treated before IVF** — toxic to embryo
5. **Letrozole is now first-line, not clomiphene** in PCOS infertility

---

## All Chapter 6 PYQs Integrated

### Q-Bank Practice Block

---

### Q1 — WHO 2021 sperm concentration

WHO 2021 lower reference limit for sperm concentration:

- A. 5 million/mL
- B. 10 million/mL
- C. 16 million/mL
- D. 20 million/mL

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. 16 million/mL** — WHO 6th edition: concentration ≥ 16 M/mL, total motility ≥ 42%, progressive ≥ 30%, morphology ≥ 4% (strict), volume ≥ 1.4 mL.

</details>

---

### Q2 — Best timing for HSG

Best timing for HSG in the menstrual cycle:

- A. Day 2–5
- B. Day 6–12
- C. Day 14–16
- D. Day 21–28

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Day 6–12** — Post-menses, pre-ovulatory. Assesses tubal patency and uterine cavity without risk of pregnancy exposure.

</details>

---

### Q3 — First-line ovulation induction in PCOS

First-line ovulation induction agent in PCOS infertility:

- A. Clomiphene citrate
- B. Letrozole
- C. Gonadotropins
- D. Metformin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Letrozole** — Aromatase inhibitor; higher live-birth rate than clomiphene in PCOS (ESHRE 2018, WHO 2023 guidelines).

</details>

---

### Q4 — IVF: severe OHSS

29-year-old PCOS woman, 5 days after oocyte retrieval: severe abdominal distension, nausea, shortness of breath. USG: ovaries > 10 cm, massive ascites. Hct 52%. Diagnosis:

- A. Ruptured ovarian cyst
- B. Severe OHSS
- C. Ovarian torsion
- D. PID post-retrieval

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Severe OHSS** — Ovaries > 10 cm, massive ascites, haemoconcentration (Hct > 45%), triggered by hCG in PCOS patient after oocyte retrieval.

</details>

---

### Q5 — Cornual tubal block: management

A woman with infertility and bilateral tubal block at the cornua on HSG. Best management:

- A. Laparoscopy and hysteroscopy
- B. Hydrotubation
- C. IVF
- D. Tuboplasty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Laparoscopy and hysteroscopy** — Cornual block is often a spasm artefact; confirms true occlusion + treats simultaneously. IVF reserved for confirmed true obstruction.

</details>

---

### Q6 — Aspermia definition

Aspermia refers to:

- A. Complete absence of semen (ejaculate)
- B. Absence of sperm in the ejaculate (azoospermia)
- C. Absence of sperm motility (asthenozoospermia)
- D. Occurrence of abnormal sperm morphology (teratozoospermia)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Complete absence of semen** — Aspermia = no ejaculate. Azoospermia = ejaculate present but no sperm. Classic MCQ distinction.

</details>

---

### Q7 — Bilateral cornual block management (2016)

An infertile woman has bilateral tubal block at the cornua on HSG. Next treatment of choice:

- A. IVF
- B. Laparoscopy and hysteroscopy
- C. Tuboplasty
- D. Hydrotubation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Laparoscopy and hysteroscopy** — HSG cornual block is often spasm; laparoscopy + chromopertubation confirms and hysteroscopy treats.

</details>

---

### Q8 — Male azoospermia, normal testes and FSH

25-year-old man with azoospermia, normal-sized testes, normal FSH and testosterone. Most likely cause:

- A. Obstructed vas deferens
- B. Kallmann syndrome
- C. Klinefelter syndrome
- D. Noonan syndrome

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Obstructed vas deferens** — Normal FSH + normal testes = spermatogenesis intact; azoospermia due to obstruction. Treatment: surgical sperm retrieval + ICSI.

</details>

---

### Q9 — Asherman's syndrome diagnosis

Woman with secondary amenorrhoea following curettage for abortion. FSH is 7 IU/L. Most likely diagnosis:

- A. Pituitary failure
- B. Ovarian failure
- C. Uterine synechiae (Asherman's syndrome)
- D. Pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Uterine synechiae (Asherman's syndrome)** — Normal FSH excludes pituitary/ovarian failure. Post-curettage + secondary amenorrhoea = Asherman's. Diagnosis: hysteroscopy. Treatment: adhesiolysis + oestrogen.

</details>

---

### Q10 — Hydrosalpinx on HSG

HSG of a 26-year-old infertile woman shows blocked fallopian tubes with fluid-filled dilatation at the fimbrial end. Diagnosis:

- A. Hydrosalpinx
- B. Retroverted fixed uterus
- C. Uterine fibroids
- D. Cervical fibroid

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Hydrosalpinx** — Distal tubal occlusion + serous fluid accumulation. HSG: clubbed blind-ending tubes. Reduces IVF success by ~50%; salpingectomy/proximal occlusion before IVF is recommended.

</details>

---

### Q11 — Hydrosalpinx: cogwheel appearance (2024)

29-year-old woman, infertility + chronic pelvic pain. TVS: adnexal mass with incomplete septations and cogwheel appearance. Most likely diagnosis:

- A. Hydrosalpinx
- B. Follicular ovarian cyst
- C. Endometriosis
- D. Haemorrhagic ovarian cyst

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Hydrosalpinx** — Cogwheel sign on TVS = cross-sectional view of fallopian tube wall folds; pathognomonic of hydrosalpinx.

</details>

---

### Q12 — Infertility workup: initial investigations

29-year-old couple, 3 years infertility, regular cycles, normal coitus. Initial investigations:

- A. Semen analysis, CXR, Mantoux
- B. Semen analysis, tubal patency test, ovulation assessment
- C. Ovulation assessment, tubal patency test, Mantoux
- D. Testicular biopsy, scrotal USG, sperm penetration assay

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Semen analysis, tubal patency test, ovulation assessment** — Basic triad. Testicular biopsy is invasive and comes later.

</details>

---

### Q13 — Severe endometriosis + infertility management

29-year-old nulliparous with severe endometriosis: laparoscopy shows severe adhesions, kissing ovaries (6–8 cm), bowel involvement. Best management:

- A. GnRH analogues
- B. Combined OCP
- C. Hysterectomy with bilateral oophorectomy
- D. Bilateral cystectomy, adhesiolysis, prepare for IVF

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Bilateral cystectomy, adhesiolysis, and prepare for IVF** — Conservative surgery first; IVF for residual infertility. GnRH and OCP manage symptoms but do not restore fertility.

</details>

---

### Q14 — Genital TB: most common site

Most common site of female genital TB:

- A. Fallopian tubes
- B. Endometrium
- C. Ovaries
- D. Vulva/vagina

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Fallopian tubes (~100%)** — Always involved. Endometrium 60–80%, ovaries 20–30%. Common cause of tubal infertility in India.

</details>

---

### Q15 — HSG findings in genital TB (2016)

HSG findings suggestive of genital TB:

- A. Beaded tubes
- B. Honeycomb uterus
- C. Golf-club tube
- D. All of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. All of the above** — All three are pathognomonic HSG features of genital tuberculosis.

</details>

---

### Q16 — Genital TB: clinical presentation

32-year-old with 6-month secondary amenorrhoea, chronic pelvic pain, intermittent fever, weight loss, adnexal mass. Most likely diagnosis:

- A. PCOS
- B. Endometriosis
- C. Genital tuberculosis
- D. Ectopic pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Genital tuberculosis** — Constitutional features + secondary amenorrhoea + adnexal mass in endemic region. Endometrial biopsy (culture, PCR, histology) is the diagnostic gold standard.

</details>

---

### Q17 — PID and infertility investigation

22-year-old with history of PID, 12 months infertility, normal examination. Next investigation:

- A. HSG
- B. AMH
- C. Hysteroscopy
- D. CA-125

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. HSG** — Post-PID infertility = first assess tubal patency. HSG detects tubal blockage or hydrosalpinx.

</details>

---

### Q18 — Ovarian reserve markers — NOT

All are markers of ovarian reserve EXCEPT:

- A. Inhibin A
- B. Estradiol (day 3)
- C. Inhibin B
- D. Antral follicle count / ovarian volume

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Inhibin A** — Inhibin A is mainly placental; inhibin B is the granulosa-cell reserve marker.

</details>

---

### Q19 — Day 21 progesterone

Best day for serum progesterone evaluation in a 28-day cycle to confirm ovulation:

- A. Day 3
- B. Day 8
- C. Day 14
- D. Day 21

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Day 21** — Mid-luteal (7 days before next period). Level > 5 ng/mL confirms ovulation.

</details>

---

### Q20 — PCOS: cyst formation mechanism

The cysts in PCOS are formed by:

- A. Failure of atretic follicles to undergo apoptosis
- B. Oocyte proliferation
- C. Multiple corpora lutea
- D. Cystic degeneration of the ovarian cortex

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Failure of atretic follicles to undergo apoptosis** — Elevated androgens inhibit follicular atresia; multiple small follicles persist in peripheral necklace pattern.

</details>

---

### Q21 — Ovulation-to-implantation interval

After ovulation, embryo implantation occurs after approximately:

- A. 3–5 days
- B. 7–9 days
- C. 10–12 days
- D. 13–15 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 7–9 days** — Blastocyst hatches from zona (day 5–6) and implants into secretory endometrium day 7–9 post-ovulation.

</details>

---

### Q22 — Secondary oocyte arrested in (2024)

After ovulation, the egg released is arrested at:

- A. Secondary oocyte arrested in meiosis I
- B. Secondary oocyte arrested in meiosis II
- C. Primary oocyte arrested in meiosis I
- D. Primary oocyte arrested in meiosis II

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Secondary oocyte arrested in meiosis II** — LH surge triggers completion of meiosis I (first polar body extruded) → secondary oocyte formed → arrested in metaphase II until fertilisation.

</details>

---

### Q23 — Preimplantation genetic testing (2016)

Test used to detect genetic abnormality in embryo before transferring to uterus in IVF:

- A. Embryo cell biopsy
- B. CVS
- C. ICSI
- D. All of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Embryo cell biopsy** — Preimplantation genetic testing (PGT) = trophectoderm biopsy (day 5) or blastomere biopsy (day 3). CVS is postnatal; ICSI is a fertilisation technique.

</details>

---

### Q24 — OHSS: HCG withholding

25-year-old undergoing COS with 8 growing follicles and estradiol 800 pg/mL. Best management:

- A. Retrieve all follicles immediately
- B. Give cabergoline
- C. Cancel the cycle
- D. Withhold HCG trigger

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Withhold HCG trigger** — 8 follicles = high OHSS risk; withholding hCG (or switching to GnRH agonist trigger) prevents severe OHSS.

</details>

---

### Q25 — Endometriosis: long-term sequela

23-year-old with dyspareunia, retroverted fixed uterus, adnexal + cervical motion tenderness. Most important long-term sequela:

- A. Recurrent pregnancy loss
- B. Infertility
- C. Pelvic organ prolapse
- D. Amenorrhoea

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Infertility** — Endometriosis causes infertility through adhesions, peritoneal toxins, and endometriomas. ~30–50% of infertile women have endometriosis.

</details>

---

### Q26 — Endometriosis + infertility on exam (2016)

26-year-old obese infertile female: fixed retroverted uterus, nodular uterosacral ligaments. Most likely diagnosis:

- A. PCOS
- B. Endometriosis
- C. Adenomyosis
- D. TB

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Endometriosis** — Fixed retroverted uterus + nodular uterosacral ligaments = pathognomonic. Pouch of Douglas obliterated by adhesions.
