</details>

---

### Q27 — Hysteroscopy for Asherman (2023)

For which procedure can hysteroscopy preferably be done?

- A. Tubal ligation
- B. Asherman syndrome
- C. Endocervical polyp
- D. Subserosal fibroid

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Asherman syndrome** — Hysteroscopic adhesiolysis + oestrogen. Tubal ligation = laparoscopy; subserosal fibroid = laparoscopic myomectomy.

</details>

---

### Q28 — Decreased tubal motility syndrome

Decreased motility/ciliary dysfunction of the fallopian tube is associated with:

- A. Noonan syndrome
- B. PCOS
- C. Churg-Strauss syndrome
- D. Kartagener's syndrome

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Kartagener's syndrome** — Primary ciliary dyskinesia; absent dynein arms; immotile cilia → bronchiectasis, situs inversus, male + female subfertility.

</details>

---

### Q29 — Submucosal fibroid on hysteroscopy

28-year-old with menorrhagia, primary infertility, Hb 8; hysteroscopy shows round firm mass on stalk in uterine cavity. Diagnosis:

- A. Pedunculated subserosal fibroid
- B. Submucosal fibroid
- C. Carcinoma endometrium
- D. Gestational sac

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Submucosal fibroid** — Protrudes into uterine cavity; causes menorrhagia + infertility. FIGO types 0 and 1 treated hysteroscopically.

</details>

---

### Q30 — Blastocyst formation timing

Blastocyst formation occurs after fertilisation on approximately:

- A. Day 4–7
- B. Day 10–12
- C. Day 12–15
- D. Day 15–20

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Day 4–7** — Morula (Day 3–4) → blastocyst by cavitation (Day 4–5) → hatching → implantation Day 6–7.

</details>

---

### Q31 — AUB-O in obese anovulatory woman

35-year-old obese (BMI 34) with irregular heavy bleeding, simple endometrial hyperplasia without atypia, luteal progesterone < 3 ng/mL. AUB mechanism:

- A. AUB-L (leiomyoma)
- B. AUB-O (anovulatory cycles with unopposed oestrogen)
- C. AUB-C (coagulopathy)
- D. AUB-P (endometrial polyp)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. AUB-O** — Obesity → peripheral aromatisation → unopposed oestrogen → anovulatory bleeding + endometrial hyperplasia. Low luteal progesterone confirms anovulation.

</details>

---

### Q32 — Premature ovarian failure: hormone profile

Which hormone is elevated in premature ovarian failure?

- A. Serum inhibin
- B. Serum FSH
- C. Serum estradiol
- D. Both A and B

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Serum FSH** — POI: FSH > 25–40 IU/L on two occasions; low estradiol, low AMH, low inhibin B.

</details>

---

# Chapter 7 — AUB, Menstrual Disorders & Endocrine Gynaecology

> "The uterus does not bleed in a vacuum — every abnormal bleed has a cause. Your job is to find the cause before prescribing a drug."

---

## 🎯 Big Picture First

Abnormal uterine bleeding (AUB) is one of the top three reasons women present to a gynaecology clinic. For NEET-PG and INI-CET, the examiner is testing one fundamental concept repeatedly: **before you label it DUB, you must rule out everything structural.** DUB (now subsumed under the PALM-COEIN framework) is a diagnosis of exclusion.

The 2011 FIGO classification — PALM-COEIN — organised AUB into structural causes (PALM: Polyp, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma, Malignancy/hyperplasia) and non-structural causes (COEIN: Coagulopathy, Ovulatory, Endometrial, Iatrogenic, Not yet classified). Understanding where DUB sits in this framework is the single most important conceptual anchor for this chapter.

Equally central to this chapter is the endocrine axis governing menstruation — the HPO axis. When it misfires at any level (hypothalamus, pituitary, ovary, or outflow tract), bleeding and amenorrhoea result. The chapter takes you from the top of the axis to the uterine cavity, hitting every examiner favourite along the way.

---

## 📊 Question Pattern Map

From past NEET-PG papers, AUB and endocrine gynaecology questions cluster around these themes:

| Tested Concept | What the Examiner Wants |
|---|---|
| DUB definition | That it does NOT occur postmenopausally |
| Metropathia haemorrhagica | Amenorrhoea → heavy bleed; anovulatory mechanism |
| PALM-COEIN | Classification recognition; which category |
| Primary amenorrhoea | Most common cause = ovarian dysgenesis (Turner) |
| Secondary amenorrhoea | First investigation after pregnancy test = prolactin |
| Sheehan's syndrome | PPH → pituitary necrosis → panhypopituitarism |
| AIS (testicular feminisation) | 46,XY + female phenotype + absent pubic hair |
| MRKH | 46,XX + absent uterus + normal ovaries |
| Imperforate hymen | Bluish bulge at introitus; cyclical pain with no menarche |
| Granulosa cell tumour | Oestrogen-secreting → menorrhagia |
| Menorrhagia management | Perimenopausal → D&C first to exclude pathology |

---

## 📚 Core Concept Deep Dive

### 1. Normal Menstrual Parameters — The Baseline You Must Know

Before diagnosing any disorder, you need the normal range burned into memory.

| Parameter | Normal Range | Abnormal Label |
|---|---|---|
| Cycle length | 21–35 days | Polymenorrhoea (<21d); Oligomenorrhoea (>35d) |
| Duration of flow | 3–7 days | Prolonged (>7d) |
| Blood loss | 20–80 mL per cycle | Menorrhagia (>80 mL) |
| Pain | Minimal dysmenorrhoea acceptable | Dysmenorrhoea if disabling |

**Memory peg:** "21–35 / 3–7 / 20–80" — three number pairs. Anything outside is abnormal.

Menorrhagia is objectively >80 mL but is clinically assessed by passage of clots >1 cm, soaking more than one pad per hour, or iron-deficiency anaemia.

---

### 2. PALM-COEIN Classification (FIGO 2011) — The Examiner's Favourite

The classification organises AUB causes into two groups:

**PALM = Structural** (visible on imaging or histology)

| Letter | Cause | Key Feature |
|---|---|---|
| P | Polyp | Endometrial or cervical; premenopausal most common benign structural cause |
| A | Adenomyosis | Uterus uniformly enlarged; dysmenorrhoea + menorrhagia |
| L | Leiomyoma | Fibroids; submucosal fibroids most responsible for AUB |
| M | Malignancy/Hyperplasia | Must always exclude in perimenopausal/postmenopausal |

**COEIN = Non-structural** (no imaging abnormality)

| Letter | Cause | Key Feature |
|---|---|---|
| C | Coagulopathy | Von Willebrand disease commonest; always ask in teenagers |
| O | Ovulatory dysfunction | Classic DUB — anovulation; includes PCOS, thyroid, hyperprolactinaemia |
| E | Endometrial | Local endometrial defect — primary (no systemic cause found) |
| I | Iatrogenic | Anticoagulants, copper IUD, progestogen-only methods |
| N | Not classified | Arteriovenous malformation, myometrial hypertrophy |

**Memory Technique: "PALM trees COIN the diagnosis"**
- PALM = structural (you can see it)
- COIN = non-structural (you can't see it)

---

### 3. DUB — The Diagnosis of Exclusion

DUB (dysfunctional uterine bleeding) = AUB with no structural, systemic, or iatrogenic cause. It is now classified under AUB-O (ovulatory dysfunction) in FIGO terminology but the old term appears extensively in exam questions.

**Two critical rules for the exam:**
1. DUB is a **diagnosis of exclusion** — only after ruling out PALM causes.
2. DUB does **NOT** occur in postmenopausal women. Postmenopausal bleeding = cancer until proven otherwise.

> **MCQ Alert — This exact question appears multiple times in NEET-PG:**

---

**Q415 / Q584 — DUB: Postmenopausal Exclusion**

Dysfunctional uterine bleeding (DUB) is commonly seen in all EXCEPT:

- A. Adolescence
- B. Following childbirth
- C. Premenopausal period
- D. Postmenopausal period

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Postmenopausal period** — DUB is anovulatory by definition and does not occur after menopause; postmenopausal bleeding always requires investigation to exclude endometrial carcinoma.

</details>

**Why this keeps appearing:** The trap is that "following childbirth" sounds pathological — but the newly postpartum uterus is oestrogen-dominant, anovulatory, and can bleed dysfunctionally during the transition back to cycles. This is legitimate DUB. Postmenopausal bleeding is never DUB.

---

**Q836 — Perimenopausal Menorrhagia: First Step**

A 46-year-old P3L3 complains of menorrhagia for 3 months. Next line of management?

- A. D&C
- B. Progesterone x 6 months
- C. OC pills x 6 months
- D. Hysterectomy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. D&C** — In a perimenopausal woman with menorrhagia, D&C (dilatation and curettage) is performed first to exclude endometrial pathology (hyperplasia/carcinoma) before any medical therapy is started.

</details>

**Examiner's logic:** Age 46 is perimenopausal. The risk of endometrial hyperplasia and carcinoma is significant. You cannot prescribe progestogens or OCPs until malignancy is excluded. D&C provides both diagnosis and temporary treatment. Note: In modern practice, pipelle endometrial biopsy + hysteroscopy is increasingly preferred, but D&C remains the classic exam answer for this scenario.

---

### 4. Metropathia Haemorrhagica — The Classic Anovulatory Bleed

This is a specific clinical pattern that examiners love because it encapsulates the entire physiology of anovulation.

**Pathophysiology chain:**
1. Anovulatory cycle → no corpus luteum → no progesterone
2. Prolonged unopposed oestrogen → continuous endometrial proliferation
3. Endometrium becomes thickened, cystically hyperplastic ("Swiss cheese" pattern on histology)
4. Oestrogen level eventually falls (follicle atresia)
5. No organised shedding → irregular, heavy, prolonged bleeding after a period of amenorrhoea

**Classic presentation:** A premenopausal or perimenopausal woman with a period of weeks of amenorrhoea followed by sudden, profuse, irregular bleeding. This is the hallmark.

**Histology:** Cystic glandular hyperplasia (dilated glands in a sea of stroma — "Swiss cheese endometrium"). This is benign but distinguishable from complex atypical hyperplasia (which is premalignant).

---

**Q420 / Q590 — Metropathia Haemorrhagica (appears twice)**

Period of amenorrhoea followed by massive bleeding in a premenopausal woman is seen in:

- A. Irregular ripening
- B. Irregular shedding
- C. Metropathia haemorrhagica
- D. All of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Metropathia haemorrhagica** — Prolonged oestrogenic stimulation without progesterone (anovulation) leads to cystic glandular hyperplasia; when the oestrogen level falls, the thickened hyperplastic endometrium sheds irregularly causing heavy breakthrough bleeding after amenorrhoea.

</details>

**Option analysis:**
- Irregular ripening: defective corpus luteum → short luteal phase → shortened cycles with light bleeding. No preceding amenorrhoea.
- Irregular shedding: prolonged corpus luteum activity → bleeding continues days after expected cessation. The pattern is prolonged bleeding, not amenorrhoea then flood.
- Metropathia: the only pattern with the signature amenorrhoea-then-flood sequence.

---

### 5. AUB: Structural Cause on Hysteroscopy

**Q899 — AUB on Hysteroscopy**

In a woman complaining of AUB, the following image was seen on endoscopic examination of the uterus. Diagnosis?

- A. Leiomyoma
- B. Adenomyosis
- C. Ovarian neoplasm
- D. Carcinoma of uterus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Leiomyoma** — Hysteroscopy in AUB revealing a smooth, rounded, well-circumscribed intracavitary or submucosal mass is diagnostic of a leiomyoma (fibroid); submucosal fibroids are the most common structural cause of AUB identified on hysteroscopy.

</details>

**Why adenomyosis is wrong:** Adenomyosis is a myometrial disease — it cannot be seen directly at hysteroscopy (only irregular openings on endometrial surface, not a discrete mass). It is diagnosed by MRI or transvaginal ultrasound.

---

### 6. Ovarian Tumour Causing Menorrhagia

**Q824 — Granulosa Cell Tumour**

Which ovarian tumour can present with menorrhagia?

- A. Dermoid cyst
- B. Epithelial ovarian cancer
- C. Granulosa cell tumour
- D. Yolk sac tumour

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Granulosa cell tumour** — Granulosa cell tumour is an oestrogen-secreting sex-cord stromal tumour; excess oestrogen stimulates the endometrium causing menorrhagia in premenopausal women or postmenopausal bleeding in older women. The classic associated marker is inhibin B.

</details>

**Complete picture:** Granulosa cell tumours also cause endometrial hyperplasia and even endometrial carcinoma due to chronic unopposed oestrogen. In prepubertal girls, they cause isosexual precocious puberty. Call sign = inhibin B elevated on tumour marker testing.

---

### 7. Amenorrhoea — Framework and Investigation

**Definition:**
- **Primary amenorrhoea:** No menstruation by age 14 with absent secondary sexual characteristics, OR by age 16 regardless of secondary sexual characteristics
- **Secondary amenorrhoea:** Absence of periods for ≥3 months in a woman who previously had normal cycles (or ≥6 months if cycles were irregular)

**Investigation ladder:**
1. Pregnancy test (always first — never skip this)
2. Serum prolactin (hyperprolactinaemia = commonest correctable cause)
3. TSH (hypothyroidism raises TRH → elevates prolactin)
4. FSH/LH (to determine if hypo- or hypergonadotrophic)
5. Pelvic ultrasound (uterus, ovarian morphology)
6. Karyotype (if primary amenorrhoea with absent secondary characteristics)

**Hypergonadotrophic hypogonadism (high FSH, high LH):**
- Ovarian failure: Turner syndrome, premature ovarian failure (POF), radiation
- Streak gonads present in Turner

**Hypogonadotrophic hypogonadism (low FSH, low LH):**
- Hypothalamic: weight loss, exercise, stress, Kallmann syndrome
- Pituitary: Sheehan's syndrome, prolactinoma, haemochromatosis

---

**Q480 — Primary Amenorrhoea Most Common Cause**

Most common cause of primary amenorrhoea is:

- A. Constitutional/idiopathic
- B. MRKH syndrome
- C. Ovarian dysgenesis
- D. None of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Ovarian dysgenesis (Turner syndrome)** — Ovarian dysgenesis (45,X Turner syndrome) is the most common single cause of primary amenorrhoea, accounting for ~30–40% of cases.

</details>

---

**Q649 — Secondary Amenorrhoea: Initial Investigation**

A 32-year-old with 6-month secondary amenorrhoea and a history of hypothyroidism; pregnancy test negative. Initial diagnostic test?

- A. Serum prolactin
- B. Serum FSH and LH
- C. Pelvic ultrasound
- D. Serum estradiol

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Serum prolactin** — Hypothyroidism raises TRH, which stimulates prolactin; hyperprolactinaemia inhibits GnRH → secondary amenorrhoea. Prolactin is the first-line test after excluding pregnancy.

</details>

**The clinical logic:** The history of hypothyroidism is a deliberate clue — it points you toward the TRH-prolactin-GnRH axis. But even without that clue, prolactin is the answer because hyperprolactinaemia is the commonest correctable cause of secondary amenorrhoea overall.

---

### 8. Turner Syndrome (45,X0) — The High-Yield Triad

**Clinical triad:** Short stature + primary amenorrhoea + webbed neck

**Additional features:** Shield chest, widely spaced nipples, cubitus valgus, horseshoe kidney, bicuspid aortic valve (cardiovascular complication), streak ovaries

**Hormonal profile:** High FSH, high LH (hypergonadotrophic hypogonadism) — the ovaries are streaks, producing no oestrogen, so no negative feedback

---

**Q391 — Turner Syndrome Investigation**

A 16-year-old girl presents with primary amenorrhoea, short stature, and webbed neck. Karyotype is 45,X0. Which investigation would show streak gonads on imaging?

- A. MRI of pituitary
- B. Pelvic ultrasound
- C. Laparoscopy
- D. CT scan of abdomen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Pelvic ultrasound** — Pelvic ultrasound in Turner syndrome typically shows streak ovaries (fibrous bands replacing gonads), an infantile uterus, and absent follicles; serum FSH and LH are markedly elevated (hypergonadotrophic hypogonadism). Laparoscopy is definitive but ultrasound is first-line imaging.

</details>

---

### 9. MRKH Syndrome vs AIS vs Turner — The Three-Way Differential

This is a classic NEET-PG trap question scenario. Three conditions present with primary amenorrhoea — but the clues distinguish them clearly.

| Feature | Turner (45,X0) | MRKH (46,XX) | AIS — CAIS (46,XY) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Karyotype | 45,X0 | 46,XX | 46,XY |
| Secondary sexual characteristics | Absent/poor | Normal female | Advanced (Tanner V breast) |
| Pubic/axillary hair | Reduced | Normal | Absent/sparse |
| Uterus | Absent (streak) | Absent (Müllerian aplasia) | Absent |
| Ovaries | Streak gonads | Normal ovaries | Testes (inguinal/intra-abdominal) |
| FSH/LH | High (hypergonadotrophic) | Normal | High after gonadectomy |
| Short stature | Yes | No | No |

---

**Q392 — MRKH Syndrome**

A 17-year-old presents with primary amenorrhoea, normal secondary sexual characteristics, and a blind vaginal pouch of 2 cm. Ultrasound shows absent uterus with bilateral normal ovaries. Karyotype is 46,XX. This is:

- A. Complete androgen insensitivity syndrome (CAIS)
- B. Mayer-Rokitansky-Küster-Hauser (MRKH) syndrome
- C. Swyer syndrome (46,XY pure gonadal dysgenesis)
- D. Imperforate hymen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Mayer-Rokitansky-Küster-Hauser (MRKH) syndrome** — MRKH: absent uterus + upper vagina (Müllerian aplasia), 46,XX karyotype, normal ovaries, normal female secondary sexual characteristics. Distinguished from CAIS by karyotype (46,XX vs 46,XY) and from imperforate hymen by absent uterus.

</details>

---

**Q393 — AIS: Long-term Management**

A phenotypically female 18-year-old presents with primary amenorrhoea and absence of pubic/axillary hair. Karyotype is 46,XY. Testes are found in the inguinal canals. Most important long-term management concern?

- A. Cyclical oestrogen-progestogen HRT to induce menstruation
- B. Gonadectomy to prevent malignant transformation of intra-abdominal/inguinal testes
- C. Testosterone supplementation to masculinise
- D. Clomiphene to stimulate gonadotrophin release

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Gonadectomy to prevent malignant transformation of intra-abdominal/inguinal testes** — In complete AIS (46,XY), the testes have a 2–5% lifetime risk of malignant transformation (gonadoblastoma, dysgerminoma); gonadectomy is recommended after puberty (allowing puberty to complete with endogenous oestrogen from testosterone aromatisation), followed by oestrogen-only HRT.

</details>

**Critical details:**
- Gonadectomy is done AFTER puberty completes — the endogenous oestrogen from aromatisation of testosterone in the testes drives feminisation (breast development, feminine habitus)
- Post-gonadectomy: oestrogen-only HRT (no uterus present, so no progestogen needed)
- Option A is wrong: there is no uterus to menstruate
- Option C is the opposite of desired management

---

**Q582 — Testicular Feminisation Syndrome**

A girl with primary amenorrhoea, advanced breast development (Tanner V), minimal pubic hair (Tanner II), and absent axillary hair. Most likely diagnosis:

- A. Testicular feminisation syndrome (AIS)
- B. Müllerian agenesis (MRKH)
- C. Turner syndrome
- D. Gonadal dysgenesis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Testicular feminisation syndrome (AIS)** — Complete AIS: 46,XY karyotype, normal female habitus with excellent breast development (Tanner V), absent/sparse pubic and axillary hair (the pathognomonic sign), blind vagina, no uterus.

</details>

**The pathognomonic clue:** Absent/sparse pubic and axillary hair in a well-feminised girl = AIS until proven otherwise. The hair follicles are androgen-dependent; without androgen receptors, pubic/axillary hair does not grow despite normal androgen levels.

---

### 10. Imperforate Hymen — Cryptomenorrhoea

**Q513 / Q1001 — Imperforate Hymen**

A 13-year-old girl presents with cyclical lower abdominal pain for 6 months and primary amenorrhoea. Examination reveals a tense bluish bulge at the introitus. Diagnosis?

- A. MRKH syndrome
- B. Imperforate hymen
- C. Asherman's syndrome
- D. Congenital adrenal hyperplasia

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Imperforate hymen** — Cryptomenorrhoea: haematocolpos behind an imperforate membrane. Bulging bluish hymen on inspection is pathognomonic. Treatment: cruciate incision and drainage.

</details>

**Clinical logic — why not MRKH:**
- MRKH has no bulge at introitus (no haematocolpos forms because there is no outflow from the uterus at all — the uterus is absent)
- Imperforate hymen: uterus PRESENT, outflow BLOCKED → blood accumulates (haematocolpos) → bluish bulge
- The cyclical pain is the uterus contracting to expel blood with nowhere to go

**Second 2023 version (Q1001):** Suprapubic bulge + rectal bulge on PR exam — this is haematometra (blood in uterus) + haematocolpos, confirmed by clinical and PR findings.

---

### 11. Lactational Amenorrhoea

**Q504 — Mechanism**

Lactational amenorrhoea is due to:

- A. Prolactin-induced inhibition of GnRH
- B. Prolactin-induced inhibition of FSH
- C. Oxytocin-induced inhibition of GnRH
- D. Oxytocin-induced inhibition of FSH

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Prolactin-induced inhibition of GnRH** — Suckling → prolactin surge → suppression of hypothalamic GnRH → ↓ LH/FSH → anovulation. Effective contraception only if: <6 months postpartum, exclusively breastfeeding, amenorrhoeic (LAM method).

</details>

**Why not oxytocin:** Oxytocin is the milk ejection hormone — it acts on myoepithelial cells. It has no significant role in suppressing GnRH. Prolactin is the anti-reproductive hormone of lactation.

---

### 12. Ovarian Hyperstimulation Syndrome (OHSS)

**Q623 — OHSS Clinical Scenario**

A 26-year-old with delayed cycles triggered with hCG for follicular rupture develops, on day 19, dyspnoea, reduced urine output, abdominal bloating, and pain. Diagnosis:

- A. Theca lutein cysts
- B. Ovarian hyperstimulation syndrome (OHSS)
- C. Ruptured ectopic pregnancy
- D. Ruptured corpus luteum cyst

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Ovarian hyperstimulation syndrome (OHSS)** — OHSS typically appears 5–10 days after hCG trigger; features include massive ovarian enlargement, ascites, pleural effusion, oliguria, and haemoconcentration.

</details>

**Distinguishing features:** The timing (post-hCG trigger, delayed onset) and the combination of abdominal symptoms + respiratory symptoms + oliguria are the OHSS signature. Ruptured ectopic would present acutely with haemoperitoneum; corpus luteum rupture does not cause pleural effusion.

---

### 13. Menorrhagia: Drug Without Evidence

**Q509 — Drugs for Menorrhagia**

Use of which drug for menorrhagia is NOT supported by clinical evidence?

- A. Tranexamic acid
- B. Combined oral contraceptive pill
- C. Ethamsylate
- D. Progesterone-only pills (cyclical)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Ethamsylate** — Studies have failed to demonstrate efficacy of ethamsylate in reducing menstrual blood loss. Tranexamic acid (antifibrinolytic), OCPs, and cyclical progestogens all have documented efficacy.

</details>

**Memory aid — drugs that WORK for menorrhagia:**
- **Tranexamic acid** — antifibrinolytic; 30–58% reduction in blood loss
- **NSAIDs (mefenamic acid)** — reduce prostaglandins; 25–35% reduction
- **Cyclical progestogens** — for anovulatory DUB
- **LNG-IUS (Mirena)** — most effective long-term (80% reduction)
- **OCP** — reduces flow by 40–50%
- **Ethamsylate** — theoretically reduces capillary fragility; NO good trial evidence

---

### 14. Risk Factors for Carcinoma Cervix

**Q592 — Cervical Cancer Risk Factors**

Risk factors for carcinoma cervix include: a) HPV b) Smoking c) Late menarche d) Nulliparity e) Early sexual intercourse

- A. a, b, e
- B. b, c, d
- C. c, d, e
- D. a, c, d

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. a, b, e (HPV, smoking, early sexual intercourse)** — HPV infection (especially types 16 and 18), smoking, and early sexual activity are major risk factors. Late menarche and nulliparity are not risk factors.

</details>

**Mnemonic — cervix cancer risk: "HEMP-S"**
- **H** — HPV (16, 18, 31, 33)
- **E** — Early sexual intercourse (< age 16)
- **M** — Multiple sexual partners
- **P** — Parity (multiparous, multiple partners through deliveries)
- **S** — Smoking (doubles risk independently of HPV)

Late menarche is protective (less cervical metaplasia during adolescent high-risk period). Nulliparity is actually not a risk factor — it is a protective factor for cervical cancer.

---

### 15. Chadwick's Sign — A Misplaced Question

**Q705 — Chadwick's Sign Classification**

2-month amenorrhoea, bluish vaginal discoloration (Chadwick's sign) — sign type?

- A. Presumptive signs
- B. Probable signs
- C. Positive signs
- D. Diagnostic signs

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Probable signs** — Chadwick's sign (bluish discoloration of vagina/cervix due to vascular congestion) is a probable sign of pregnancy. Presumptive = missed period, nausea. Positive = FHR heard, US visualisation.

</details>

This question appears in amenorrhoea/endocrine sections because Chadwick's sign is a CAUSE of secondary amenorrhoea (pregnancy). Remember the hierarchy: Presumptive (subjective) → Probable (objective but not conclusive) → Positive (conclusive).

---

## ⚠️ Bias Removal Section

### Common Confusions

**Metropathia haemorrhagica vs Endometrial Hyperplasia vs Carcinoma:**
| Feature | Metropathia | Simple Hyperplasia | Atypical Hyperplasia | Carcinoma |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Pattern | Cystic glandular hyperplasia | Glandular crowding without atypia | Atypia present | Invasion |
| Age | Perimenopausal | Any reproductive age | Perimenopausal | Postmenopausal usually |
| Malignant risk | Nil (benign) | <1% | 29% → cancer | Already malignant |
| Treatment | Progestogens, D&C | Progestogens | Hysterectomy | Surgery ± RT |

**DUB vs PMB:**
- DUB = reproductive age + anovulatory + no structural cause
- PMB (postmenopausal bleeding) = ANY bleeding >12 months after last period → endometrial cancer until proven otherwise → ALWAYS do endometrial sampling

**Hypothalamic vs Pituitary amenorrhoea:**
- Both are hypogonadotrophic (low FSH/LH)
- Distinguish with GnRH stimulation test: pituitary responds to GnRH (levels rise); hypothalamic cause = pituitary responds to exogenous GnRH (pituitary is intact, the problem is upstream)

---

## 🔄 Variations and Twists

**Scenario: Teenager with menorrhagia from day 1 of menarche**
Think coagulopathy (AUB-C category) — Von Willebrand disease, thrombocytopaenia. The PALM causes are unlikely in a young teen with no history of procedures. Always ask about bleeding from other sites (gums, easy bruising).

**Scenario: Athlete with secondary amenorrhoea, low BMI**
This is the female athlete triad: low energy availability + bone stress injury + hypothalamic amenorrhoea. FSH and LH will both be low (hypogonadotrophic). Treatment = weight gain and reduced training intensity; not hormonal replacement alone.

**PCOS trap in amenorrhoea:**
Rotterdam criteria for PCOS diagnosis (need 2 of 3):
1. Oligo/anovulation (irregular cycles)
2. Clinical or biochemical hyperandrogenism (acne, hirsutism; elevated testosterone)
3. Polycystic ovarian morphology on ultrasound (≥12 follicles 2–9mm in one ovary OR ovarian volume >10 mL)

**Trap question:** LH:FSH ratio elevated in PCOS is a classic exam point (typically >2:1 or >3:1 in classic PCOS), BUT it is NOT part of the Rotterdam criteria and is NOT required for diagnosis. Many exams try to make you choose LH:FSH as a diagnostic criterion — it is not.

**Sheehan's syndrome:**
- Cause: Massive PPH → anterior pituitary infarction (pituitary enlarges during pregnancy, making it vulnerable to ischaemia)
- Sequence of loss: Growth hormone (first, most sensitive) → LH/FSH → TSH → ACTH (last)
- Clinical triad: Failure to lactate + no return of menstruation + cold intolerance/fatigue
- Prolactin is low (not high) — this distinguishes it from prolactinoma

---

## 🧠 Memory Engineering

**PALM-COEIN:** "PALM trees need COINS to grow"
- PALM = structural (you can PALM them — feel them)
- COEIN = non-structural (coin-sized problems you cannot feel)

**Amenorrhoea investigation order:** "P-P-T-G-K"
1. **P**regnancy test
2. **P**rolactin
3. **T**SH
4. **G**onadotrophins (FSH/LH)
5. **K**aryotype (if primary, with absent secondary characteristics)

**Turner vs AIS memory:**
- Turner: "TurNER — No Egg Reserves" → hypergonadotrophic
- AIS: "AIS — Absent Inguinal Sensitivity" → testes in inguinal canal, no androgen response

**Imperforate hymen:** "Bluish Bulge Before Bleeding" — blood backs up, bulges blue at introitus

**Metropathia haemorrhagica:** "No Progesterone → No Stop Signal → Floods after Drought"

---

## ⚡ Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### Normal Menstrual Parameters Quick Reference
- Cycle: 21–35 days | Duration: 3–7 days | Loss: 20–80 mL

### PALM-COEIN Quick Reference
- **P**olyp | **A**denomyosis | **L**eiomyoma | **M**alignancy
- **C**oagulopathy | **O**vulatory | **E**ndometrial | **I**atrogenic | **N**ot classified

### Primary Amenorrhoea — Top 3 Causes
1. Ovarian dysgenesis (Turner) — 30–40% (most common)
2. MRKH syndrome — 15%
3. Imperforate hymen — common outflow tract cause

### Secondary Amenorrhoea — Investigation Priority
1. Pregnancy test → 2. Prolactin → 3. TSH → 4. FSH/LH → 5. USS

### AUB-O (DUB) Key Rules
- Diagnosis of exclusion
- Does NOT occur postmenopausally
- Metropathia = amenorrhoea then flood
- Perimenopausal menorrhagia → D&C first

### Drugs for Menorrhagia — Evidence Table
| Drug | Evidence | Mechanism |
|---|---|---|
| Tranexamic acid | Strong | Antifibrinolytic |
| LNG-IUS | Strongest (long-term) | Local progestogen |
| NSAIDs | Good | ↓ Prostaglandins |
| OCP | Good | ↓ Endometrial growth |
| Cyclical progestogens | Moderate | Oppose oestrogen |
| Ethamsylate | NONE | — |

---

# Chapter 8 — Menopause, HRT & Pelvic Floor Disorders

> "The pelvis supports not just the uterus but the entire quality of a woman's later life. When it fails — whether through hormonal withdrawal or structural collapse — understanding the mechanism tells you exactly how to fix it."

---

## 🎯 Big Picture First

This chapter covers two seemingly distinct areas — the hormonal transition of menopause and the mechanical failures of the pelvic floor — but they are linked by one physiological thread: **oestrogen withdrawal**. Oestrogen maintains the epithelium, the bone, the vascular wall, and critically, the collagen and fascial support of the pelvic floor. When oestrogen falls, everything falls.

For NEET-PG and INI-CET, this chapter generates a reliable cluster of questions every year:

**Menopause/HRT questions** almost always test the same three facts:
1. HRT is NOT indicated for coronary artery disease prevention (WHI trial — the most high-yield clinical trial in this chapter)
2. Oestrogen-only HRT is used only after hysterectomy; intact uterus always requires combined therapy
3. Raloxifene does NOT treat hot flushes (it may worsen them)

**Pelvic floor questions** cluster around:
1. Incontinence type identification from clinical description
2. Prolapse grade and its surgical management
3. Fistula aetiology in India vs developed countries
4. Asherman's syndrome — cause, investigation, treatment

---

## 📊 Question Pattern Map

| Tested Concept | Exam Pattern |
|---|---|
| HRT not for CAD | "HRT useful in all EXCEPT…" (coronary heart disease) — appears 3+ times |
| Menopause hormone profile | FSH↑↑, LH↑, oestrogen↓ (high FSH + high LH) |
| Hot flush mechanism | Increased noradrenaline + decreased oestrogen |
| Raloxifene and hot flushes | Does NOT help; may worsen |
| Prolapse complete family | Vaginal hysterectomy + pelvic floor repair |
| Ring pessary verification | Remains in situ after Valsalva |
| Mackenrodt's ligament | Primary support damaged in prolapse |
| Asherman's syndrome | Amenorrhoea after D&C; hysteroscopy gold standard |
| Asherman's highest risk | D&C for postpartum haemorrhage |
| Cystocele location | Upper 2/3 anterior vaginal wall |
| Rectovaginal fistula initial | Diversion colostomy first |
| VVF post-op advice | Sexual abstinence 3 months + avoid pregnancy 1 year |

---

## 📚 Core Concept Deep Dive

### 1. Menopause — Physiology and Definition

**Definition:** Menopause is the **permanent cessation of menstruation** resulting from loss of ovarian follicular activity. It is diagnosed clinically as **12 consecutive months of amenorrhoea** with no other pathological or physiological cause (e.g., not pregnancy, not breastfeeding).

**Key numbers to commit:**
- Average age of menopause: 51 years (Indian data: 45–47 years)
- Premature ovarian failure (POF)/premature ovarian insufficiency (POI): menopause before age 40
- Premature menopause: before age 45

**Perimenopause:** The transition period beginning with irregular cycles and ending 12 months after the final menstrual period. Can last 2–8 years. Vasomotor symptoms are most prominent during this phase.

**The hormonal cascade of menopause:**

The ovary runs out of responsive follicles. Here is the chain:
1. Follicular depletion → ↓ Inhibin B (first to fall, years before menopause)
2. ↓ Inhibin B → ↑ FSH (FSH starts rising as early as 5–10 years before menopause — FSH is the earliest hormonal marker)
3. Eventually ↓ oestradiol → ↑ LH as well
4. Final state: FSH >30–40 IU/L, LH elevated, oestradiol <30 pmol/L

**FSH is the most sensitive single test** — it rises earliest and most markedly. LH also rises but less dramatically.

---

**Q662 — Menopause: Hormone Profile**

A 52-year-old woman presents with 16 months of amenorrhoea. Expected hormone profile?

- A. High LH and high FSH
- B. Low FSH and low LH
- C. Low FSH and normal LH
- D. High FSH and low LH

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. High LH and high FSH** — Ovarian failure → loss of oestrogen/inhibin feedback → pituitary releases high FSH (>30–40 IU/L) and LH. FSH is the most sensitive single marker of menopause.

</details>

**Why D is wrong:** High FSH with LOW LH would suggest a selective problem — but in true ovarian failure, both gonadotrophins are elevated because both receive inhibitory feedback from oestrogen and inhibin. If only FSH were high with low LH, think inhibin B deficiency in isolation (not a standard clinical scenario tested at NEET-PG).

---

**Q754 — Premature Ovarian Failure**

Which is increased in premature ovarian failure?

- A. Serum Inhibin
- B. Serum FSH
- C. Serum Estradiol
- D. Both A and B

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Serum FSH** — POF shows hypergonadotrophic hypogonadism: FSH rises (>40 IU/L) due to loss of negative feedback from depleted follicles producing low estradiol and inhibin.

</details>

**Pattern recognition:** Whenever you see "premature ovarian failure" in a question — think hypergonadotrophic hypogonadism, elevated FSH, the hormonal profile identical to surgical menopause or radiation-induced menopause but occurring before age 40.

---

### 2. Symptoms of Menopause

**Vasomotor symptoms (hot flushes, night sweats):**
- Affect 75–80% of menopausal women
- Most severe in the first 1–2 years after menopause
- Mechanism: oestrogen withdrawal → decreased hypothalamic endorphins → central norepinephrine and serotonin dysregulation → narrowed thermoregulatory set point → small temperature rises trigger disproportionate heat dissipation

---

**Q709 — Hot Flush Mechanism**

Hot flushes are experienced as a result of:

- A. Increased noradrenaline alone
- B. Decreased oestrogen alone
- C. Increased noradrenaline and decreased oestrogen
- D. Increased noradrenaline and oestrogen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Increased noradrenaline and decreased oestrogen** — Oestrogen deficiency reduces hypothalamic endorphins, increasing norepinephrine and serotonin, which disturb the hypothalamic thermoregulatory set-point.

</details>

**Why not B alone:** Decreased oestrogen is the initial trigger, but the immediate pathophysiology involves increased noradrenaline acting on the thermoregulatory centre. Both components are part of the mechanism. Examiner is specifically testing whether you know the neurotransmitter pathway.

---

**Complete symptom overview for examination:**

| Domain | Symptoms |
|---|---|
| Vasomotor | Hot flushes, night sweats, palpitations |
| Urogenital | Vaginal dryness, dyspareunia, urinary frequency, recurrent UTIs |
| Psychological | Mood changes, sleep disturbance, poor concentration |
| Skeletal | Accelerated bone loss → osteoporosis (most rapid in first 5 years post-menopause) |
| Cardiovascular | Increased CVD risk (after menopause) |
| Skin/hair | Skin thinning, hair loss |

---

### 3. Hormone Replacement Therapy — The Most Tested Topic

HRT is oestrogen administered to replace the ovarian oestrogen lost at menopause. The choice of regimen depends on whether the uterus is present.

**Critical decision rule:**
- **Intact uterus → Combined oestrogen + progestogen HRT** (progestogen protects endometrium from oestrogen-driven hyperplasia/carcinoma)
- **Post-hysterectomy → Oestrogen-only HRT** (no endometrium to protect; adding unnecessary progestogen increases breast cancer risk)

---

**Q386 — HRT and Endometrial Protection**

A 53-year-old woman with an intact uterus requires HRT for severe menopausal symptoms. Why must progestogen be added to oestrogen therapy?

- A. To prevent cardiovascular events
- B. To protect against oestrogen-induced endometrial hyperplasia and cancer
- C. To prevent breast cancer from oestrogen
- D. To improve mood and libido

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. To protect against oestrogen-induced endometrial hyperplasia and cancer** — Unopposed oestrogen stimulates endometrial proliferation, increasing endometrial hyperplasia and carcinoma risk; progestogen (continuous or cyclical) is mandatory in women with an intact uterus. Women after hysterectomy can take oestrogen-only HRT.

</details>

---

**HRT Indications — Confirmed by Evidence:**
1. Vasomotor symptoms (hot flushes, night sweats) — MOST effective treatment
2. Genitourinary syndrome of menopause (vaginal atrophy, urinary symptoms)
3. Osteoporosis prevention (especially in women <60 or within 10 years of menopause)
4. Premature ovarian failure/premature menopause (HRT until age 51 as physiological replacement)

**WHI Trial (Women's Health Initiative) — The Most Important Clinical Trial Here:**
The WHI trial (2002) changed practice globally. Key findings:
- Combined E+P HRT → increased breast cancer, MI, stroke, VTE
- Oestrogen-only HRT → decreased breast cancer (but still increased stroke/VTE)
- **HRT does NOT prevent coronary artery disease** — this is the most repeatedly examined WHI finding

**The "Timing Hypothesis" (nuance for advanced questions):**
- WHI studied women who were >10 years post-menopause when starting HRT
- In younger women (<60 years, within 10 years of menopause), HRT may reduce CVD risk
- This is the basis for the "window of opportunity" — but for exams, the tested fact remains: HRT is NOT recommended for CAD prevention

---

**Q409 / Q541 / Q579 / Q663 — HRT Not for CAD (appears 4 times)**

HRT is useful in all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Vasomotor symptoms (hot flushes)
- B. Osteoporosis prevention
- C. Vaginal atrophy
- D. Coronary heart disease prevention

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Coronary heart disease prevention** — Current evidence (WHI trial) shows HRT does NOT reduce cardiovascular events in older postmenopausal women. HRT relieves vasomotor symptoms, protects bone, and reverses urogenital atrophy.

</details>

**Examiner's pattern:** This exact question with these exact options has appeared at least four times across NEET-PG 2015, 2016, PrepLadder, and PG Blazer. Answer is always D. Do not overthink it. The WHI trial data is definitive for the exam context.

---

**Q498 — Vulvovaginal Atrophy Treatment**

Drug of choice for symptomatic vulvovaginal atrophy (atrophic vaginitis) in menopause?

- A. Topical oestrogen cream
- B. Systemic antihistamines
- C. Tamoxifen
- D. Systemic HRT only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Topical oestrogen cream** — Local oestrogen (cream/pessary/ring) relieves vulvovaginal atrophy with minimal systemic absorption. Tamoxifen is anti-oestrogenic and worsens vaginal dryness.

</details>

**Why topical, not systemic:** Topical oestrogen acts locally on the urogenital epithelium. It has minimal systemic absorption, meaning it can be used even in women where systemic HRT is contraindicated (e.g., those with previous breast cancer, in consultation with oncology). Tamoxifen is a selective oestrogen receptor modulator (SERM) — it is anti-oestrogenic in breast and uterine tissue but paradoxically worsens vaginal dryness.

---

### 4. Raloxifene — The Hot Flush Trap

**Q527 — Treatment of Hot Flushes: Raloxifene**

Which does NOT help decrease hot flushes in menopausal women?

- A. Androgens
- B. Raloxifene
- C. Isoflavones (phytoestrogens)
- D. Tibolone

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Raloxifene** — Raloxifene (SERM) protects bone and breast/endometrium but does NOT relieve vasomotor symptoms and may worsen hot flushes. Tibolone, isoflavones, androgens, and HRT all reduce flushes.

</details>

**Raloxifene's profile:**
- Acts as oestrogen agonist in bone → prevents osteoporosis
- Acts as oestrogen antagonist in breast and endometrium → reduces breast cancer risk (STAR trial)
- Acts as oestrogen antagonist in hypothalamus → WORSENS hot flushes
- Does NOT relieve vaginal dryness

This is why raloxifene is used for osteoporosis prevention (not vasomotor symptoms) in postmenopausal women who cannot take HRT. It is NOT HRT.

**Non-hormonal options for hot flushes:** Venlafaxine (SNRI), clonidine, gabapentin, phytoestrogens (isoflavones), cognitive behavioural therapy.

---

### 5. Lichen Sclerosus — Postmenopausal Vulval Condition

**Q395 — Lichen Sclerosus Treatment**

A 60-year-old post-menopausal woman presents with severe vulval pruritus and white, parchment-like atrophic skin of the vulva in a "figure-of-8" distribution around the vulva and perianal area. Biopsy shows epidermal thinning and homogenised dermis. Treatment of choice?

- A. Topical oestrogen cream
- B. High-potency topical corticosteroid (clobetasol propionate 0.05%)
- C. Topical antifungal (clotrimazole)
- D. Vulvectomy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. High-potency topical corticosteroid (clobetasol propionate 0.05%)** — Lichen sclerosus is treated with ultra-potent topical corticosteroids (clobetasol propionate); topical oestrogen is ineffective as it is NOT an oestrogenic condition. Long-term follow-up is needed as LS carries ~5% risk of squamous cell carcinoma of the vulva.

</details>

**Critical trap:** Lichen sclerosus looks like it should respond to oestrogen (white atrophic postmenopausal skin) — but it is an autoimmune condition, NOT caused by oestrogen deficiency. Topical oestrogen does nothing for lichen sclerosus. The treatment is immunosuppressive — ultra-potent topical steroid.

**Figure-of-8 distribution** (involving both vulva and perianal area) is pathognomonic of lichen sclerosus. Candida vulvovaginitis would have a cheesy discharge; atrophic vaginitis responds to topical oestrogen.

**Long-term risk:** ~5% risk of vulval squamous cell carcinoma — these patients need indefinite surveillance.

---

### 6. Urinary Incontinence — Clinical Differentiation

Incontinence questions in NEET-PG require you to identify the TYPE from a clinical scenario. The types are:

| Type | Mechanism | Clinical Feature | Treatment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stress (GSI) | Urethral sphincter failure / pelvic floor laxity | Leak on coughing, sneezing, exercise; no urgency | Pelvic floor exercises, TVT/TOT surgery |
| Urge (OAB) | Detrusor overactivity | Strong urge, cannot defer, frequency, nocturia | Anticholinergics (oxybutynin), bladder training |
| Overflow | Detrusor underactivity / outflow obstruction | Dribbling, incomplete emptying, distended bladder | Catheterisation, treat obstruction |
| Fistula | Anatomical defect | Continuous leakage day and night | Surgical repair |
| Mixed | Combined stress + urge | Features of both | Treat dominant component first |

**The key clinical differentiators:**
- Stress: leak WITH physical stress, NO urgency, NO frequency
- Urge: leak PRECEDED by urgency, increased frequency, nocturia
- Overflow: constant dribble, large post-void residual, no urge component
- Fistula: CONTINUOUS leakage, even lying down — this is the distinguishing feature

---

**Q398 — Stress Urinary Incontinence Mechanism**

A 45-year-old multiparous woman leaks urine on coughing, sneezing, and exercise. She denies urgency or frequency. Urodynamics shows involuntary urine loss with increased intra-abdominal pressure without detrusor contraction. The pathophysiological mechanism is:

- A. Detrusor overactivity
- B. Inadequate urethral closure mechanism (urethral sphincter deficiency/hypermobility)
- C. Vesicovaginal fistula
- D. Overflow incontinence from detrusor underactivity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Inadequate urethral closure mechanism (urethral sphincter deficiency/hypermobility)** — Stress urinary incontinence results from failure of the urethral closure pressure to exceed bladder pressure during physical stress; caused by pelvic floor weakness, urethral hypermobility, or intrinsic sphincter deficiency (ISD) — not detrusor overactivity (which causes urge incontinence).

</details>

**Urodynamics insight:** The gold standard for diagnosing incontinence type is urodynamic study. Stress incontinence shows urine loss during provocation WITHOUT a detrusor contraction. Urge incontinence (detrusor overactivity) shows an involuntary detrusor contraction coinciding with the urge.

---

**Q399 — Urgency Incontinence: First-Line Drug**

A 55-year-old woman complains of sudden, compelling urge to void followed by involuntary urine leakage. She voids 12 times per day and twice at night. Urodynamics confirms detrusor overactivity. First-line pharmacological treatment?

- A. Duloxetine (SNRI)
- B. Oxybutynin (anticholinergic)
- C. Mirabegron (beta-3 adrenergic agonist)
- D. Topical oestrogen vaginal cream

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Oxybutynin (anticholinergic)** — Anticholinergics (oxybutynin, solifenacin, tolterodine) are first-line pharmacological treatment for overactive bladder/detrusor overactivity; they reduce detrusor contractions by blocking muscarinic receptors. Mirabegron is an alternative with fewer anticholinergic side effects.

</details>

**Drug mechanism quick reference:**
- Oxybutynin: M2/M3 muscarinic receptor blocker → relaxes detrusor
- Duloxetine: SNRI → used for STRESS incontinence (enhances urethral sphincter tone via pudendal nerve), NOT urge
- Mirabegron: β3 agonist → detrusor relaxation; used when anticholinergics are contraindicated (glaucoma, cognitive impairment in elderly)

---

### 7. Uterovaginal Prolapse

**Anatomy of pelvic support:**

Three levels of pelvic support (DeLancey levels):
- **Level I:** Apex/uterus — cardinal and uterosacral ligaments
- **Level II:** Mid-vagina — pubocervical fascia (anterior), rectovaginal fascia (posterior)
- **Level III:** Distal — urogenital diaphragm, perineal body

**Primary supporting ligament of the uterus: Mackenrodt's (cardinal/transverse cervical) ligament**

---

**Q959 — Primary Damaged Ligament in Prolapse**

P2L2 with uterine prolapse — which ligament is primarily damaged?

- A. Sacrospinous ligament
- B. Mackenrodt's (cardinal/transverse cervical) ligament
- C. Broad ligament
- D. Round ligament

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Mackenrodt's (cardinal) ligament** — Primary support of uterus at cervical level. Combined with uterosacral ligaments. Damage in multiparity → uterovaginal prolapse.

</details>

**Ligament function summary:**
- Cardinal = primary cervical support (horizontal)
- Uterosacral = posterior suspension (keeps cervix pulled posteriorly)
- Round ligament = maintains anteversion (NOT a support ligament for prolapse)
- Broad ligament = NOT a true ligament; it is a peritoneal fold

---

**Classification of Uterovaginal Prolapse:**

**Classical (UK) grading:**
- 1st degree: Cervix descends but remains within vagina
- 2nd degree: Cervix reaches introitus
- 3rd degree (Procidentia): Uterus entirely outside vagina (the entire uterus protrudes)

**Compartments of prolapse:**
- Anterior wall prolapse: Cystocele (upper 2/3) + Urethrocele (lower 1/3)
- Posterior wall prolapse: Rectocele (lower 2/3) + Enterocele (upper posterior)
- Apical: Uterine prolapse / vault prolapse post-hysterectomy

---

**Q878 — Cystocele Location**

Upper two-thirds anterior vaginal wall prolapse is?

- A. Cystocele
- B. Urethrocele
- C. Rectocele
- D. Enterocele

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Cystocele** — The upper two-thirds of the anterior vaginal wall is supported by the pubocervical fascia overlying the bladder base; weakness here allows the bladder to herniate, forming a cystocele.

</details>

**Anatomical aid:** "Cystocele = upper anterior (bladder herniates). Urethrocele = lower anterior (urethra protrudes)." In clinical practice, they often coexist as cystourethrocele.

---

**Q804 — Cystocele Treatment Options**

Which of the following are treatment options for cystocele?

- A. Anterior colporrhaphy
- B. Transvaginal tape
- C. Transobturator tape
- D. All of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. All of the above** — Cystocele is treated by anterior colporrhaphy (surgical repair), transvaginal tape (TVT), or transobturator tape (TOT) depending on associated stress urinary incontinence.

</details>

---

**Surgical management of complete prolapse:**

**Q474 / Q745 / Q922 — Complete Prolapse in Completed Family (appears 3 times)**

Preferred treatment of complete uterine prolapse in a female with completed family is:

- A. Sling surgery
- B. Vaginal hysterectomy
- C. Le Forte's repair
- D. Pessary

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Vaginal hysterectomy** — Vaginal hysterectomy (usually with anterior and posterior colporrhaphy) is the definitive surgical option for major degree uterine prolapse in a parous woman with a completed family.

</details>

**Management matrix:**

| Patient | Management |
|---|---|
| Completed family, fit for surgery | Vaginal hysterectomy + pelvic floor repair |
| Completed family, elderly, fit | Vaginal hysterectomy + repair (same) |
| Completed family, unfit for surgery | Ring pessary |
| Desirous of fertility | Pelvic floor repair (Manchester repair) — uterus preserved |
| Very elderly, medically unfit | Le Fort's colpocleisis (obliterates vagina) — only if no desire for intercourse |

**Le Fort's repair:** A colpocleisis — vagina is partially closed off. Used only in elderly medically unfit women who are not sexually active. The answer "Le Fort's" is a trap — it sounds surgical and definitive but is NOT the answer for a completed-family standard patient.

---

**Q891 / Q909 — Ring Pessary Verification (appears twice)**

In uterine prolapse, how do you know the ring pessary is in place?

- A. If not expelled after increased abdominal pressure
- B. If bleeding does not occur
- C. If the patient feels discomfort
- D. None of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. If not expelled after increased abdominal pressure** — A correctly placed ring pessary should remain in situ even with Valsalva manoeuvre/increased intra-abdominal pressure.

</details>

---

### 8. Vesicovaginal and Rectovaginal Fistulae

**Aetiology in India vs Developed Countries:**

| Country | Most Common Cause of VVF |
|---|---|
| India / Sub-Saharan Africa | Obstetric — prolonged obstructed labour causing pressure necrosis |
| Developed countries | Surgical — post-hysterectomy (most common), post-pelvic radiation |

For NEET-PG in India: **obstetric VVF** is the most common cause of vesicovaginal fistula.

**Clinical presentation of VVF:** Continuous, painless, uncontrollable leakage of urine through the vagina — even while sleeping (this distinguishes it from incontinence, which has some voluntary control component).

**Q968 / Q975 — VVF Post-operative Advice (appears twice)**

While discharging a patient after vesicovaginal fistula repair, what would you recommend?

- A. Sexual abstinence for 3 months and avoid pregnancy for a year
- B. Sexual abstinence for 3 weeks and avoid pregnancy for 6 months
- C. Sexual abstinence for 6 weeks and avoid pregnancy for a year
- D. Sexual abstinence for 6 months and avoid pregnancy for 6 years

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Sexual abstinence for 3 months and avoid pregnancy for a year** — After VVF repair, prolonged abstinence and delay of pregnancy allow adequate healing and reduce recurrence risk.

</details>

**Memory peg: "3 months REST, 1 year BABY-WAIT"**
- 3 months abstinence (coital pressure on repair site)
- 1 year before next pregnancy (uterine scar healing + VVF repair healing)

---

**Q526 — Rectovaginal Fistula: Initial Management**

Patients with rectovaginal fistula should be initially treated with:

- A. Colostomy
- B. Primary repair
- C. Posterior colporrhaphy
- D. Anterior resection

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Colostomy** — Initial diversion colostomy reduces inflammation and faecal contamination, improving surgical outcomes of definitive repair. Direct repair without diversion has higher failure rates for most rectovaginal fistulas.

</details>

**Principle:** Faecal contamination at a fresh fistula site causes infection and repair breakdown. The sequence is:
1. Diversion colostomy → allows fistula track to become clean, inflammation to settle
2. Definitive repair of rectovaginal fistula (after 3–6 months)
3. Closure of colostomy

For VVF: urinary diversion (catheter) serves the analogous purpose — Foley catheter drainage allows some small VVFs to close spontaneously, and larger ones to be repaired in optimal conditions.

**Boari flap:** A bladder flap mobilisation technique used to bridge a defect in the lower third of the ureter when direct uretero-ureteral anastomosis is not possible. It is used in uretero-vaginal fistula repair or ureteric injuries. Remember: Boari = bladder mobilised to reach ureter. Not used for VVF directly.

---

### 9. Asherman's Syndrome — Intrauterine Adhesions

**Definition:** Intrauterine adhesions (synechiae) caused by trauma to the endometrium, resulting in partial or complete obliteration of the uterine cavity.

**Q490 — Asherman Syndrome Characteristic**

Asherman syndrome is characterised by:

- A. Amenorrhoea
- B. Menorrhagia
- C. Polymenorrhoea
- D. All of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Amenorrhoea** — Asherman syndrome (intrauterine adhesions/synechiae) following uterine trauma (D&C, infection) causes amenorrhoea or hypomenorrhoea due to obliteration of the uterine cavity.

</details>

**Full clinical picture:**
- Secondary amenorrhoea or markedly reduced menstrual flow (hypomenorrhoea) after a uterine procedure
- Cyclical pelvic pain (the uterus contracts but blood cannot escape — similar mechanism to imperforate hymen but acquired)
- Infertility / recurrent miscarriage (the endometrium cannot support implantation)
- Diagnosis: Hysteroscopy (GOLD STANDARD — directly visualises adhesions)
- Saline infusion sonography and HSG can show filling defects but hysteroscopy is definitive

---

**Q695 — Asherman Syndrome: Highest Risk Procedure**

The risk of Asherman syndrome is highest when dilatation and curettage is performed for:

- A. Medical termination of pregnancy
- B. Missed abortion
- C. Dysfunctional uterine bleeding
- D. Postpartum haemorrhage

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Postpartum haemorrhage** — D&C on an infected/necrotic postpartum uterus carries the highest risk of Asherman syndrome. Curettage at 2–4 weeks postpartum (fibrotic uterus) is particularly dangerous. Missed abortion is also a significant risk.

</details>

**Why postpartum D&C is most dangerous:**
The postpartum uterus is highly vascular, the decidua is thin, and the myometrium is friable. D&C at 2–4 weeks postpartum — when the uterus is involuting and the endometrium is regenerating — leads to raw opposing endometrial surfaces that adhere as they heal. Combined with any superimposed infection (endometritis), scar formation is rapid and severe.

---

**Q390 — Asherman Syndrome: Definitive Treatment**

A 28-year-old woman with secondary amenorrhoea following suction evacuation undergoes hysteroscopy, revealing intrauterine synechiae obliterating the cavity. Definitive treatment?

- A. Combined oral contraceptive pill for 3 months
- B. GnRH agonist followed by add-back therapy
- C. Hysteroscopic adhesiolysis followed by high-dose oestrogen and intrauterine device/balloon
- D. IVF with donor embryos

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Hysteroscopic adhesiolysis followed by high-dose oestrogen and intrauterine device/balloon** — Treatment of Asherman's syndrome is hysteroscopic division of adhesions; post-operative high-dose oestrogen (± intrauterine device or Foley catheter balloon) prevents re-formation of adhesions and promotes endometrial regeneration.

</details>

**Why each part of the treatment is necessary:**
- Hysteroscopic adhesiolysis: removes the adhesions (mechanical)
- High-dose oestrogen post-operatively: stimulates endometrial regeneration, prevents re-adhesion
- Intrauterine device / Foley balloon: physical separator — prevents the raw surfaces from touching and re-adhering while they heal under oestrogen stimulus
- After 1–3 cycles of cyclical oestrogen-progestogen (to shed regenerated endometrium), the IUD is removed

---

## ⚠️ Bias Removal Section

### HRT Contraindications — Absolute vs Relative

**Absolute contraindications to HRT:**
- Oestrogen-dependent malignancy (breast cancer, endometrial cancer)
- Undiagnosed vaginal bleeding
- Active venous thromboembolism (DVT/PE)
- Active cardiovascular disease (recent MI, angina)
- Liver disease (active hepatitis, cirrhosis)
- Pregnancy

**Relative contraindications:**
- Past VTE (can consider with careful risk assessment ± anticoagulation)
- Hypertension (poorly controlled) — though transdermal oestrogen avoids hepatic first pass and has lower VTE risk
- Migraine with aura (increased stroke risk)
- Fibroids or endometriosis (relative — may be exacerbated by oestrogen)

**Common exam trap:** "Can a woman with a previous hysterectomy for endometrial cancer take HRT for menopausal symptoms?" — This is debated, but exam answer = NO (absolute contraindication to oestrogen in oestrogen-sensitive tumour).

### Confusions to Eliminate

| Confusion | Resolution |
|---|---|
| Raloxifene = HRT? | NO — raloxifene is a SERM. It does NOT relieve hot flushes |
| Topical oestrogen for lichen sclerosus? | NO — lichen sclerosus is autoimmune, not oestrogenic |
| VVF presentation = stress incontinence? | NO — VVF is continuous, day and night; stress incontinence is only with physical exertion |
| Le Fort's for all prolapse? | NO — only for elderly sexually inactive women unfit for major surgery |
| Asherman's treated with D&C? | NO — D&C CAUSES Asherman's; treatment is hysteroscopic adhesiolysis |

---

## 🔄 Variations and Twists

**Scenario — Incontinence type identification:**

Woman leaks when she coughs → Stress incontinence (no urgency)
Woman leaks before she reaches the toilet → Urge incontinence (urgency-driven)
Woman has constant wet underwear → Fistula or overflow
Elderly woman with dementia, no awareness of leaking → Functional incontinence (separate category)

**Scenario — PCOS and menopause:**
PCOS women often have late menopause (more follicular reserve due to polycystic ovaries). They may still have cycles in their mid-50s. Their FSH will eventually rise but later than average.

**Scenario — Premature ovarian insufficiency:**
A 32-year-old with hot flushes, elevated FSH, and secondary amenorrhoea. This is NOT menopause (by definition) — it is premature ovarian insufficiency (POI). Management differs: HRT is given as physiological replacement (not just symptom relief) and continued until the expected age of natural menopause (~51 years) to protect bone and cardiovascular health.

**HRT: Cyclical vs Continuous combined regimen:**
- Cyclical (sequential): Oestrogen continuously + progestogen added in second half of cycle. A monthly bleed occurs (planned withdrawal bleed). Used in perimenopausal women (accept a bleed).
- Continuous combined: Oestrogen + progestogen every day. No bleed (amenorrhoea). Used in postmenopausal women (>1 year post-menopause). If used too early, causes irregular spotting.

---

## 🧠 Memory Engineering

**Menopause hormone changes: "FSH Goes First and Fastest"**
- FSH ↑↑ (first and highest rise, most sensitive marker)
- LH ↑ (rises too but later and less)
- Oestrogen ↓
- Inhibin ↓ (the actual trigger for FSH rise)

**HRT rule: "Uterus Present → Protect It (add Progestogen)"**
- P for Progestogen, P for Protection, P for Presence of uterus

**WHI trial memory: "WHI Warned Heart Is Not Improved"**
- HRT ≠ heart protection — WHI proved it

**Raloxifene: "RAL protects RAL bones, but RAL runs from hot flushes"**
- Bone agonist, hot flush antagonist

**Incontinence types: "SU-FO"**
- **S**tress — physical exertion triggers
- **U**rge — cannot hold, must void now
- **F**istula — constant, cannot control at all
- **O**verflow — dribbles continuously, distended bladder

**Asherman's: "D&C Does Damage → Hysteroscopy Heals"**
- Caused by D&C; investigated and treated by hysteroscopy

**VVF post-op: "3 months no sex, 1 year no baby"**

**Prolapse treatment: "If family complete = Vaginal Hysterectomy + Floor Repair"**

---

## ⚡ Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### Menopause Quick Facts
- Definition: 12 months amenorrhoea (natural, no pathological cause)
- Average age India: 45–47 years; Global: 51 years
- POF: menopause <40 years
- Hormone profile: FSH ↑↑, LH ↑, Oestradiol ↓, Inhibin ↓

### HRT Decision Table

| Uterus Present? | HRT Choice |
|---|---|
| Yes (intact) | Combined oestrogen + progestogen |
| No (post-hysterectomy) | Oestrogen only |
| Breast cancer contraindication | Topical oestrogen for urogenital only (consult oncology) |
| For hot flushes, not HRT candidate | Venlafaxine, clonidine, gabapentin, isoflavones |
| For osteoporosis, not HRT candidate | Raloxifene, bisphosphonates |

### HRT Indications vs NOT-Indications

| Indication | HRT Status |
|---|---|
| Hot flushes / vasomotor symptoms | YES — first-line |
| Osteoporosis prevention | YES |
| Vaginal atrophy | YES (topical preferred) |
| Coronary artery disease prevention | NO — WHI trial |

### Incontinence Quick Comparison

| Feature | Stress | Urge | Overflow | Fistula |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Trigger | Exertion | Urgency | Obstruction/atony | None |
| Urgency | No | Yes | No | No |
| Frequency | No | Yes | No | No |
| Timing | With exertion only | After urge | Constant dribble | Constant |
| Detrusor on urodynamics | Normal | Overactive | Underactive | Normal |
| Drug treatment | Duloxetine | Oxybutynin | Bethanecol (if detrusor) | Surgery |
| Surgery | TVT/TOT | Sacral neuromodulation | Catheter ± surgery | Fistula repair |

### Prolapse Management Summary

| Scenario | Management |
|---|---|
| Grade 3 prolapse, completed family, fit | Vaginal hysterectomy + pelvic floor repair |
| Grade 3 prolapse, elderly, unfit | Ring pessary |
| Grade 3 prolapse, elderly, sexually inactive | Le Fort's colpocleisis |
| Grade 1-2 prolapse, young, desires fertility | Pelvic floor physiotherapy + Manchester repair |

### Fistula Key Points

| Fistula | India Cause | Developed World Cause | Initial Rx | Post-op |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| VVF | Obstetric (obstructed labour) | Post-hysterectomy | Catheter drainage | Abstinence 3m, no pregnancy 1yr |
| RVF | Obstetric tear / infection | Post-radiation / Crohn's | Diversion colostomy | Repair after 3–6 months |

### Asherman's Syndrome at a Glance

| Feature | Detail |
|---|---|
| Cause | D&C (esp. postpartum haemorrhage, missed abortion) |
| Highest risk D&C | For postpartum haemorrhage (fibrotic, infected uterus) |
| Presentation | Secondary amenorrhoea / hypomenorrhoea / infertility |
| Investigation | Hysteroscopy (gold standard), HSG (filling defect) |
| Treatment | Hysteroscopic adhesiolysis + high-dose oestrogen + IUD/balloon |

---

*Chapter 8 covers 30 questions from the NEET-PG question bank (11 menopause/HRT + 19 pelvic floor). All concepts tested above have appeared in previous papers from 2015–2024, with several questions appearing 2–4 times across different years.*




---

# Chapter 9: Benign Gynaecological Conditions
### Fibroids · Endometriosis · Ovarian Cysts · PCOS · Adenomyosis

---

## 🎯 Big Picture First

Benign gynaecological conditions dominate the gynaecology section of NEET-PG. Year after year, roughly **25–30% of all gynaecology MCQs** come from just four topics covered here: uterine fibroids, endometriosis, PCOS, and ovarian cysts. These are not pure recall topics — the examiner tests whether you can apply concepts to clinical vignettes. A stem that says "35-year-old, menorrhagia, uterus 16-week size, wants fertility" expects you to reason through fibroid type, fertility implications, and surgical choice — not just recite definitions.

**The three patterns the examiner loves:**
1. Identifying the correct subtype from a clinical feature or complication (e.g., which fibroid causes menorrhagia vs. obstructive symptoms)
2. Choosing the right management step at the right life stage (young woman wanting fertility vs. 45-year-old with completed family)
3. Knowing which investigation is gold standard vs. which is useful-but-not-definitive (laparoscopy vs. USS for endometriosis; MRI vs. CT for fibroids)

---

## 📊 Question Pattern Map

| Trap | What Students Write | What Examiners Want |
|------|--------------------|--------------------|
| Most common fibroid degeneration | Red degeneration (because it's dramatic) | **Hyaline degeneration** (~65% of all) |
| Red degeneration: when in pregnancy | Any trimester | **2nd trimester** (rapid uterine growth) |
| Fibroid: malignant transformation risk | "It's common" | Extremely rare, < 0.1% |
| Endometriosis gold standard | CA-125 | **Laparoscopy + biopsy** |
| Endometriosis vs adenomyosis | Confused in clinical vignettes | Adenomyosis = glands **inside** myometrium; globular uterus |
| PCOS USG description | "cysts" | **String of pearls** = 12+ follicles (2–9 mm) at periphery |
| PCOS Rotterdam: how many criteria needed | 3 of 3 | **2 of 3** criteria |
| CT vs MRI for fibroid mapping | Both acceptable | CT is NOT an adequate alternative; **MRI is gold standard** |
| Ovarian torsion: which side | Either side equally | **Right side** more common (sigmoid limits left) |

---

## 📚 Core Concept Deep Dive

---

### PART 1: Uterine Fibroids (Leiomyomas)

#### What They Are

Fibroids are benign monoclonal tumours of uterine smooth muscle, dependent on oestrogen and progesterone for growth. They are the most common solid pelvic tumours in women. Incidence: 20–40% of women of reproductive age; more common in African women. They are oestrogen-sensitive — hence they grow during pregnancy and regress after menopause.

#### Types and Their Clinical Signatures

The type of fibroid determines the dominant symptom. This is a favourite MCQ angle.

| Type | Location | Dominant Symptom | Key Points |
|------|----------|-----------------|------------|
| **Submucosal** | Protrudes into uterine cavity | **Menorrhagia** (most severe) | Even a small submucosal fibroid causes heavy bleeding; type 0 = fully intracavitary |
| **Intramural** | Within myometrium | Menorrhagia + pelvic pressure | Most common type overall |
| **Subserosal** | Outer surface of uterus | Pressure symptoms (urinary, bowel) | Least likely to cause menorrhagia |
| **Pedunculated** | On a stalk (submucosal or subserosal) | Torsion; submucosal type can prolapse through cervix | Pedunculated subserosal = risk of torsion |
| **Broad ligament** | Between leaves of broad ligament | Ureteric compression, flank pain | Can mimic adnexal mass |
| **Cervical** | In cervical tissue | Obstructive dystocia, retention of urine | Rare |

**Exam pearl:** Submucosal fibroids cause the most menorrhagia per unit size because they distort the endometrial cavity. A 1 cm submucosal fibroid causes more bleeding than a 5 cm intramural fibroid.

---

#### PYQ Integrated: Fibroid Subtypes and Imaging

**Question from database:**

> Regarding imaging of uterine fibroids, which statement is FALSE?
> - A. Ultrasound is ideal to confirm the diagnosis
> - B. Saline infusion sonography (SIS) better detects submucous fibroids
> - C. MRI is superior to ultrasound for identifying exact fibroid location
> - **D. CT scanning is an adequate alternative to MRI ← CORRECT ANSWER (FALSE statement)**

**Why this matters:** MRI is the gold standard for fibroid mapping pre-myomectomy or uterine artery embolisation (UAE). It identifies exact location (submucosal vs. intramural), number, vascularity, and relationship to the endometrial cavity. CT has poor soft-tissue contrast for the uterus and cannot substitute for MRI. USS is the first-line investigation; SIS (saline infusion sonography) enhances detection of submucosal fibroids by filling the cavity with saline.

---

#### Fibroid Degeneration — The Most Tested Complication

**Types of degeneration** (remember: "Hyaline Monsters Cry Red Calcified Sarcomas"):
- **H**yaline — most common (65%), acellular collagen replacement
- **M**yxomatous — gelatinous, mucoid change
- **C**ystic — liquefaction of hyaline; large cavities
- **R**ed (Carneous) — haemorrhagic infarction; most common in **pregnancy** (2nd trimester)
- **C**alcification — "womb stones"; post-menopausal; dystrophic
- **S**arcomatous — malignant transformation; extremely rare (< 0.1%)

**PYQ — Red degeneration:**

> Red (carneous) degeneration of a uterine fibroid most commonly occurs in:
> - A. 1st trimester
> - **B. 2nd trimester ← ANSWER**
> - C. 3rd trimester
> - D. Puerperium

**Mechanism:** Rapid uterine growth → relative ischaemia of fibroid → haemorrhagic infarction. The haemoglobin leeches into the fibroid, giving the cut surface a raw meat (carneous/red) appearance. Clinically: acute abdominal pain, low-grade fever, focal uterine tenderness, no peritoneal signs. **Management is conservative** — analgesics and hydration. Do NOT operate; myomectomy in pregnancy carries catastrophic risk.

**PYQ — Fibroid in pregnancy:**

> 28 weeks pregnancy with abdominal pain, fever, and ultrasound showing a fibroid. Next management:
> - A. Hysterectomy
> - **B. Conservative management (analgesics, hydration) ← ANSWER**
> - C. Termination of pregnancy
> - D. Myomectomy

**PYQ — Least likely change:**

> An asymptomatic woman with a uterine fibroid declines treatment. Which change is LEAST likely?
> - A. Calcium deposition (calcification)
> - **B. Malignant transformation (sarcoma) ← ANSWER**
> - C. Infection and haemorrhage
> - D. Torsion (pedunculated fibroid)

Do not confuse leiomyosarcoma (a separate entity, not transformation of a fibroid) with sarcomatous degeneration of a fibroid. Sarcomatous degeneration in a pre-existing fibroid is < 0.1%.

---

#### Fibroid Management — Decision Framework

This is the single most important table for clinical scenario MCQs:

| Clinical Scenario | Preferred Management |
|------------------|---------------------|
| Asymptomatic fibroid, premenopausal | Expectant — reassure, annual review |
| Symptomatic, completed family, perimenopausal | **Hysterectomy** (definitive) |
| Symptomatic, young woman, wants fertility, submucosal | **Hysteroscopic myomectomy** |
| Symptomatic, intramural/subserosal, wants fertility | **Laparoscopic or open myomectomy** |
| Pre-operative shrinkage or perimenopausal bridge | **GnRH agonist** (3–6 months; not long-term) |
| UAE (Uterine Artery Embolisation) | Non-surgical; good for multiple fibroids; not if fertility desired |
| Fibroids in pregnancy (red degeneration) | Conservative (analgesics) |
| 45-year-old, menorrhagia, submucosal fibroid | **Hysterectomy** (most definitive) |

**PYQ — Management of menorrhagia with submucosal fibroid (45-year-old):**

> A 45-year-old woman with 3 months of menorrhagia and a 2–3 cm submucosal fibroid. Best management?
> - A. OCP for 3 months
> - B. Cyclical progesterone for 3 months
> - C. Endometrial sampling alone
> - **D. Hysterectomy ← ANSWER**

**Why D?** Age 45, completed family, submucosal fibroid is the structural cause. Medical options (OCP, progestins) are temporising and do not address the structural cause. Hysteroscopic myomectomy would be reasonable if fertility preservation was desired, but with completed family and persistent heavy bleeding, hysterectomy is definitive.

---

#### GnRH Agonists for Fibroids — Why Only Short-Term?

GnRH agonists (leuprolide, goserelin) suppress oestrogen to near-castrate levels → fibroid volume decreases 30–50% over 3 months. **Why not use long-term?**
- Hypo-oestrogenic side effects: hot flushes, vaginal atrophy, bone loss (osteoporosis)
- Fibroids return to pre-treatment size within 3–6 months of stopping
- Use: pre-operative shrinkage (reduces intraoperative blood loss), correction of anaemia, or as a bridge to menopause in perimenopausal women

---

#### Hysterectomy Types — The Examiner Always Asks

| Type | What Is Removed | When Used |
|------|----------------|-----------|
| **Subtotal (supracervical)** | Uterine body; cervix kept | Simple benign conditions |
| **Total hysterectomy** | Uterus + cervix | Standard for benign disease |
| **Wertheim's (radical) hysterectomy** | Uterus + cervix + parametrium + upper vaginal cuff + pelvic nodes | **Cervical cancer (Stage IA2–IIA)** |
| **Schauta's operation** | Vaginal radical hysterectomy | Same indications as Wertheim's, vaginal approach |

---

### PART 2: Endometriosis

#### Definition and Pathogenesis

Endometriosis = presence of functional endometrial glands and stroma **outside the uterus**. The dominant theory is **Sampson's retrograde menstruation** — menstrual blood flows retrograde through the tubes into the peritoneal cavity, where shed endometrial cells implant and grow.

**Do not confuse with adenomyosis:**
- Adenomyosis = endometrial glands/stroma > 2.5 mm deep within the **myometrium** (internal endometriosis)
- Endometriosis = ectopic implants **outside** the uterus (external endometriosis)

**PYQ — Definition:**

> Endometriosis is:
> - A. Endometrium within the myometrium
> - **B. Functional endometrium outside the uterus ← ANSWER**
> - C. Myometrium within the endometrium
> - D. Rare squamous variety of carcinoma endometrium

Option A describes adenomyosis. The word "functional" is key — the ectopic tissue responds to cyclical hormonal changes, bleeds monthly, and creates inflammation and fibrosis.

---

#### Sites — In Descending Frequency

1. Ovaries (most common — "chocolate cyst" / endometrioma)
2. Uterosacral ligaments
3. Pouch of Douglas (Rectovaginal septum)
4. Fallopian tubes
5. Bladder / ureter
6. Distant sites: lung (catamenial haemothorax), scar endometriosis (umbilicus, laparotomy scars)

---

#### Clinical Features — The Classic Triad

1. **Dysmenorrhoea** — severe, secondary, progressive (gets worse with each cycle)
2. **Dyspareunia** — deep, due to involvement of uterosacral ligaments and pouch of Douglas
3. **Infertility** — from adhesions, tubal blockage, altered peritoneal fluid environment

Other features: dyschezia (pain on defaecation), haematuria (bladder endo), cyclical haemoptysis (pulmonary endo), pelvic mass (endometrioma).

**Important:** Severity of symptoms does not correlate with stage. A woman with minimal-stage disease may have crippling dysmenorrhoea.

---

#### Diagnosis

| Investigation | Role | Notes |
|--------------|------|-------|
| **Laparoscopy + biopsy** | **Gold standard** | Powder-burn/blue-black spots, chocolate cysts, adhesions |
| Transvaginal USS | First-line | Identifies endometriomas (chocolate cysts) as homogeneous low-level echo mass |
| CA-125 | Elevated in ~80% | Elevated in many conditions (PID, pregnancy); NOT diagnostic; used for monitoring |
| MRI | Good for deep infiltrating endo | Rectovaginal, bladder involvement |

**Laparoscopy findings:** Powder-burn spots (blue-black deposits), red vesicles (early active lesions), white fibrotic plaques, chocolate cysts, "kissing ovaries," violin-string adhesions in advanced disease.

---

#### Management

**Medical management** (suppresses cyclical stimulation of ectopic tissue):

| Drug | Mechanism | Duration/Notes |
|------|-----------|---------------|
| **COCP** (continuous) | Suppress ovulation; anovulatory amenorrhoea | First-line for mild-moderate symptoms |
| **Progestins** (norethisterone, medroxyprogesterone) | Decidualisation of ectopic tissue | Injectable DMPA every 3 months |
| **GnRH agonists** (leuprolide, goserelin) | Surgical menopause; profound oestrogen suppression | Max 6 months (bone loss); add-back HRT if longer |
| **Danazol** | Androgenic; creates pseudomenopause | Androgenic side effects; rarely used now |
| **Gestrinone** | 19-nortestosterone derivative | **2.5–5 mg TWICE WEEKLY** × 6–9 months (not daily — common MCQ trap) |
| **LNG-IUS** (Mirena) | Local progestin; reduces dysmenorrhoea | Good for endometriosis-associated dysmenorrhoea |

**PYQ — False statement about gestrinone:**

> All are TRUE regarding medical treatment of endometriosis EXCEPT:
> - A. Oral progestins cause decidualisation of endometrial tissue
> - B. Injectable progestagen is used once every 3 months
> - C. LNG-IUS reduces dysmenorrhoea in endometriosis
> - **D. Gestrinone is given orally 2.5–5 mg per day ← ANSWER (FALSE)**

Gestrinone is given **twice weekly**, not daily. This is a consistent PYQ trap.

**Surgical management:**
- **Laparoscopy:** ablation/excision of implants, adhesiolysis, cystectomy (for endometrioma > 3 cm) — preferred when fertility desired
- **Laparotomy:** for extensive disease, bowel/bladder involvement
- **Definitive:** Hysterectomy + bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy — only for severe, completed family

**PYQ — Endometriosis failing GnRH, next step:**

> A 30-year-old with moderate-to-severe pelvic pain remains symptomatic after 3 months on leuprolide. Next step?
> - A. Increase GnRH agonist dose
> - B. Expectant management
> - **C. Diagnostic and operative laparoscopy ← ANSWER**
> - D. Add progestins

GnRH agonists are a bridge, not a cure. When symptoms persist or recur, laparoscopy is the next step — it confirms the diagnosis histologically and allows concurrent surgical treatment.

---

#### Adenomyosis — The Great Masquerader

Adenomyosis = endometrial glands > 2.5 mm deep into the myometrium.
- Uterus is **bulky, globular, and tender** (not irregular as in fibroids)
- Classic patient: 40-year-old multiparous woman with progressive dysmenorrhoea and menorrhagia
- **Diagnosis:** MRI (best non-invasive); definitive = histology on hysterectomy specimen
- **Treatment:** Hysterectomy is curative (GnRH agonist helps temporarily)

**PYQ — Adenomyosis:**

> A 30-year-old with dysmenorrhoea, dyspareunia, chronic pelvic pain. Hysterectomy specimen shows endometrial glands deep within myometrium. Diagnosis?
> - A. Endometriosis
> - **B. Adenomyosis ← ANSWER**
> - C. Leiomyoma
> - D. Endometrial polyp

Key: "within myometrium" = adenomyosis. Endometriosis is outside the uterus.

---

### PART 3: Ovarian Cysts

#### Functional vs. Neoplastic — First Distinction

**Functional cysts** (physiological, arise from follicular development):
- **Follicular cysts**: unruptured dominant follicle; thin-walled, unilocular, < 8 cm; resolves spontaneously in 4–8 weeks
- **Corpus luteum cyst**: from failed regression of corpus luteum; haemorrhagic; may cause pain if it bleeds
