Management of simple ovarian cyst in premenopausal woman:
- < 5 cm, unilocular, no septae/solid components → **conservative: repeat USS in 6 weeks**
- Persisting or > 5 cm → consider laparoscopic cystectomy

---

#### Dermoid Cyst (Mature Cystic Teratoma)

The single most important benign ovarian tumour to know:

**Key facts:**
- Most common ovarian tumour in **women under 30** and in **pregnancy**
- Contains elements from all three germ cell layers: hair, sebum, teeth, bone, neural tissue, thyroid
- **Rokitansky protuberance (dermoid plug):** solid protuberance projecting into the cyst lumen — the dense spot on USS/X-ray
- **USS:** hyperechoic nodule with acoustic shadowing; "tip of iceberg" sign
- **X-ray:** teeth visible (50% calcified components)
- Most common cause of **ovarian torsion** in young women (right side > left)
- **Malignant transformation:** < 2%; squamous cell carcinoma arising from dermoid (in older women with large, long-standing cysts)
- **Management:** Cystectomy (preserve ovary), ideally laparoscopic; spillage risk during surgery (chemical peritonitis from sebaceous material)

---

#### Chocolate Cyst (Endometrioma)

- Ovarian endometriosis → repeated cyclical bleeding inside the ovary → thick, dark brown "chocolate" fluid (old blood, haemosiderin)
- USS: homogeneous low-level echoes ("ground glass" appearance); thick-walled unilocular cyst
- CA-125 is elevated but not specific
- **Gold standard: laparoscopy + histology**
- **Management:**
  - Medical: GnRH agonists (temporary; cyst returns on stopping)
  - Surgical: Laparoscopic cystectomy (preferred for cysts > 3–4 cm, especially if symptomatic or fertility investigation planned)
  - Drainage alone = high recurrence rate

---

#### Ovarian Torsion

**PYQ:**

> Which side ovarian torsion is more common?
> - A. Left side
> - **B. Right side ← ANSWER**
> - C. Equal on both sides
> - D. Bilateral simultaneous

**Why right?** The sigmoid colon (left side of pelvis) acts as a physical barrier limiting left ovarian movement. The right ovary has more freedom to rotate. Also, in pregnant women, the right side is more affected due to uterine dextrorotation.

**Presentation:** Sudden-onset severe unilateral pelvic pain, nausea/vomiting; may be intermittent (partial/intermittent torsion). USS shows enlarged ovary, absence of blood flow on Doppler. **Management: laparoscopic detorsion** (untwist before adnexectomy; even if the ovary looks dusky, it is usually viable). Reserve salpingo-oophorectomy for clearly necrotic tissue.

---

### PART 4: Polycystic Ovary Syndrome (PCOS)

#### Diagnostic Criteria (Rotterdam 2003)

Diagnosis requires **2 of 3** criteria:
1. Oligo/anovulation (irregular cycles, amenorrhoea)
2. Clinical or biochemical hyperandrogenism (hirsutism, acne, elevated testosterone/DHEA-S)
3. Polycystic ovarian morphology on USS

**PYQ — NOT a Rotterdam criterion:**

> All of the following are Rotterdam criteria for diagnosing PCOS EXCEPT:
> - A. Hyperandrogenism (clinical or biochemical)
> - B. Oligomenorrhoea / anovulation
> - **C. Elevated oestrogen levels ← ANSWER**
> - D. Polycystic ovarian morphology on ultrasound

Oestrogen is NOT part of Rotterdam criteria. In PCOS, oestrone (not oestradiol) is elevated due to peripheral aromatisation of androgens, but oestrogen level is not a diagnostic criterion.

---

#### USG Findings in PCOS — "String of Pearls"

**PYQ:**

> Pearl necklace appearance on ultrasound is characteristic of:
> - A. Ectopic pregnancy
> - **B. PCOS ← ANSWER**
> - C. Endometriosis
> - D. PID

**Specific USS criteria (AES or Rotterdam):** At least one ovary with ≥ 12 follicles measuring 2–9 mm in diameter, arranged at the periphery ("pearl necklace" / "string of pearls"), and/or increased ovarian volume (> 10 mL). Increased ovarian stromal echogenicity.

Why not true cysts? These follicles have not undergone atresia due to androgen-mediated failure of apoptosis.

**PYQ — Cyst formation mechanism:**

> Cysts in polycystic ovarian syndrome are formed by:
> - **A. Failure of atretic follicles to undergo apoptosis ← ANSWER**
> - B. Oocyte proliferation
> - C. Multiple corpus lutea
> - D. Cystic degeneration of ovarian cortex

---

#### Hormonal Profile

| Hormone | PCOS Level | Explanation |
|---------|-----------|-------------|
| LH | **Elevated** | Increased pulse frequency from GnRH dysregulation |
| FSH | Normal or low | Relatively low compared to LH |
| LH/FSH ratio | **> 2:1** (classically > 3:1) | Characteristic but not in Rotterdam criteria |
| Androgens (Testosterone, DHEA-S) | **Elevated** | Theca cell overproduction under LH |
| Insulin | **Elevated** | 60–80% have insulin resistance |
| Prolactin | **Normal** (mild elevation occasionally) | Hyperprolactinaemia is a SEPARATE diagnosis |
| Oestradiol | Normal or slightly low | |
| Oestrone | Elevated | Peripheral aromatisation of androgens in adipose tissue |

**PYQ — False regarding hormone levels:**

> Which is FALSE regarding hormone levels in PCOS?
> - A. Increased androgen
> - **B. Increased prolactin ← ANSWER (FALSE)**
> - C. Increased LH
> - D. Increased LH/FSH ratio > 2:1

Prolactin is typically **normal** in PCOS. If prolactin is elevated, investigate separately for prolactinoma.

---

#### PCOS Management

**PYQ — Drug NOT used in PCOS:**

> Which of the following is NOT used in PCOS?
> - A. OC pills
> - B. Cyclical progestogens
> - C. Myoinositol
> - **D. Danazol ← ANSWER**

Danazol is an androgen; it would worsen the androgen excess in PCOS. It has no role here.

| Goal | Drug/Intervention |
|------|-----------------|
| Regulate cycles | OC pills (preferred), cyclical progestogens |
| Hirsutism / acne | COC + anti-androgen (spironolactone, cyproterone acetate) |
| Insulin resistance | **Metformin** (first-line insulin sensitiser); myoinositol also used |
| Ovulation induction for fertility | **Clomifene** (first-line); letrozole (emerging preferred); FSH injections if clomifene fails |
| Weight loss | 5–10% weight reduction restores ovulation in many cases |

---

### PART 5: AMH and Ovarian Reserve

**PYQ:**

> In low ovarian reserve, anti-Müllerian hormone (AMH) level is:
> - **A. < 1 ng/mL ← ANSWER**
> - B. 1–4 ng/mL
> - C. > 7 ng/mL
> - D. > 10 ng/mL

| AMH Level | Interpretation |
|-----------|---------------|
| < 1 ng/mL | Poor/diminished ovarian reserve |
| 1–3.5 ng/mL | Normal range |
| > 3.5–7 ng/mL | Good reserve |
| > 7 ng/mL | High reserve (PCOS, OHSS risk) |

AMH is secreted by granulosa cells of **preantral and small antral follicles**. It is **cycle-independent** (can be measured on any day of cycle) — this makes it superior to Day-3 FSH for ovarian reserve assessment.

**PYQ — AMH vs FSH:**

> AMH is a better marker of ovarian reserve than day-3 FSH because:
> - **A. AMH is cycle-independent ← ANSWER**
> - B. AMH is produced by corpus luteum
> - C. AMH is elevated in premature ovarian failure
> - D. AMH requires fasting for accurate measurement

---

## ⚠️ Bias Removal: Classic Confusions

### 1. Fibroid vs. Adenomyosis

| Feature | Fibroid | Adenomyosis |
|---------|---------|-------------|
| Uterine contour | **Irregular, nodular** | **Smooth, globular** |
| Uterine size | Variable enlargement | Symmetrically enlarged (like 8–10 week size) |
| Tenderness | Non-tender | **Tender** (especially pre-menstrually) |
| USS | Distinct focal masses, acoustic shadowing | Asymmetric myometrial thickening, loss of junctional zone |
| MRI | Well-circumscribed masses | Junctional zone > 12 mm |
| Diagnosis | Clinical + USS | Definitive on hysterectomy histology |
| Curative treatment | Myomectomy (if fertility desired) or hysterectomy | **Hysterectomy** (no myomectomy option) |

### 2. Endometriosis vs. PID — Differentiating Features

| Feature | Endometriosis | PID |
|---------|--------------|-----|
| Onset | Gradual, progressive | Acute |
| Fever | Absent | **Present** |
| Discharge | Absent | **Purulent** |
| Dysmenorrhoea | **Secondary, progressive** | Cyclic pain but not typical secondary dysmenorrhoea |
| CA-125 | Elevated | Also elevated (less helpful) |
| WBC | Normal | **Elevated** |
| Laparoscopy | Powder-burn implants, endometriomas | Hyperaemia, pyosalpinx, tubo-ovarian abscess |
| Treatment | Hormonal / laparoscopy | **Antibiotics** |

### 3. Dermoid (Teratoma) vs. Endometrioma

| Feature | Dermoid | Endometrioma |
|---------|---------|-------------|
| Age | Reproductive age, < 30 common | Reproductive age, 25–40 |
| USS | Hyperechoic with acoustic shadow; Rokitansky protuberance | Homogeneous low-level echoes ("ground glass") |
| Contents | Hair, teeth, sebum | Old blood (chocolate fluid) |
| CA-125 | Normal or mildly elevated | **Elevated** |
| Associated with | Torsion risk | Endometriosis, infertility |

---

## 🔄 Variations and Twists

### Scenario 1: Young Woman with Recurrent Miscarriage and Fibroid

A 28-year-old has had 3 first-trimester miscarriages. Hysterosalpingography shows a filling defect in the uterine cavity.

**Think:** Submucosal fibroid (or endometrial polyp, or septate uterus). The filling defect indicates an intracavitary pathology. Next step: hysteroscopy (diagnostic and therapeutic). If submucosal fibroid confirmed → **hysteroscopic myomectomy** → improves pregnancy outcomes.

### Scenario 2: Pelvic Pain Not Responding to NSAIDs

A 29-year-old has progressive dysmenorrhoea, deep dyspareunia, and subfertility for 2 years. USS shows a 4 cm left ovarian cyst with ground-glass echoes.

**Think:** Endometrioma. Next step: **laparoscopy** — confirms endometriosis, biopsies implants, performs ovarian cystectomy. If she wants fertility: conservative surgery (ovarian cystectomy, not ablation — lower recurrence).

### Scenario 3: Postmenopausal Woman with Uterine Enlargement

A 55-year-old presents with an irregular uterus of 12-week size. No bleeding.

**Think:** Fibroids post-menopause should regress (oestrogen withdrawal). If enlarging or new, consider **leiomyosarcoma** (very rare but important). Investigate with MRI; offer hysterectomy for histological diagnosis.

---

## 🧠 Memory Engineering

### Fibroid Degeneration (HMCRCS — "Hungry Monsters Can't Run Carefully, Sadly")
- **H**yaline (most common, 65%)
- **M**yxomatous
- **C**ystic
- **R**ed/Carneous (pregnancy — 2nd trimester)
- **C**alcification (postmenopausal)
- **S**arcomatous (< 0.1%, rare)

### PCOS Rotterdam Criteria — "HOT"
- **H**yperandrogenism (clinical or biochemical)
- **O**ligo/anovulation
- **T**ransabdominal or transvaginal USS showing polycystic ovaries

Need 2 of 3. Oestrogen is NOT in HOT.

### Dermoid Cyst Contents — "Hair To Skin"
- **H**air, teeth (ectoderm)
- **T**hyroid (endoderm — struma ovarii when > 50% thyroid tissue)
- **S**kin, sebaceous glands (ectoderm)

---

## ⚡ Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### Fibroid Management Decision Table

| Patient | Family Status | Symptom | Choice |
|---------|--------------|---------|--------|
| 25-year-old | Wants children | Menorrhagia, submucosal | Hysteroscopic myomectomy |
| 32-year-old | Wants children | Intramural 6 cm | Laparoscopic/open myomectomy |
| 40-year-old | Completed | Menorrhagia, multiple fibroids | Hysterectomy |
| 48-year-old | Completed | Perimenopausal, mild symptoms | GnRH agonist 3–6 months → observe → may reach menopause |
| Any age | — | Asymptomatic | Expectant management |
| Fibroid in pregnancy (red degeneration) | — | Pain, fever | Conservative (analgesics) |

### Tumour Markers Quick Reference

| Marker | Tumour |
|--------|--------|
| CA-125 | Epithelial ovarian cancer, endometriosis (non-specific) |
| AFP | Yolk sac (endodermal sinus) tumour |
| LDH + Placental ALP | **Dysgerminoma** |
| hCG | Choriocarcinoma, gestational trophoblastic disease |
| Inhibin | Granulosa cell tumour |
| CEA | Mucinous ovarian tumours |

**PYQ — Dysgerminoma marker:**

> In a case of dysgerminoma of the ovary, which tumour marker is most likely raised?
> - A. Serum HCG
> - B. Serum alpha-fetoprotein
> - **C. Serum LDH ← ANSWER**
> - D. Serum inhibin

### PCOS Hormone Summary

LH high, FSH relatively low, LH/FSH > 2, androgens high, insulin high, prolactin NORMAL, oestradiol normal/low, oestrone HIGH (peripheral aromatisation).

### Endometriosis: Medical Drugs and Key Details

| Drug | Key Fact |
|------|---------|
| GnRH agonist | Max 6 months; add-back HRT if longer; 30–50% fibroid shrinkage |
| Gestrinone | **Twice weekly** (not daily) |
| Danazol | Androgenic; rarely used; causes acne, hirsutism, voice changes |
| DMPA (depot medroxyprogesterone) | Every 3 months |
| LNG-IUS | Reduces dysmenorrhoea; local effect |

---

*Chapter 9 covers the highest-density benign gynaecological topics. Revisit the Decision Tables before exam day — scenario questions can be solved directly from these frameworks.*




---

# Chapter 10: Gynaecological Infections, Developmental Anomalies & Miscellaneous
### Müllerian Anomalies · Developmental Disorders · STDs · PID · Vaginal Physiology

---

## 🎯 Big Picture First

This chapter is a tale of two halves. The developmental anomaly half (Müllerian anomalies, imperforate hymen, AIS) is concept-dense and directly tested — examiners love the "most common anomaly" question, the "septate vs. bicornuate" management distinction, and the "primary amenorrhoea with cyclic pain" clinical scenario. The infections half (BV, trichomonas, gonorrhoea, chlamydia, PID) is classic association territory — every organism has a pathognomonic sign, every treatment has a key rule.

**The two unbreakable exam rules for this chapter:**
1. **Septate uterus** is the most common Müllerian anomaly AND the most common cause of recurrent pregnancy loss from uterine anomalies → treated by **hysteroscopic septoplasty**.
2. **Clue cells = BV (Gardnerella)**, **Strawberry cervix = Trichomonas**, **Gram-negative diplococci = Gonorrhoea** — never mix these.

---

## 📊 Question Pattern Map

| Trap | What Students Write | What Examiners Want |
|------|--------------------|--------------------|
| Most common Müllerian anomaly | Bicornuate uterus | **Septate uterus** |
| Septate uterus management | Laparotomy metroplasty | **Hysteroscopic septoplasty** |
| Bicornuate uterus management | "Same as septate" | **No surgery needed** (different embryology) |
| BV clue — wet mount | Flagellate organism | **Clue cells** (stippled epithelial cells) = Gardnerella |
| Trichomonas treatment | Vaginal clotrimazole | **Oral metronidazole** (both partners) |
| PID antibiotic | Single drug | Triple therapy: ceftriaxone + doxycycline + metronidazole |
| Fitz-Hugh-Curtis | Liver disease | **PID complication** (perihepatitis; violin string adhesions) |
| AIS karyotype | 46,XX | **46,XY** (male karyotype, female phenotype) |
| MRKH: ovarian function | Absent | **Ovaries normal** (46,XX; only uterus/upper vagina absent) |

---

## 📚 Core Concept Deep Dive

---

### PART 1: Müllerian Anomalies (Uterovaginal Anomalies)

#### Embryological Background

The uterus, cervix, fallopian tubes, and upper two-thirds of the vagina all develop from the **Müllerian (paramesonephric) ducts**. The two Müllerian ducts fuse in the midline in a caudal-to-cranial direction during weeks 8–12 of embryogenesis. The fused segment becomes the uterus and cervix; the midline septum must be resorbed. Failure of any of these steps produces the array of Müllerian anomalies.

**Classification (AFS/ASRM):**

| Class | Anomaly | Embryological Failure | Clinical Notes |
|-------|---------|----------------------|---------------|
| I | **Uterine agenesis/hypoplasia (MRKH)** | Müllerian duct aplasia | No uterus, no upper vagina; normal ovaries |
| II | **Unicornuate uterus** | One duct develops partially | Banana-shaped; 65% have rudimentary horn |
| III | **Uterus didelphys** | Failure of fusion | Two separate uteri and cervices; two vaginae |
| IV | **Bicornuate uterus** | Partial fusion failure | Two horns; single cervix; Y-shaped cavity |
| V | **Septate uterus** | Failure of septal resorption | **Most common anomaly**; single external uterus; internal septum |
| VI | **Arcuate uterus** | Near-complete resorption; minor indentation | Near-normal; minimal clinical significance |
| VII | DES-related anomalies | Exogenous drug exposure | T-shaped uterus |

---

#### Septate Uterus — The High-Yield Anomaly

**Why is it the most important?**
- Most common Müllerian anomaly overall
- Most common cause of recurrent pregnancy loss (RPL) from uterine anomalies
- Has a surgical cure: **hysteroscopic septoplasty (septum resection)**

**The septum is avascular fibrous tissue.** When an embryo implants on the septum, it gets poor blood supply → miscarriage (usually first trimester). The septum can be:
- **Complete:** extends to the cervical os
- **Partial (incomplete):** extends partway into the cavity

**Diagnosis:**
- Hysterosalpingography (HSG): shows divided cavity; cannot distinguish septate from bicornuate
- **MRI:** gold standard to distinguish septate vs. bicornuate (external uterine contour is key)
- 3D ultrasonography
- **Hysteroscopy + laparoscopy:** combined approach is definitive

**Management:**

**PYQ:**

> Septate uterus with recurrent pregnancy loss. Management?
> - A. Hysterectomy
> - B. Laparoscopy and metroplasty
> - **C. Hysteroscopy and septoplasty (resection) ← ANSWER**
> - D. Laparotomy and metroplasty

**PYQ:**

> A 24-year-old with recurrent abortions found to have a septate uterus. Which surgery has the best reproductive outcome?
> - A. Strassmann metroplasty
> - B. Tompkins metroplasty
> - **C. Transcervical hysteroscopic resection of septum ← ANSWER**
> - D. Jones metroplasty

Strassmann, Tompkins, and Jones are all **open metroplasty procedures** — they are now obsolete for septate uterus. Hysteroscopic resection is minimally invasive, does not require uterine incision (no weakened scar for future pregnancy), and has superior outcomes.

---

#### Bicornuate Uterus vs. Septate — The Critical Distinction

This is the most tested distinction in this chapter. Students consistently confuse these two.

| Feature | Septate Uterus | Bicornuate Uterus |
|---------|---------------|-------------------|
| Cause | Failure of septal **resorption** | Failure of ductal **fusion** |
| External uterine contour | **Normal** (fundus convex or flat) | **Concave fundal notch** (heart-shaped) |
| Internal division | Fibrovascular septum | Muscular septum (myometrium) |
| MRI/USS | Intact outer contour | External fundal indentation > 1 cm |
| Most common anomaly | **YES** | No |
| RPL association | **Strong** (avascular septum) | Less severe |
| Surgical treatment | **Hysteroscopic septoplasty** | Usually NOT treated surgically |
| Open surgery | Not indicated | Strassmann metroplasty (rarely done) |

**Key rule:** On HSG, both look like a divided cavity. MRI is needed to see the **external contour** — this distinguishes them. If the external uterine fundus is convex/flat = septate. If it has a cleft/notch = bicornuate.

---

#### Uterus Didelphys

**PYQ:**

> Uterus didelphys is NOT typically complicated by:
> - A. Recurrent abortion
> - B. Endometriosis
> - C. Preterm labour
> - **D. Transverse lie ← ANSWER (NOT a complication)**

Two completely separate uterine horns = each horn can accommodate a fetus in longitudinal lie. Transverse lie is NOT a specific complication. Recognised complications: recurrent miscarriage (small cavity), preterm labour, endometriosis (retrograde menstruation with two horns), ectopic pregnancy (in rudimentary horn).

---

#### Unicornuate Uterus and Rudimentary Horn

- Single Müllerian duct develops → banana-shaped uterus
- 65% have a contralateral **rudimentary horn**
- If the rudimentary horn has a functioning endometrium and is non-communicating → **haematometra** and cyclic pain
- **Risk:** ectopic pregnancy in the rudimentary horn — can rupture catastrophically in the 2nd trimester (presents like uterine rupture); management = **excision of the rudimentary horn**

---

### PART 2: Developmental Disorders

#### Imperforate Hymen

- Hymen fails to perforate at puberty → **obstructed outflow tract**
- **Clinical presentation:** Primary amenorrhoea + cyclic pelvic pain (cyclical haematocolpos)
- Examination: **bluish, bulging membrane** at the introitus
- USS/MRI: haematocolpos (blood-filled vagina), haematometra (blood in uterus)
- **Treatment: cruciate incision** (hymenotomy) — drainage of accumulated blood; excellent prognosis

**The classic scenario:** Adolescent girl with cyclical lower abdominal pain every month but no visible menstrual flow. Do not miss the bulging bluish membrane on examination.

---

#### MRKH Syndrome (Mayer-Rokitansky-Küster-Hauser)

- Müllerian aplasia → **absent uterus and upper vagina**
- Karyotype: **46,XX** (normal female)
- **Ovaries: normal and functional** → secondary sexual characteristics develop normally (unlike Turner's)
- Presentation: Primary amenorrhoea; no uterus on USS; normal female phenotype; normal breast development
- Associated: renal anomalies (horseshoe kidney, renal agenesis), skeletal anomalies
- Treatment: Progressive vaginal dilators (Frank's technique) to create a functional neovagina; surgical (McIndoe procedure) if dilators fail

**PYQ:**

> In which condition do the ovaries function normally?
> - A. Turner's syndrome
> - **B. Rokitansky-Küster-Hauser (MRKH) syndrome ← ANSWER**
> - C. Androgen insensitivity syndrome
> - D. Swyer's syndrome

Turner = streak gonads (dysgenetic); AIS = testes (not ovaries); Swyer = streak gonads (46,XY). MRKH is the only one with **normal functional ovaries** — hence normal pubertal development, normal FSH/LH, but no menstruation (no uterus).

---

#### Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome (AIS) — Complete (CAIS)

- Karyotype: **46,XY** (genetically male)
- Phenotype: **female** (complete female external genitalia)
- Cause: Defect/absence of androgen receptors → testosterone cannot act on external genitalia or Wolffian ducts → female phenotype develops by default
- **Testes:** present in inguinal canals or labia majora (produce testosterone and MIF/AMH)
- **MIF (Mullerian inhibiting factor):** still produced → Müllerian structures **absent** (no uterus, no tubes, no upper vagina)
- **Features:**
  - Primary amenorrhoea
  - Female phenotype with normal breast development (peripheral aromatisation of testosterone → oestrogen)
  - **Absent/sparse axillary and pubic hair** (no androgen effect on hair follicles)
  - **Blind-ending vagina** (short, no cervix)
  - **No uterus** on USS
- **Treatment:** Orchidectomy (gonads are testes; increased risk of malignant transformation — gonadoblastoma, seminoma) — usually after puberty is complete; HRT thereafter

**AIS vs. MRKH comparison:**

| Feature | AIS (Complete) | MRKH |
|---------|---------------|------|
| Karyotype | **46,XY** | 46,XX |
| Gonads | **Testes** (inguinal canal) | Normal ovaries (in pelvis) |
| Uterus | Absent (MIF from testes) | Absent (Müllerian aplasia) |
| Pubic/axillary hair | **Absent/sparse** | Normal |
| Breast development | Normal (oestrogen from aromatisation) | Normal |
| FSH/LH | Normal or elevated | Normal |

---

#### Virilisation of Female Fetus — Urogenital Sinus Concept

**PYQ:**

> Excess androgen exposure of a female fetus in early embryogenesis leads to virilisation of:
> - A. Müllerian ducts
> - B. Ovary
> - **C. Urogenital sinus ← ANSWER**
> - D. Mesonephric ducts

**Mechanism:** Androgens (from fetal adrenal CAH, maternal tumour, or exogenous) cause labioscrotal fusion and clitoral hypertrophy (urogenital sinus derivatives). The Müllerian-derived internal genitalia (uterus, tubes, upper vagina) develop normally because:
- Androgens don't suppress Müllerian development
- Wolffian ducts don't develop (no testosterone-mediated SRY pathway)

**Clinical result:** A 46,XX infant born with ambiguous genitalia, virilised external genitalia, but **normal uterus and ovaries** — typical of **congenital adrenal hyperplasia (21α-hydroxylase deficiency)**.

---

### PART 3: Gynaecological Infections

---

#### Bacterial Vaginosis (BV)

BV is not a true infection but a **dysbiosis** — replacement of normal Lactobacillus-dominant flora by anaerobes (Gardnerella vaginalis, Mobiluncus, Prevotella).

**Amsel Criteria — diagnose BV if 3 of 4 criteria present:**

| Criterion | Detail |
|-----------|--------|
| 1. Thin, greyish-white homogeneous discharge | Adherent to vaginal walls |
| 2. Vaginal pH > 4.5 | (Normal = 3.5–4.5) |
| 3. Positive whiff (amine) test | Add 10% KOH → fishy amine odour (amines released from anaerobes) |
| 4. Clue cells on wet mount | Vaginal squamous epithelial cells studded with bacteria (Gardnerella) |

**PYQ — Clue cells:**

> "Clue cells" on vaginal wet mount are pathognomonic of:
> - **A. Bacterial vaginosis ← ANSWER**
> - B. Vaginal candidiasis
> - C. Chlamydial vaginitis
> - D. Trichomoniasis

Clue cells = squamous cells with stippled/granular appearance due to Gardnerella bacteria coating the cell surface. Pathognomonic.

**PYQ — Organism and treatment:**

> A woman presents with fishy-smelling greyish discharge; pH 5.5, whiff test positive, clue cells on wet mount. Organism and drug?
> - A. Candida albicans — fluconazole
> - B. Trichomonas vaginalis — metronidazole
> - **C. Gardnerella vaginalis — metronidazole ← ANSWER**
> - D. Neisseria gonorrhoeae — doxycycline

**Treatment:** Metronidazole 500 mg BD × 7 days (oral) OR 0.75% vaginal gel × 5 days. Alternative: clindamycin 2% vaginal cream × 7 days. Partner treatment is **NOT** routine in BV (it is not primarily sexually transmitted, though it is associated with sexual activity).

**PYQ — Malodorous discharge cause:**

> Malodorous vaginal discharge is due to:
> - **A. Bacterial vaginosis ← ANSWER**
> - B. Chlamydia trachomatis
> - C. Trichomonas vaginalis
> - D. Neisseria gonorrhoeae

The amine odour in BV is from metabolic products of anaerobes, not from Gardnerella itself.

---

#### Trichomonas Vaginalis

- **Organism:** Trichomonas vaginalis (protozoan flagellate, sexually transmitted)
- **Symptoms:** Intense pruritus, frothy yellow-green discharge, vulvovaginal irritation, dysuria
- **Sign:** **Strawberry cervix** (colpitis macularis) — punctate haemorrhagic spots on cervix; visible on colposcopy in ~50%, naked eye in 10%
- **Vaginal pH:** > 4.5 (alkaline, unlike candida)
- **Wet mount:** motile flagellated trichomonads (pear-shaped, motile)

**PYQ — Strawberry cervix:**

> Strawberry cervix (punctate haemorrhages) is caused by:
> - **A. Trichomonas vaginalis ← ANSWER**
> - B. Gardnerella vaginalis
> - C. Neisseria gonorrhoeae
> - D. Candida albicans

**PYQ — Green frothy discharge:**

> Green frothy vaginal discharge is produced by:
> - A. Herpes simplex virus
> - B. Candida albicans
> - **C. Trichomonas vaginalis ← ANSWER**
> - D. Normal vaginal flora

**PYQ — True statement about Trichomonas:**

> Regarding Trichomonas vaginalis infection, which statement is TRUE?
> - A. Vaginal pH is typically < 4.5
> - **B. Male sexual partners must be treated simultaneously ← ANSWER**
> - C. Pruritus is usually absent
> - D. Treated by intravaginal clotrimazole

**Treatment:** Metronidazole 2 g single dose (oral) OR 500 mg BD × 7 days. **Both partners must be treated simultaneously** — Trichomonas is sexually transmitted and ping-pong reinfection occurs without partner treatment. Clotrimazole is antifungal — completely wrong for Trichomonas (this option appears in PYQs to trap students).

---

#### Vaginal Candidiasis

- **Organism:** Candida albicans (90%)
- **Risk factors:** Antibiotics, uncontrolled diabetes, pregnancy, immunosuppression
- **Symptoms:** Intense pruritus, thick white **"cottage cheese"** discharge, dysuria, vulval burning
- **pH:** < 4.5 (acidic — normal pH)
- **Wet mount (KOH prep):** pseudohyphae and budding yeast cells
- **Treatment:** Clotrimazole vaginal pessary (single dose or 3–7 days) OR oral fluconazole 150 mg single dose
- Partner treatment: NOT routine (not primarily STI)

---

#### STD Quick Reference Table

| Organism | Characteristic Finding | Treatment |
|----------|----------------------|-----------|
| **Gardnerella vaginalis** | Clue cells, fishy odour, grey discharge | Metronidazole |
| **Trichomonas vaginalis** | Strawberry cervix, frothy green discharge, motile flagellates | Metronidazole (both partners) |
| **Candida albicans** | Cottage cheese discharge, pseudohyphae on KOH | Clotrimazole / fluconazole |
| **Neisseria gonorrhoeae** | Gram-negative intracellular diplococci; urethritis, cervicitis | Ceftriaxone |
| **Chlamydia trachomatis** | Silent salpingitis → tubal infertility; intracellular; serovars D–K | Doxycycline / azithromycin |
| **HSV-2** | Painful genital ulcers, active lesions → LSCS | Aciclovir |
| **Treponema pallidum** | Primary: painless chancre; FTA-ABS specific test | Benzathine penicillin |

---

#### Chlamydia Trachomatis — The Silent Epidemic

**PYQ:**

> Chlamydia trachomatis infection commonly causes:
> - **A. Infertility ← ANSWER**
> - B. Post-coital bleeding
> - C. Amenorrhoea
> - D. Malignancy

Chlamydia causes **silent salpingitis** — most patients are asymptomatic. The tubal mucosa is progressively damaged → tubal scarring and peritubal adhesions → **tubal factor infertility**. It is the most common STI-related cause of infertility worldwide. It can also cause ectopic pregnancy (damaged ciliary function + scarring). The absence of symptoms means diagnosis is often delayed.

---

#### Pelvic Inflammatory Disease (PID)

**Definition:** Ascending infection from the lower genital tract (cervix/vagina) to the upper genital tract (endometrium, fallopian tubes, ovaries, peritoneum).

**Causative organisms:** Polymicrobial — Neisseria gonorrhoeae, Chlamydia trachomatis, and vaginal anaerobes. Both may be responsible.

**Diagnosis (CDC criteria — need all 3 minimum criteria):**
- Uterine tenderness
- Adnexal tenderness
- **Cervical motion tenderness (cervical excitation)** — the most specific sign

Additional features supporting diagnosis: fever > 38.3°C, mucopurulent cervical discharge, elevated CRP/ESR, elevated WBC, laparoscopy (gold standard confirmation).

**PYQ — Outpatient antibiotic regimen:**

> A 24-year-old sexually active woman diagnosed with PID, suitable for outpatient treatment. Appropriate regimen?
> - A. Azithromycin 1 g + metronidazole × 14 days
> - **B. Ceftriaxone 500 mg IM single dose + doxycycline 100 mg BD × 14 days + metronidazole 500 mg BD × 14 days ← ANSWER**
> - C. Levofloxacin 500 mg + metronidazole × 14 days
> - D. Amoxicillin-clavulanate + doxycycline × 14 days

**Rationale:**
- **Ceftriaxone:** covers Neisseria gonorrhoeae (3rd-gen cephalosporin)
- **Doxycycline:** covers Chlamydia trachomatis
- **Metronidazole:** covers anaerobes and BV-associated organisms

**Inpatient indications:** Tubo-ovarian abscess, surgical emergency cannot be excluded, failed outpatient treatment, pregnancy, severe illness (high fever, vomiting, peritoneal signs).

---

#### Fitz-Hugh-Curtis Syndrome (Perihepatitis)

**PYQ:**

> A 24-year-old sexually active woman presents with right upper quadrant pain, fever, and cervical motion tenderness. Laparoscopy shows "violin string" adhesions between the liver capsule and anterior abdominal wall. This is:
> - A. Acute cholecystitis
> - **B. Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome (perihepatitis complicating PID) ← ANSWER**
> - C. Hepatic abscess
> - D. Budd-Chiari syndrome

**Key features:**
- Complication of PID (Neisseria gonorrhoeae or Chlamydia reach the liver capsule via peritoneal spread or lymphatics)
- **Violin string adhesions** (fibrinous then fibrous bands) between liver capsule and anterior abdominal wall/diaphragm
- RUQ pain can mimic cholecystitis or pleurisy
- Confirmed on laparoscopy; mildly elevated LFTs
- **Treatment: same as PID** (antibiotics, NOT surgical)

---

#### Genital Herpes (HSV) in Labour

**PYQ:**

> A pregnant woman presents in labour with active genital ulcers likely due to HSV. Management?
> - **A. LSCS (Lower Segment Caesarean Section) ← ANSWER**
> - B. Classic Caesarean Section
> - C. Vaginal Delivery
> - D. Induction of Labour

Active genital herpes at onset of labour = indication for LSCS to prevent vertical transmission (neonatal herpes is a devastating infection). Vaginal delivery is only acceptable when there are no active lesions and no prodromal symptoms.

---

#### Tubercular Salpingitis — Special Points

**PYQ:**

> All of the following about tubercular salpingitis are correct EXCEPT:
> - A. Abdominal ostium may be patent with eversion of fimbriae
> - B. Early lesions may be confused with adenocarcinoma on histology
> - C. Genital TB is always secondary, with tubes as primary genital site
> - **D. Salpingitis isthmica nodosa is exclusive to tuberculosis ← ANSWER (FALSE)**

**Salpingitis isthmica nodosa (SIN)** = diverticular outpouching of tubal epithelium into the muscularis at the isthmus. It occurs in TB but is **not exclusive** to TB — also seen in gonorrhoeal and other chronic salpingitis. This is a consistent "NOT exclusive" trap.

Other truths about genital TB:
- Always secondary (primary site = lungs or lymph nodes; spreads haematogenously)
- Fallopian tubes are the **primary genital site** (100% of genital TB cases)
- "Tobacco pouch" appearance of tubes (fibrous, thickened fimbriae)
- Caseous granulomas on histology; early stages mimic adenocarcinoma
- **Asherman's syndrome** (intrauterine adhesions) is a known complication
- Treatment: standard 6-month anti-TB regimen (HRZE × 2 months → HR × 4 months)

---

### PART 4: Vaginal Physiology and Cervical Examination

#### Vaginal Changes in Pregnancy

**PYQ:**

> Vaginal changes during pregnancy include:
> - A. Decreased Lactobacilli
> - **B. Increased glycogen metabolism ← ANSWER**
> - C. pH > 6
> - D. Epithelial thinning

**Mechanism:** Oestrogen → **epithelial thickening** + glycogen accumulation in squamous cells → Lactobacilli metabolise glycogen → **lactic acid** → acidic pH (3.5–4.5) → protective against infection. In pregnancy, Lactobacilli **increase** (not decrease) because substrate (glycogen) is abundant. Vaginal pH remains acidic, not alkaline.

#### Eosinophilic (Karyopyknotic) Index

**PYQ:**

> The eosinophilic (karyopyknotic) index of lateral vaginal wall cells indicates:
> - A. Effect of LH
> - **B. Oestrogenic effect ← ANSWER**
> - C. Progesterone effect
> - D. Prolactin effect

The eosinophilic index measures the percentage of superficial vaginal cells with eosinophilic cytoplasm and pyknotic nuclei. This reflects **oestrogen stimulation** — oestrogen causes superficial cell maturation (pink, small nucleus). It peaks at ovulation and is used in vaginal cytology to assess hormonal status. Progesterone causes intermediate cell predominance (cyanophilic, folded edges).

#### Cervical Ectropion (Erosion)

**PYQ:**

> "Congenital" cervical erosion (ectropion) regresses after birth but may reappear at:
> - A. One year
> - B. Two years
> - C. Five years
> - **D. Puberty ← ANSWER**

Congenital ectropion: columnar epithelium extends onto the ectocervix (visible as red velvety area) due to maternal oestrogen stimulation. After birth, as maternal oestrogen declines, it regresses back behind the squamocolumnar junction. At **puberty**, rising oestrogens cause cervical eversion again. It also recurs with pregnancy and OCP use.

#### Physiological Leucorrhoea

**PYQ:**

> Normal physiological vaginal discharge (leucorrhoea) is caused by:
> - A. Cervical mucus + Bartholin gland secretions
> - **B. Transudation of plasma through vaginal epithelium ← ANSWER**
> - C. Endometrial secretions
> - D. Peritoneal fluid

Normal discharge = plasma transudation through vaginal epithelium + cervical mucus. Bartholin glands provide only minor secretions for lubrication at the introitus. Leucorrhoea increases at mid-cycle (oestrogen peak, abundant clear cervical mucus), in pregnancy, and with arousal.

---

## ⚠️ Bias Removal: Key Confusions

### 1. Septate vs. Bicornuate — Never Confuse Again

The external contour of the uterus distinguishes them:
- **Septate:** External = **normal** (round/flat fundus); Internal = divided by septum; treat with hysteroscopy
- **Bicornuate:** External = **cleft fundus** (heart-shaped); Internal = muscular separation; surgery generally NOT needed

HSG shows both as divided cavity — you cannot tell them apart on HSG. MRI shows the external contour. 3D USS also helpful.

The exam will give you an HSG or MRI finding + history of RPL and ask "management." If it says "septate" → hysteroscopic septoplasty. If it says "bicornuate" → NO surgery (Strassmann metroplasty is an option but not the expected answer unless specifically asked).

### 2. BV vs. Trichomonas — The Discharge Mixup

| Feature | BV | Trichomonas |
|---------|----|----|
| Discharge | Thin, grey, homogeneous | **Frothy**, yellow-green |
| Odour | **Fishy** (amine) | Mild odour |
| pH | > 4.5 | > 4.5 |
| Wet mount | **Clue cells** | **Motile flagellates** |
| Cervix | Normal | **Strawberry cervix** |
| Treatment | Metronidazole (not partner) | Metronidazole (**both partners**) |
| STI? | Associated but not classic STI | **Yes, STI** |

### 3. AIS vs. MRKH — Same Presentation, Different Cause

Both present with **primary amenorrhoea and absent uterus**. The critical distinguishing features:

| AIS | MRKH |
|-----|------|
| 46,XY | 46,XX |
| Testes in inguinal canal | Normal ovaries in pelvis |
| Absent/sparse pubic and axillary hair | Normal hair |
| Short blind vagina | Short or absent vagina |
| Normal breast development | Normal breast development |
| High testosterone (but no effect) | Normal female hormone levels |

---

## 🔄 Variations and Twists

### Scenario 1: Primary Amenorrhoea with Cyclic Pelvic Pain

A 16-year-old presents with crampy lower abdominal pain every month for 6 months, no menstrual bleeding. Examination shows a bulging bluish membrane at the introitus.

**Diagnosis: Imperforate hymen with haematocolpos**

Management: Cruciate incision (hymenotomy) under anaesthesia. Pre-operative USS/MRI confirms haematocolpos and haematometra. Excellent prognosis if managed early (before secondary tubal damage from retrograde blood flow).

**Do not mistake this for** a vaginal septum (no visible bulging membrane) or MRKH (no cyclic pain — no uterus to cause pain).

### Scenario 2: Recurrent Pregnancy Loss — Investigation Pathway

A 27-year-old has had 3 first-trimester pregnancy losses. Investigations show normal parental karyotype, normal thrombophilia screen, normal hormones.

**Next investigation: 3D USS or HSG to assess uterine cavity**

If a filling defect is found → **hysteroscopy** (diagnostic and therapeutic). If septate uterus found → **hysteroscopic septoplasty**.

**Complete work-up for RPL:**
1. Parental karyotypes (chromosomal cause, 5%)
2. Antiphospholipid syndrome (lupus anticoagulant + anti-cardiolipin antibodies — dRVVT test)
3. Uterine anatomy (3D USS, HSG, hysteroscopy)
4. Thyroid function, diabetes screen
5. Thrombophilia screen (factor V Leiden, protein C/S)

**PYQ — Antiphospholipid syndrome test:**

> A 26-year-old with recurrent abortions. Which investigation confirms antiphospholipid syndrome?
> - A. PT
> - B. BT
> - **C. Anti-Russell viper venom (anti-RVV) antibodies ← ANSWER**
> - D. Clot solubility test

Lupus anticoagulant is detected by the **dilute Russell viper venom time (dRVVT)**. Together with anti-cardiolipin antibodies, it confirms APS.

### Scenario 3: Right Upper Quadrant Pain in a Young Sexually Active Woman

A 22-year-old with RUQ pain, mild fever, mildly elevated ALT, and pelvic pain. On pelvic examination: cervical motion tenderness.

**Think Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome.** The combination of RUQ pain + cervical motion tenderness is classic. Laparoscopy would show "violin string" adhesions. Treat as PID.

---

## 🧠 Memory Engineering

### Müllerian Anomaly Classification — "Absent / Uni / Didel / Bi / Sep / Arc"

| Number | Memory | Anomaly |
|--------|--------|---------|
| I | **A**bsent | MRKH (agenesis) |
| II | **Uni**corn | Unicornuate |
| III | **Di**del | Didelphys |
| IV | **Bi**corn | Bicornuate |
| V | **Sep**tate | Septate (most common) |
| VI | **Arc**uate | Arcuate (near-normal) |

### Amsel Criteria for BV — "PCDW"
- **P**H > 4.5
- **C**lue cells on wet mount
- **D**ischarge (thin, grey, homogeneous)
- **W**hiff test positive (KOH → fishy amine)

3 of 4 = BV diagnosed.

### STD Pathognomonic Signs — "Clue-Straw-Gram"
- **Clue** cells = BV (Gardnerella)
- **Straw**berry cervix = Trichomonas
- **Gram**-negative diplococci = Gonorrhoea
- **Pseudo**hyphae = Candida
- **Schiller-Duval** bodies = Yolk sac tumour (not STD — bonus memory)

### AIS Memory — "X-Y, No Y-es" 
AIS = 46,**XY** karyotype, **No** uterus, **No** axillary/pubic hair, **Yes** breast development (aromatisation), **Yes** testes (in inguinal canal).

---

## ⚡ Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### Müllerian Anomaly Summary Table

| Anomaly | Most Common | RPL Risk | Surgery | Special Feature |
|---------|------------|---------|---------|----------------|
| Septate | **YES** | **Highest** | **Hysteroscopic septoplasty** | External contour normal |
| Bicornuate | — | Moderate | Not usually | Fundal cleft on MRI |
| Didelphys | — | Moderate | Not usually | Two cervices, two vaginae |
| Unicornuate | — | Moderate | Rudimentary horn excision | Risk of ectopic in horn |
| MRKH | — | — | Vaginal dilators (Frank's) | Normal ovaries; 46,XX |
| Arcuate | — | Minimal | None | Minor fundal indentation |

### Vaginal Discharge Differential — One-Glance Table

| Feature | BV | Trichomonas | Candida | Normal |
|---------|----|----|--------|--------|
| Colour | Grey-white | Yellow-green | White "cheesy" | Clear/white |
| Consistency | Thin, homogeneous | Frothy | Thick, curdy | Variable |
| Odour | **Fishy** | Mild | None | None |
| pH | > 4.5 | > 4.5 | < 4.5 | 3.5–4.5 |
| Wet mount | Clue cells | Motile flagellates | Pseudohyphae | Lactobacilli |
| Itch | Mild | **Severe** | **Severe** | None |
| Cervix | Normal | Strawberry | Normal | Normal |
| Treatment | Metronidazole | Metronidazole (partners) | Clotrimazole/fluconazole | — |

### PID Management Summary

| Severity | Setting | Regimen |
|----------|---------|---------|
| Mild-moderate | **Outpatient** | Ceftriaxone 500 mg IM × 1 dose + Doxycycline 100 mg BD × 14d + Metronidazole 500 mg BD × 14d |
| Severe / TOA | **Inpatient IV** | Cefoxitin 2 g IV 6-hourly OR clindamycin + gentamicin; switch to oral after 24h improvement |
| Both partners | — | Screen and treat male partner for gonorrhoea/chlamydia |

### Developmental Anomaly — Primary Amenorrhoea Decision

| Features | Diagnosis |
|----------|-----------|
| Cyclic pain + bulging blue membrane | **Imperforate hymen** |
| No pain + absent uterus on USS + 46,XX + normal ovaries | **MRKH** |
| Female phenotype + absent uterus + no pubic hair + 46,XY | **AIS (CAIS)** |
| Short stature + webbed neck + streak gonads + 45,XO | **Turner syndrome** |
| Female body + no secondary sex characteristics + delayed bone age | Primary ovarian insufficiency / hypogonadotrophic hypogonadism |

---

*The infectious/anomaly pairing in this chapter looks broad, but the exam tests the same 6–8 specific facts repeatedly. Mastering the tables in the Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer and the bias-removal section will cover the vast majority of MCQs on these topics.*




---

# Chapter 11: Cervical Cancer & Endometrial Cancer

---

## Big Picture First

Gynaecological oncology is the single heaviest exam topic in OBG — expect 8–12 marks across cervical, endometrial, ovarian, and GTD questions combined. Cervical cancer is the **number one female cancer in India** (second globally after breast cancer). Examiners love three things: (1) FIGO 2018 staging questions — especially the hydronephrosis trap, (2) Pap smear / Bethesda system interpretation, and (3) treatment selection by stage. Endometrial cancer questions focus on Type 1 vs Type 2 distinction, hyperplasia risk table, and Lynch syndrome. Both topics are highly rewarding per hour of study because the same facts appear year after year.

---

## Question Pattern Map — What Examiners Actually Ask

**Cervical cancer traps (appearing in multiple NEET-PG years):**
- Hydronephrosis = Stage **IIIB** NOT IVA (IVA = bladder/rectal invasion — a different finding entirely)
- FIGO 2018 changed the parametrium rule: Stage IIB now includes any parametrial extension; Stage IIIC added pelvic and para-aortic lymph node metastases (IIIC1 = pelvic LN, IIIC2 = para-aortic LN) — old question banks may use pre-2018 staging
- Microinvasion dimensions: IA1 = depth ≤ 3 mm; IA2 = depth > 3 mm and ≤ 5 mm (width ≤ 7 mm, now removed in 2018)
- Wertheim's hysterectomy is for Stage IB1 to IIA — never for Stage IIB or beyond
- Point A (brachytherapy) = 2 cm above external os AND 2 cm lateral to uterine canal (NOT 5 cm — classic distractors use wrong lateral distance)
- HPV 16 = most common in squamous cell carcinoma; HPV 18 = more associated with adenocarcinoma
- Squamous cell carcinoma = 70–80% of cervical cancers; adenocarcinoma = 20–25% (rising)

**Endometrial cancer traps:**
- Type 2 endometrial cancer arises on atrophic endometrium, NOT on hyperplastic endometrium — the inverse of what you might expect
- Tamoxifen for breast cancer → oestrogen agonist on uterus → increased endometrial cancer risk
- Lynch syndrome (HNPCC) = endometrial cancer is the SENTINEL cancer in women (not colon — colon comes first in men; in women, endometrial cancer may precede colon cancer)
- Progestin is both the treatment for hyperplasia AND the protective factor against endometrial cancer from unopposed oestrogen
- EIN (Endometrial Intraepithelial Neoplasia) = old terminology "complex atypical hyperplasia" = highest risk of progression (~29%)

---

## Core Concept Deep Dive

### 11.1 HPV and Cervical Carcinogenesis

Human papillomavirus is a double-stranded DNA virus transmitted sexually. Of 200+ HPV types, ~14 are classified as high-risk (oncogenic).

**Key HPV types to memorise:**

| HPV Type | Association |
|---|---|
| **16** | Squamous cell carcinoma of cervix (40–70%) |
| **18** | Adenocarcinoma of cervix; aggressive SCC |
| **6, 11** | Condyloma acuminata (genital warts) — LOW-RISK |
| **31, 33, 45** | Also oncogenic, less commonly tested |

The oncogenic mechanism: E6 protein of HPV 16/18 degrades p53 (tumour suppressor); E7 protein inactivates pRb (retinoblastoma protein). Both drive uncontrolled cell proliferation. The transformation zone (squamocolumnar junction) of the cervix is the vulnerable site — columnar cells undergoing squamous metaplasia here are most susceptible to HPV integration.

**HPV Vaccines — the exam now asks about schedule:**

- **Cervarix (bivalent):** covers HPV 16 + 18 (oncogenic only)
- **Gardasil 4 (quadrivalent):** covers HPV 6, 11, 16, 18 (warts + cancer)
- **Gardasil 9 (nonavalent):** covers 9 types including 31, 33, 45, 52, 58

**Vaccination schedule (WHO / India MoHFW):**
- Age 9–14 years: **2 doses**, 6 months apart
- Age ≥ 15 years or immunocompromised: **3 doses** (0, 1–2, 6 months)
- Note: WHO now endorses single-dose efficacy for routine national programmes — this is tested in recent NEET-PG questions

---

### Exam Question — HPV Type (NEET-PG 2018-style)

**Q116 — HPV type most commonly associated with carcinoma cervix:**

- A. HPV 35
- B. HPV 33
- C. HPV 18
- D. HPV 16

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. HPV 16** — HPV 16 causes 40–70% of invasive squamous cell carcinomas of the cervix. HPV 18 is more strongly linked to adenocarcinoma and accounts for an additional 10–15% of all cervical cancers. Together, HPV 16 + 18 account for approximately 70% of all cervical cancers — which is why the bivalent vaccine offers significant protection. Options A (HPV 35) and B (HPV 33) are high-risk types but far less common.

</details>

---

### 11.2 Risk Factors for Cervical Cancer

**Risk factors (memory: "SIMPLE"):**
- **S** — Sexual activity: early age of first intercourse (< 16 years), multiple partners, partner with multiple partners
- **I** — Immunosuppression (HIV, transplant patients)
- **M** — Multiple pregnancies (multiparity)
- **P** — Partner: high-risk male (history of penile cancer, multiple partners)
- **L** — Long-term OCP use (slight increase in adenocarcinoma risk)
- **E** — Environment: **smoking** (doubles the risk), low socioeconomic status, HPV exposure

**NOT risk factors:** Late menarche, nulliparity, obesity (these are endometrial cancer risk factors — the examiner deliberately places them in cervical cancer options to trap you).

---

### Exam Question — Risk Factors (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q126 — Risk factors for carcinoma cervix include: (a) HPV (b) Smoking (c) Late menarche (d) Nulliparity (e) Early sexual intercourse**

- A. a, b, e
- B. b, c, d
- C. c, d, e
- D. a, c, d

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. a, b, e (HPV, Smoking, Early sexual intercourse)** — These are the three established major risk factors. Late menarche (option c) is actually a PROTECTIVE factor for endometrial cancer (fewer anovulatory cycles, less oestrogen exposure). Nulliparity (option d) is a risk factor for endometrial cancer, not cervical cancer — in fact, high parity increases cervical cancer risk, while nulliparity does not. The examiner specifically places "late menarche" and "nulliparity" here because candidates confuse cervical and endometrial risk factor profiles.

</details>

---

### 11.3 Cervical Cancer Screening — The Full Picture

**Pap Smear (Papanicolaou Smear):**
- Collects exfoliated cells from the transformation zone (using Ayre's spatula + endocervical brush or combined brush)
- Interpreted using the **Bethesda System (2001):**

| Bethesda Classification | Old CIN Terminology | Clinical Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| NILM | Normal | No lesion |
| ASC-US | Atypical cells, undetermined significance | Most common abnormal result; reflex HPV testing |
| ASC-H | Atypical cells, cannot exclude HSIL | Urgent colposcopy |
| **LSIL** | **CIN I + HPV effect (koilocytosis)** | Low-grade; often regresses spontaneously |
| **HSIL** | **CIN II + CIN III** | High-grade; immediate colposcopy + biopsy |
| AGC | Atypical glandular cells | Investigate for endocervical/endometrial pathology |
| AIS | Adenocarcinoma in situ | Cone biopsy required |
| Carcinoma | Invasive cancer | Urgent referral |

**Key Bethesda fact for NEET-PG:** LSIL = CIN I; HSIL = CIN II + CIN III. This is tested almost every year.

**Koilocytes** = hallmark cytological feature of HPV infection: cells with perinuclear clearing (halo) and wrinkled/raisinoid nucleus. Their presence = active HPV cytopathic effect.

**CIN Grading (histological):**
- **CIN I (mild dysplasia):** Dysplastic cells confined to lower 1/3 of epithelium; high rate of spontaneous regression (~60%)
- **CIN II (moderate dysplasia):** Dysplasia in lower 2/3; intermediate behaviour
- **CIN III (severe dysplasia / Carcinoma in situ):** Full-thickness or near-full-thickness dysplasia; ~30% progress to invasive cancer if untreated

---

### Exam Question — Bethesda System (NEET-PG 2018-style)

**Q41 — LSIL in the Bethesda classification corresponds to:**

- A. CIN I (mild dysplasia)
- B. CIN II (moderate dysplasia)
- C. CIN III (severe dysplasia/CIS)
- D. Squamous metaplasia

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. CIN I** — Bethesda 2001: LSIL = CIN I + HPV cytopathic effect (koilocytosis). HSIL encompasses both CIN II and CIN III. Option D (squamous metaplasia) is a physiological process, not a dysplastic lesion — it is the normal process at the transformation zone where columnar cells are replaced by squamous cells, and it appears pink/mature on cytology, not dysplastic. ASC-US is the single most common abnormal Pap result (not LSIL — another frequent confusion).

</details>

---

**VIA (Visual Inspection with Acetic Acid):**
- Acetic acid 3–5% applied to cervix; acetowhite lesions = positive
- Detects high-grade CIN (CIN II/III) by causing temporary opacity in dysplastic cells due to protein coagulation
- Acetowhite lesions at the transformation zone are colposcopically suspicious
- Sensitivity ~79%, specificity ~85% for CIN II+
- Used in screen-and-treat programmes in low-resource settings (India's national programme)

**VILI (Visual Inspection with Lugol's Iodine / Schiller's Test):**
- Normal glycogen-rich squamous cells stain mahogany brown (Schiller-positive = iodine-positive = normal)
- Dysplastic cells lack glycogen → **iodine-negative (Schiller-negative)** = abnormal area = positive VILI test
- Note the confusing double-negative: Schiller-negative means abnormal. "Schiller-negative" lesion = suspicious.
- False positives (Schiller-negative but benign): columnar epithelium, metaplastic areas, atrophic epithelium, erosions

**Colposcopy:**
- Magnified visualisation of cervix after acetic acid application
- Colposcopic features of high-grade CIN: acetowhite epithelium with coarse punctuation, mosaic pattern, atypical vessels, abnormal transformation zone
- Colposcopy-directed (punch) biopsy is the diagnostic gold standard for confirming CIN grade before treatment
- Step in management: Abnormal Pap → Colposcopy → Directed biopsy → Histological confirmation → Treatment

---

### Exam Question — Next Step after Abnormal Pap (NEET-PG 2018)

**Q175 — A 45-year-old woman has post-coital bleeding and a positive Pap smear. Next line of management?**

- A. Colposcopy-directed biopsy
- B. Cone biopsy
- C. Repeat Pap smear
- D. Hysterectomy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Colposcopy-directed biopsy** — A positive Pap smear (abnormal cytology) requires HISTOLOGICAL confirmation before any treatment decision. Colposcopy identifies the most abnormal-appearing area; directed (punch) biopsy provides the tissue. Option B (cone biopsy) is both diagnostic AND therapeutic but is reserved for: (1) unsatisfactory colposcopy (transformation zone not fully visible), (2) suspected microinvasive disease, (3) positive endocervical curettage. Option C (repeat Pap) only wastes time when a positive result is already in hand. Option D (hysterectomy) without histological staging would be inappropriate — you cannot stage or select treatment without biopsy confirmation.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — CIN on Pap Smear (NEET-PG 2020-style)

**Q691 — PAP smear shows carcinoma-in-situ (CIN III / HSIL). Best next logical procedure:**

- A. Cold-knife conisation
- B. Colposcopy + directed biopsy
- C. Hysterectomy
- D. HPV viral DNA typing

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Colposcopy + directed biopsy** — Even for CIN III on cytology, histological confirmation is mandatory before definitive treatment. Colposcopy will confirm the lesion; directed biopsy will provide the tissue diagnosis. Cold-knife conisation (option A) is used when colposcopy is unsatisfactory or microinvasion is suspected — not as the first step after an abnormal Pap. Hysterectomy (option C) is never done based on cytology alone without histology. HPV typing (option D) adds no management value when HSIL is already demonstrated.

</details>

---

### 11.4 Treatment of CIN

| Grade | Treatment |
|---|---|
| CIN I | Observe (regression in 60%); treat at 24 months if persistent |
| CIN II | LLETZ / LEEP (loop excision); cryotherapy in resource-limited settings |
| CIN III | LLETZ / LEEP as first line; cold-knife cone if lesion extends to endocervical canal |
| CIN III + pregnancy | Defer treatment until after delivery; re-biopsy at 6 weeks postpartum |

**LLETZ (Large Loop Excision of the Transformation Zone) = LEEP in Indian terminology:**
- Uses low-voltage diathermy loop to excise the transformation zone
- Advantages: quick, outpatient, provides specimen for histology (unlike ablative methods)
- Loop size < 2 cm gives a better specimen with less thermal artefact at margins
- Faster than laser ablation

---

### Exam Question — LLETZ False Statement

**Q436 — Which is NOT true regarding LLETZ?**

- A. Uses low-voltage diathermy
- B. Applicable only to the cervix
- C. Loop size > 2 cm gives a better cone specimen
- D. Shorter procedure time than laser ablation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Loop size > 2 cm gives a better cone specimen** — FALSE. A loop size LESS THAN 2 cm provides a better, more precise specimen with minimal thermal damage at the margins. The larger the loop, the greater the peripheral thermal artefact, which can compromise histological assessment of resection margins. All other options are true: LLETZ uses low-voltage diathermy (A), is cervix-specific (B), and is indeed faster than laser ablation (D) — making LLETZ the preferred modality in most settings.

</details>

---

### 11.5 FIGO 2018 Cervical Cancer Staging

FIGO 2018 introduced two major changes from the older system: (1) imaging and pathological findings can now be used to stage (previously clinical examination only), and (2) lymph node involvement is now included as Stage IIIC.

**Complete FIGO 2018 Staging Table:**

| Stage | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Stage I** | Tumour confined to cervix |
| IA | Microscopic invasion only (diagnosed by microscopy) |
| IA1 | Stromal invasion ≤ 3.0 mm |
| IA2 | Stromal invasion > 3.0 mm and ≤ 5.0 mm |
| IB | Clinically visible or microscopic lesion > Stage IA |
| IB1 | Tumour ≤ 2 cm (previously ≤ 4 cm before 2018) |
| IB2 | Tumour > 2 cm and ≤ 4 cm |
| IB3 | Tumour > 4 cm |
| **Stage II** | Beyond cervix but not to pelvic wall / lower 1/3 vagina |
| IIA | No parametrial involvement |
| IIA1 | Tumour ≤ 4 cm |
| IIA2 | Tumour > 4 cm |
| **IIB** | **Parametrial involvement (any)** |
| **Stage III** | Tumour to pelvic wall / lower 1/3 vagina / hydronephrosis / LN mets |
| IIIA | Lower 1/3 vaginal involvement (no pelvic wall) |
| **IIIB** | **Extension to pelvic wall AND/OR hydronephrosis / non-functioning kidney** |
| **IIIC1** | **Pelvic lymph node metastasis** (NEW in FIGO 2018) |
| **IIIC2** | **Para-aortic lymph node metastasis** (NEW in FIGO 2018) |
| **Stage IV** | Spread beyond true pelvis / involvement of bladder or rectum |
| **IVA** | **Invasion of bladder or rectal mucosa** |
| IVB | Distant metastasis (lung, liver, bone) |

**Visual Staircase Memory Aid:**

```
Stage I   → Cervix only
Stage II  → Beyond cervix (IIA = vagina upper 2/3; IIB = parametrium)
Stage III → Pelvic wall / lower vagina / HYDRONEPHROSIS(IIIB) / LYMPH NODES(IIIC)
Stage IV  → BLADDER/RECTUM (IVA) / Distant (IVB)
```

**The Two Biggest NEET-PG Traps:**

**Trap 1 — Hydronephrosis:** "Cervical cancer patient found to have hydronephrosis on IVP" = Stage **IIIB**, not IVA. IVA = bladder/rectal *mucosal* invasion (confirmed on cystoscopy/sigmoidoscopy, not just displacement). Hydronephrosis = ureteric obstruction by parametrial tumour = Stage IIIB.

**Trap 2 — Bilateral hydronephrosis:** Still Stage IIIB. The staging does not jump to Stage IV just because both kidneys are involved.

---

### Exam Question — Hydronephrosis Staging (NEET-PG multiple years)

**Q17 — Cervical cancer with hydronephrosis (non-functional kidney). FIGO stage?**

- A. IIA
- B. IIIA
- C. IIIB
- D. IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. IIIB** — FIGO 2018 (and even the older FIGO 2009): hydronephrosis or non-functioning kidney caused by tumour extension = Stage IIIB, regardless of primary tumour size or how mild the parametrial extension appears on examination. This is one of the highest-frequency staging traps on NEET-PG. Option A (IIA) = upper vaginal extension without parametrial involvement; Option B (IIIA) = lower 1/3 vaginal involvement; Option D (IV) requires either bladder/rectal mucosal invasion (IVA) or distant metastasis (IVB). Hydronephrosis = Stage IIIB.

</details>

---

### 11.6 Treatment of Cervical Cancer by Stage

| Stage | Treatment of Choice |
|---|---|
| IA1 (no LVSI) | Conisation (fertility-sparing) or simple hysterectomy |
| IA1 (with LVSI) | Radical hysterectomy + pelvic LND, OR chemoradiation |
| IA2 | Modified radical hysterectomy (Wertheim's Type II) + pelvic LND |
| IB1 | Radical hysterectomy (Wertheim's Type III) + pelvic LND, OR chemoradiation |
| IB2–IIA | Radical hysterectomy OR concurrent chemoradiation (equivalent outcomes) |
| **IIB and beyond** | **Concurrent chemoradiation (cisplatin-based) + brachytherapy** |
| IVB | Palliative chemotherapy ± palliative RT |

**Wertheim's Hysterectomy:** Radical (type III) hysterectomy removing uterus, upper 2–3 cm of vagina, parametrium to pelvic wall, and pelvic lymph nodes. Used for stages IA2 to IIA. **NOT** used for stage IIB or beyond (too much local disease for resection with clear margins).

**Schauta's Operation:** Radical vaginal hysterectomy (equivalent to Wertheim's but vaginal approach). Rarely used but may appear in older question banks.

**Chemoradiation:**
- External beam radiotherapy (EBRT) to pelvis + concurrent cisplatin (weekly, 40 mg/m²) + intracavitary brachytherapy (ICB)
- Standard for Stage IIB, III, IVA
- **Point A** (brachytherapy reference point): 2 cm above external os AND **2 cm** lateral to uterine canal = receives ~7000–8500 cGy total
- **Point B**: 3 cm lateral to Point A (5 cm from midline) = receives ~5000–6000 cGy (covers pelvic lymph nodes)
- Note: Point A is 2 cm lateral, NOT 5 cm lateral — the 5 cm figure is the total distance of Point B from midline

---

### Exam Question — Stage IIB Treatment (NEET-PG 2023)

**Q245 — A 50-year-old woman with foul-smelling bloody discharge and lateral parametrium involvement. Management?**

- A. Chemotherapy
- B. Brachytherapy
- C. Chemoradiation
- D. Surgery

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Chemoradiation** — Parametrial involvement = Stage IIB cervical cancer. Standard treatment is concurrent cisplatin-based chemoradiation (external beam RT + weekly cisplatin + intracavitary brachytherapy). Option A (chemotherapy alone) is never used as definitive treatment for localised cervical cancer. Option B (brachytherapy alone) is inadequate as primary therapy without EBRT for a stage IIB lesion. Option D (surgery — Wertheim's hysterectomy) is appropriate only up to Stage IIA; Stage IIB has parametrial involvement that cannot be cleared surgically with adequate margins.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Stage IIIB Treatment (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q127 — Treatment for stage IIIB carcinoma cervix:**

- A. Wertheim's hysterectomy
- B. Schauta's procedure
- C. Chemotherapy alone
- D. Intracavitary brachytherapy + external beam RT (with concurrent chemotherapy)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Brachytherapy + external beam RT (concurrent chemoradiation)** — Stage IIIB (pelvic wall extension or hydronephrosis) is not surgically resectable. Standard of care: EBRT (45–50 Gy to the pelvis) + concurrent weekly cisplatin + intracavitary brachytherapy (boost to Point A). Options A and B are surgical operations appropriate only up to Stage IIA. Chemotherapy alone (option C) has insufficient local control for Stage III disease.

</details>

---

### 11.7 Endometrial Cancer — Type 1 vs Type 2

Endometrial carcinoma is the most common gynaecological malignancy in developed countries (in India, cervical cancer is commoner). Understanding the fundamental duality of Type 1 vs Type 2 is the foundation of exam performance.

**Type 1 vs Type 2 Endometrial Cancer:**

| Feature | Type 1 (Endometrioid) | Type 2 (Serous/Clear Cell) |
|---|---|---|
| Frequency | ~80% | ~15–20% |
| Histology | Endometrioid adenocarcinoma | Serous or clear cell carcinoma |
| Oestrogen | Oestrogen-DEPENDENT | Oestrogen-INDEPENDENT |
| Background | Endometrial hyperplasia (EIN) | Atrophic endometrium |
| Typical patient | Obese, perimenopausal, younger | Thin, older, postmenopausal |
| Grade | Low-grade (G1, G2) | High-grade (G3) |
| Prognosis | Good (5-yr survival ~85%) | Poor (aggressive, early metastasis) |
| Molecular | PTEN, microsatellite instability, KRAS | TP53 mutation, HER2 amplification |
| Lynch syndrome | Associated (MLH1, MSH2 mismatch repair) | Not associated |

---

### Exam Question — Type 1 vs Type 2 (NEET-PG repeated question)

**Q19 — Which statement about Type 1 versus Type 2 endometrial carcinoma is CORRECT?**

- A. Type 1 is more aggressive with poorer prognosis than Type 2
- B. Type 1 is oestrogen-driven, arising in younger obese women on a background of hyperplasia
- C. Type 2 is low-grade and non-metastasising
- D. Type 2 is oestrogen-dependent and associated with endometrial hyperplasia

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Type 1 is oestrogen-driven, arising in younger obese women on a background of hyperplasia** — This is the correct statement. Option A reverses the prognosis: Type 2 is the aggressive one with poor prognosis. Option C reverses the grade: Type 2 is high-grade and metastasises early (peritoneal spread in serous carcinoma). Option D reverses the oestrogen dependence: Type 2 is oestrogen-INDEPENDENT — it arises on atrophic, not hyperplastic, endometrium, through TP53 mutation pathways. This question appears almost verbatim in multiple NEET-PG years because the Type 1/Type 2 reversal is the classic trap.

</details>

---

### 11.8 Risk Factors for Endometrial Cancer — Unopposed Oestrogen

The central concept: **unopposed oestrogen stimulates endometrial proliferation without progesterone opposition** → hyperplasia → carcinoma.

**Risk factors (all increase oestrogen exposure):**
- Obesity (adipose tissue converts androstenedione to oestrone via aromatase)
- Nulliparity (more anovulatory cycles, prolonged unopposed oestrogen)
- Late menopause (prolonged oestrogen exposure)
- Early menarche (same reason)
- Polycystic ovary syndrome (chronic anovulation)
- Oestrogen-secreting tumours (granulosa cell tumour)
- Exogenous unopposed oestrogen (HRT without progestogen in women with intact uterus)
- **Tamoxifen use** (for breast cancer — acts as oestrogen agonist on uterus)
- Diabetes mellitus, hypertension (associated but likely via obesity linkage)
- Lynch syndrome / HNPCC (hereditary mismatch repair gene defect)

**Protective factors (add progesterone opposition or reduce oestrogen):**
- Combined OCP use (progestogen component opposes oestrogen)
- Smoking (paradoxically anti-oestrogenic via increased 2-hydroxylation)
- Multiparity
- Physical activity
- Progestogen-only IUD (LNG-IUS)

**Lynch Syndrome (HNPCC) and Endometrial Cancer:**
- Autosomal dominant; mutations in mismatch repair genes: MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2
- Lifetime risk of endometrial cancer: 40–60% (equal to or greater than colon cancer risk in women)
- Endometrial cancer is the SENTINEL cancer in women with Lynch syndrome (may precede colon cancer)
- Screening: annual endometrial sampling from age 35; prophylactic hysterectomy + BSO after childbearing completed
- Association: endometrioid subtype of both endometrial AND ovarian carcinoma

---

### 11.9 Endometrial Hyperplasia — The Cancer Risk Progression Table

| Histological Type | Risk of Progression to Carcinoma |
|---|---|
| Simple hyperplasia without atypia | ~1% |
| Simple hyperplasia with atypia | ~8% |
| Complex hyperplasia without atypia | ~3% |
| **Complex hyperplasia WITH atypia (EIN)** | **~29%** |

**EIN (Endometrial Intraepithelial Neoplasia)** = modern terminology for complex atypical hyperplasia. The "atypia" is the key determinant — architecture alone (simple vs complex) matters much less than cytological atypia.

---

### Exam Question — Endometrial Hyperplasia Cancer Risk (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q437 — Which histological pattern of endometrial hyperplasia carries the HIGHEST risk of progression to endometrial carcinoma?**

- A. Simple hyperplasia without atypia (~1%)
- B. Simple hyperplasia with atypia (~8%)
- C. Complex hyperplasia without atypia (~3%)
- D. Complex hyperplasia with atypia (~29%)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Complex hyperplasia with atypia (~29%)** — The critical variable is cytological atypia (nuclear enlargement, prominent nucleoli, irregular chromatin), NOT the architectural pattern. Simple atypical hyperplasia (B, ~8%) already carries higher risk than complex hyperplasia without atypia (C, ~3%), because atypia outweighs architecture in predicting malignant potential. Complex atypical hyperplasia / EIN at ~29% risk is managed by: hysterectomy in women who have completed childbearing; continuous progestogen (medroxyprogesterone acetate 10 mg/day or LNG-IUS) in young women desiring fertility, with 3–6 monthly repeat biopsies until regression is confirmed.

</details>

---

### 11.10 Endometrial Cancer Staging — FIGO (2009, updated 2023)

| Stage | Definition |
|---|---|
| IA | Tumour limited to endometrium or invades < 50% myometrium |
| IB | Tumour invades ≥ 50% myometrium |
| II | Tumour invades cervical stroma (but not beyond uterus) |
| IIIA | Tumour invades serosa or adnexa |
| IIIB | Vaginal or parametrial involvement |
| IIIC1 | Pelvic lymph node metastasis |
| IIIC2 | Para-aortic lymph node metastasis |
| IVA | Bladder or bowel mucosal involvement |
| IVB | Distant metastasis |

**Key differences from cervical staging:** Endometrial staging is **surgical** (not clinical) — performed at time of hysterectomy. The lymph node categories (IIIC1/IIIC2) mirror the FIGO 2018 cervical system.

### 11.11 Endometrial Cancer Workup

**Presenting symptom:** Postmenopausal bleeding in 90% of cases (PMB = endometrial carcinoma until proven otherwise)

**Investigation sequence:**
1. Pelvic examination + Pap smear (Pap smear is often normal — it screens cervix, not endometrium)
2. **Transvaginal ultrasound (TVS):** Endometrial thickness (ET)
   - Postmenopausal, no HRT: ET > **4 mm** → biopsy required
   - Postmenopausal on HRT: ET > 8 mm → biopsy
   - Premenopausal with AUB: ET ≥ 12 mm → biopsy
3. **Endometrial biopsy** (Pipelle sampler — outpatient): First-line tissue sampling; sensitivity ~95% for carcinoma
4. **D&C (Dilatation and Curettage):** If Pipelle sample inadequate; traditional gold standard for endometrial sampling
5. **Hysteroscopy + directed biopsy:** Best for polyps, focal lesions; gold standard for identifying and biopsying specific lesions
6. **MRI pelvis:** Assesses myometrial invasion depth and cervical involvement (staging pre-operatively)

---

### Exam Question — Postmenopausal Bleeding (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q107 — A 55-year-old postmenopausal woman with blood-stained vaginal discharge — next step?**

- A. Reassure (postmenopausal discharge is normal)
- B. Immediate cardiology referral
- C. Pelvic exam, TVS, Pap smear
- D. Antihypertensives and observation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Pelvic exam, TVS, Pap smear** — The axiom: postmenopausal bleeding = endometrial cancer until proven otherwise. Option A is dangerous — postmenopausal bleeding is NEVER normal and should never be dismissed. The initial workup includes pelvic examination (to rule out vulval/vaginal causes, visualise cervix), TVS (endometrial thickness), and Pap smear (to exclude cervical pathology). If ET > 4 mm on TVS, proceed to endometrial biopsy. The most important next step is NOT to reassure and NOT to ignore.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Perimenopausal AUB with Thick Endometrium

**Q108 — A 46-year-old nulliparous woman with heavy irregular bleeding and endometrial thickness 18 mm — next step?**

- A. Combined oral contraceptive pill
- B. Hysterectomy
- C. Wait and watch 6 months
- D. Endometrial biopsy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Endometrial biopsy** — Endometrial thickness ≥ 12 mm in a perimenopausal woman with abnormal uterine bleeding mandates histological evaluation before any treatment. At 18 mm, the risk of hyperplasia or carcinoma is significant, and treatment cannot be selected without a tissue diagnosis. Starting hormonal therapy (option A) without excluding carcinoma could suppress symptoms while masking a developing malignancy. Hysterectomy (option B) without histological diagnosis is inappropriate. Waiting 6 months (option C) when ET is 18 mm and bleeding is present is unacceptable — the risk threshold has already been exceeded.

</details>

---

### 11.12 Treatment of Endometrial Cancer

**Standard treatment (Stage I–II):**
- **Total abdominal hysterectomy (TAH) + bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy (BSO) + pelvic lymph node assessment**
- Laparoscopic or robotic approach increasingly standard
- Vaginal brachytherapy for Stage IA Grade 3 (vault radiotherapy)
- External beam RT + chemotherapy for Stage III–IV

**Fertility-sparing treatment (selected Stage IA, Grade 1, young woman):**
- Continuous high-dose progestogen (MPA 200 mg/day or LNG-IUS)
- 3-monthly endometrial biopsies to confirm regression
- IVF/pregnancy once remission documented
- Definitive hysterectomy after childbearing

---

## Bias Removal — Avoiding the Classic Cervical/Endometrial Confusion

**The Four Most Common Exam Errors:**

1. **Staging confusion — hydronephrosis:** Many candidates write Stage IIIC (LN involvement) for hydronephrosis. Wrong — hydronephrosis is Stage IIIB. IIIC is lymph nodes.

2. **Wertheim's for IIB:** Candidates correctly recall "Wertheim's = radical hysterectomy" but apply it to Stage IIB. Wrong — IIB has parametrial involvement; Wertheim's cannot achieve clear parametrial margins at Stage IIB. Chemoradiation is the answer.

3. **Point A = 5 cm lateral:** The distractors consistently give "5 cm lateral" for Point A to mislead. Point A = 2 cm lateral. Point B = 5 cm from midline (= 3 cm lateral to Point A). The question always specifies "Point A" to test this exact distinction.

4. **Schiller's test interpretation:** "Schiller-negative" means the iodine did NOT stain the tissue (abnormal, glycogen-poor). "Schiller-positive" (iodine-brown) = normal tissue. The double-negative confuses many candidates. A Schiller-negative lesion = suspicious = biopsy.

---

## Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

**Top 10 Exam Facts — Cervical & Endometrial Cancer:**

1. HPV 16 = most common in SCC; HPV 18 = adenocarcinoma; HPV 6/11 = warts (not cancer)
2. LSIL = CIN I; HSIL = CIN II + CIN III (Bethesda 2001)
3. Hydronephrosis = Stage **IIIB** (not IVA; IVA = bladder/rectal mucosa invasion)
4. Wertheim's hysterectomy = up to Stage IIA only; IIB onward = chemoradiation
5. Point A = 2 cm above external os + **2 cm** lateral; Point B = 3 cm further lateral (5 cm from midline)
6. Type 1 endometrial cancer = oestrogen-driven, good prognosis; Type 2 = TP53, poor prognosis
7. Lynch syndrome = endometrial cancer is sentinel cancer in women (40–60% lifetime risk)
8. Complex atypical hyperplasia (EIN) = highest progression risk (~29%) → treat with hysterectomy or continuous progestin
9. Postmenopausal bleeding: ET > 4 mm → endometrial biopsy; ET ≤ 4 mm → 95% benign
10. Tamoxifen = anti-oestrogenic in breast but pro-oestrogenic in uterus → endometrial cancer risk (annual endometrial surveillance for all women on tamoxifen)




---

# Chapter 12: Ovarian Cancer & Gestational Trophoblastic Disease

---

## Big Picture First

**Ovarian cancer** is called the "silent killer" because 70–75% of patients present at advanced stage (Stage III–IV), having had no specific early symptoms. The exam tests tumour markers (which marker with which tumour type), FIGO staging, and the distinction between epithelial, germ cell, and sex cord-stromal tumours. Get the marker table into your memory completely — it generates 2–3 questions every year.

**Gestational Trophoblastic Disease (GTD)** is a unique group of tumours arising from abnormal trophoblastic tissue. It includes molar pregnancy (complete and partial), invasive mole, choriocarcinoma, and placental site trophoblastic tumour. The critical exam facts are: (1) complete vs partial mole genetics, (2) post-molar beta-hCG surveillance protocol, (3) WHO/FIGO risk scoring for GTN, and (4) EMA-CO chemotherapy for high-risk disease. GTD is also remarkable because it can follow a completely normal pregnancy — an exam scenario favourite.

---

## Question Pattern Map

**Ovarian cancer traps:**
- CA-125 is NOT specific to ovarian cancer — it is elevated in endometriosis, PID, fibroids, pregnancy, and other cancers. The question will specify "primary tumour marker for ovarian cancer" — answer CA-125, but know its limitations.
- Serous carcinoma = most common (70%) and most often bilateral (50%); mucinous = largest tumour; endometrioid = associated with endometriosis; Brenner = transitional (looks like urothelium)
- Germ cell marker table: AFP = yolk sac tumour; HCG = choriocarcinoma (non-gestational); LDH + PLAP = dysgerminoma; AFP + HCG = embryonal carcinoma
- Clear cell carcinoma is an EPITHELIAL tumour — NOT a germ cell tumour (despite the name "mesonephroid/clear cell" being confusingly similar to mesonephric/germ cell terminology)
- Granulosa cell tumour = Call-Exner bodies + inhibin + oestrogen production (feminising)
- Sertoli-Leydig cell tumour = androgen production (virilising/masculinising)
- Meigs syndrome = ovarian FIBROMA + ascites + right hydrothorax

**GTD traps:**
- Complete mole = 46XX (all paternal chromosomes), NO fetal parts, very high HCG → snowstorm on USS
- Partial mole = 69XXX triploid, fetal parts present, lower HCG, Swiss-cheese placenta
- "Complete mole → 15–20% risk of GTN; Partial mole → 0.5–1% risk of GTN"
- Post-molar surveillance: weekly beta-hCG until normal, then monthly for 6 months (for complete mole). NO contraception for 6–12 months — but this means use CONTRACEPTION during surveillance (to avoid pregnancy confusing HCG results — the negative is the trap: "no contraception" = wrong)
- GTN criteria: rising HCG or plateau for ≥ 3 weeks after evacuation, regardless of HCG value
- WHO/FIGO score ≥ 7 = high-risk GTN → EMA-CO (not methotrexate alone)
- Choriocarcinoma can follow ANY pregnancy — normal delivery, ectopic, abortion, or mole

---

## Core Concept Deep Dive

### 12.1 Classification of Ovarian Tumours

Ovarian tumours are classified by cell of origin:

```
OVARIAN TUMOURS
├── EPITHELIAL (65–70% of all ovarian neoplasms; 90% of malignant ovarian tumours)
│   ├── Serous (most common)
│   ├── Mucinous (largest tumours)
│   ├── Endometrioid (associated with endometriosis)
│   ├── Clear cell / Mesonephroid (aggressive; associated with endometriosis)
│   ├── Brenner (transitional cell type; mostly benign)
│   └── Mixed
│
├── GERM CELL (15–20%; most malignant germ cell tumours occur in young women)
│   ├── Dysgerminoma (most common MALIGNANT germ cell tumour)
│   ├── Mature cystic teratoma / Dermoid (most common BENIGN germ cell tumour)
│   ├── Immature teratoma
│   ├── Endodermal sinus / Yolk sac tumour
│   ├── Embryonal carcinoma
│   └── Non-gestational choriocarcinoma
│
└── SEX CORD-STROMAL (5–8%)
    ├── Granulosa cell tumour (oestrogen-producing, feminising)
    ├── Thecoma (oestrogen-producing)
    ├── Fibroma (associated with Meigs syndrome)
    ├── Sertoli-Leydig cell tumour / Arrhenoblastoma (androgen-producing, virilising)
    └── Gonadoblastoma (in dysgenetic gonads)
```

---

### 12.2 Epithelial Ovarian Cancer — Subtypes

**Serous cystadenocarcinoma:**
- Most common epithelial ovarian malignancy (~70%)
- Bilateral in ~50% of cases
- **Psammoma bodies** (calcified concentric lamellated structures) on histology — pathognomonic
- Strongly CA-125 positive
- Associated with **BRCA1/2** mutations (hereditary high-grade serous carcinoma)
- BRCA1 mutations → 40–60% lifetime ovarian cancer risk; BRCA2 → 15–20%

**Mucinous cystadenocarcinoma:**
- 15–20% of epithelial ovarian cancers
- **Largest ovarian tumours** (can reach enormous sizes filling the abdomen)
- Usually **unilateral** (bilateral mucinous = think metastatic Krukenberg)
- Rupture → pseudomyxoma peritonei (mucin-secreting cells seed the peritoneum)
- CEA may be elevated (mucinous tumours can produce CEA)

**Endometrioid carcinoma:**
- ~20% of ovarian cancers
- Associated with **ovarian endometriosis** (co-existent in ~15%)
- Associated with **Lynch syndrome** (HNPCC — PTEN, ARID1A mutations)
