- Resembles endometrial glandular epithelium histologically
- May coexist with a synchronous endometrial carcinoma (15% of cases)

**Clear cell (Mesonephroid) carcinoma:**
- 5–10%; arises in endometriosis background
- High-grade; poor prognosis relative to other epithelial subtypes
- Characterised by "hobnail" cells and clear glycogen-rich cytoplasm
- CA-125 variably positive; relatively chemoresistant

**Brenner tumour:**
- Transitional cell type (histologically resembles urothelium = "coffee bean" grooved nuclei)
- Usually benign (>95%); small, solid, unilateral
- Often an incidental finding; may be associated with mucinous cystadenoma

---

### Exam Question — Most Common Epithelial Ovarian Cancer (NEET-PG repeated)

**Q18 — A 45-year-old with bilateral cystic-solid pelvic masses and elevated CA-125. Most likely epithelial ovarian cancer subtype:**

- A. Mucinous cystadenocarcinoma
- B. Serous cystadenocarcinoma
- C. Endometrioid carcinoma
- D. Brenner tumour

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Serous cystadenocarcinoma** — High-grade serous carcinoma accounts for ~70% of epithelial ovarian malignancies and is bilateral in 50% — both features present in this scenario. Psammoma bodies on histology, strong CA-125 elevation, and BRCA1/2 association are its fingerprints. Mucinous (option A) tumours are typically unilateral and much larger (filling the abdomen). Endometrioid (option C) accounts for 20% and is associated with endometriosis — not characteristically bilateral. Brenner (option D) is almost always benign, small, and solid — not a large cystic-solid bilateral mass. The bilateral nature + CA-125 elevation points directly to serous carcinoma.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Endometrioid Ovarian Tumour Frequency

**Q434 — Which ovarian tumour accounts for approximately 20% of all ovarian cancers and often co-exists with ovarian endometriosis?**

- A. Serous cystadenocarcinoma
- B. Mucinous cystadenocarcinoma
- C. Endometrioid carcinoma
- D. Brenner tumour

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Endometrioid carcinoma** — The 20% figure and the endometriosis association are both the hallmarks of endometrioid ovarian carcinoma. This tumour shows glandular differentiation resembling endometrial glands and is associated with PTEN and ARID1A mutations. Option A (serous) accounts for ~70%; option B (mucinous) is the largest tumour, associated with pseudomyxoma peritonei if it ruptures; option D (Brenner) is mostly benign and very small. The endometriosis co-existence (option C) is the key distinguishing phrase — note that clear cell carcinoma also arises in endometriosis, but it does NOT account for 20% of ovarian cancers.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Pseudomyxoma Peritonei (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q435 — Pseudomyxoma peritonei most commonly occurs as a complication of rupture of:**

- A. Serous cystadenoma
- B. Mucinous cystadenoma / low-grade appendiceal mucinous neoplasm
- C. Dysgerminoma
- D. Gonadoblastoma

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Mucinous cystadenoma / low-grade appendiceal mucinous neoplasm** — Rupture of a mucinous tumour (ovarian or appendiceal) seeds the peritoneum with mucin-secreting epithelial cells, producing massive gelatinous peritoneal deposits — the jelly belly of pseudomyxoma peritonei. Modern evidence shows the appendix is the primary site in most true pseudomyxoma peritonei cases (with secondary ovarian involvement). The appendix should always be removed when pseudomyxoma peritonei is encountered. Dysgerminoma (option C) and gonadoblastoma (option D) are solid germ cell tumours with no mucin production capacity.

</details>

---

### 12.3 Tumour Markers — The Master Table (Memorise This)

This table generates 2–4 questions in every NEET-PG paper. Practise it until automatic.

| Tumour | Primary Marker | Secondary/Other Markers |
|---|---|---|
| **Epithelial ovarian cancer (serous)** | **CA-125** | HE4, CEA (mucinous) |
| **Dysgerminoma** | **LDH (+ PLAP)** | beta-hCG (if syncytiotrophoblast elements) |
| **Yolk sac tumour (Endodermal sinus)** | **AFP** | — |
| **Non-gestational choriocarcinoma** | **beta-hCG** | — |
| **Immature teratoma** | **AFP** | LDH |
| **Embryonal carcinoma** | **AFP + beta-hCG** | Both elevated |
| **Granulosa cell tumour** | **Inhibin** (B most specific) | Oestradiol |
| **Sertoli-Leydig cell tumour** | **Testosterone / androstenedione** | AFP (if heterologous) |
| **Mucinous tumours (ovary/appendix)** | **CEA** | CA 19-9, CA-125 |
| **GTD / Gestational choriocarcinoma** | **beta-hCG** | — |
| **Krukenberg tumour (gastric met)** | CEA | CA 19-9 |

**Memory device for germ cell markers:**
- **"AYS" = AFP → Yolk Sac**
- **"HCG → H is for trophoblast (choriocarcinoma)"**
- **"LDH → Like Dysgerminoma's Hash"**
- Granulosa = **inhibin** (makes sense — granulosa cells normally produce inhibin)

---

### Exam Question — CA-125 Marker (NEET-PG 2020-style)

**Q692 — CA-125 is a marker antigen primarily used for the diagnosis of:**

- A. Colorectal carcinoma
- B. Breast carcinoma
- C. Brain tumour
- D. Ovarian carcinoma

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Ovarian carcinoma** — CA-125 is elevated in ~80% of advanced epithelial ovarian cancers, especially high-grade serous carcinoma. However, it is NOT specific: also elevated in endometriosis, PID, uterine fibroids, early pregnancy, liver disease, and other cancers (endometrial, pancreatic). CA-125 is most useful for monitoring treatment response and detecting recurrence in known ovarian cancer, NOT as a primary diagnostic test. For screening, CA-125 combined with TVS is used in high-risk women (BRCA mutation carriers), but alone has poor sensitivity for early-stage disease.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Dysgerminoma Marker — Secreted Marker (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q431 — Which product is secreted by dysgerminoma?**

- A. Alpha-fetoprotein (AFP)
- B. Human chorionic gonadotropin (hCG)
- C. Placental alkaline phosphatase (PLAP)
- D. Serum inhibin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Placental alkaline phosphatase (PLAP)** — Pure dysgerminoma (the ovarian counterpart of testicular seminoma) secretes PLAP and LDH. It does NOT secrete AFP (that is yolk sac tumour) or hCG (that is choriocarcinoma). If a dysgerminoma has beta-hCG elevation, it suggests a mixed germ cell tumour with choriocarcinoma elements — important distinction. Inhibin (option D) is secreted by granulosa cell tumours and Sertoli-Leydig tumours, not germ cell tumours.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Dysgerminoma Monitoring Marker (NEET-PG repeated)

**Q432 — In a confirmed case of dysgerminoma, which serum marker is monitored for treatment response and recurrence?**

- A. Beta-hCG
- B. Alpha-fetoprotein
- C. Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)
- D. Serum inhibin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)** — LDH (especially isoenzyme 1) is the preferred serum marker for monitoring dysgerminoma. While PLAP is secreted, LDH is the practically used monitoring marker. Beta-hCG (option A) is monitored in gestational trophoblastic disease and non-gestational choriocarcinoma. AFP (option B) is monitored in yolk sac tumours and immature teratomas. Inhibin (option D) is the monitoring marker for granulosa cell tumour. This question appears in a slightly different form multiple times — the answer is always LDH for dysgerminoma.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Clear Cell Tumour Classification Trap

**Q433 — All of the following are germ cell tumours of the ovary EXCEPT:**

- A. Mesonephroid (clear cell) tumour
- B. Mature cystic teratoma (dermoid)
- C. Dysgerminoma
- D. Endodermal sinus tumour (yolk sac tumour)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Mesonephroid (clear cell) tumour** — Clear cell carcinoma is an EPITHELIAL (surface epithelial-stromal) ovarian tumour, NOT a germ cell tumour. The name "mesonephroid" (referring to mesonephric/Wolffian duct-like appearance) is deliberately confusing when placed next to germ cell options. Germ cell tumours arise from primordial germ cells: dysgerminoma (option C), mature and immature teratoma (option B), yolk sac tumour (option D), embryonal carcinoma, and non-gestational choriocarcinoma. Clear cell carcinoma is epithelial, arises in endometriosis, and is not related to germ cells at all.

</details>

---

### 12.4 Sex Cord-Stromal Tumours — Hormonal Behaviour

**Granulosa Cell Tumour (GCT):**
- Produces **oestrogen** → FEMINISING effects
- Postmenopausal women: postmenopausal bleeding (from oestrogen stimulation of endometrium)
- Premenopausal women: menstrual irregularity; in prepubertal girls: precocious puberty
- Histological hallmark: **Call-Exner bodies** (small follicle-like spaces filled with eosinophilic material, surrounded by granulosa cells)
- Tumour marker: **Inhibin** (B most specific); also AMH (anti-Mullerian hormone)
- Menopausal women with GCT: endometrial carcinoma in 5–10% (unopposed oestrogen effect)
- Low-grade malignancy; late recurrence (10–20 years after initial treatment)

**Sertoli-Leydig Cell Tumour (Arrhenoblastoma):**
- Produces **androgens** (testosterone) → VIRILISING effects
- Clinical features: hirsutism, clitoromegaly, voice deepening, amenorrhoea, temporal balding, breast atrophy
- Most common in women aged 20–40 years
- Low-grade malignancy; good prognosis
- AFP may be elevated if heterologous elements present

**Fibroma (Ovarian):**
- Benign, solid, fibrous
- **Meigs syndrome:** Ovarian fibroma + ascites + right-sided hydrothorax
  - Ascites and pleural effusion resolve completely after tumour removal
  - "Pseudo-Meigs syndrome" = same triad with OTHER ovarian tumours (serous cystadenoma, struma ovarii, etc.)

---

### Exam Question — Call-Exner Bodies (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q133 — Call-Exner bodies are seen in:**

- A. Dysgerminoma
- B. Theca cell tumour
- C. Granulosa cell tumour
- D. Polyembryoma

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Granulosa cell tumour** — Call-Exner bodies are small follicle-like spaces filled with eosinophilic material, surrounded by granulosa cells arranged in rosette-like patterns. They are pathognomonic for granulosa cell tumours. The granulosa cell tumour also produces inhibin and oestrogen. Option B (theca cell tumour) is closely related and also produces oestrogen, but theca cells form luteinised clusters without the follicle-like Call-Exner spaces. Option A (dysgerminoma) shows large vesicular cells with lymphocytic infiltrate — no follicle-like structures. Inhibin and Call-Exner bodies = granulosa cell tumour: remember this pairing.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Schiller-Duval Bodies (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q137 — Schiller-Duval bodies are pathognomonic of:**

- A. Endodermal sinus (yolk sac) tumour
- B. Granulosa cell tumour
- C. Dysgerminoma
- D. Dermoid cyst

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Endodermal sinus (yolk sac) tumour** — Schiller-Duval bodies are perivascular glomeruloid structures: a central capillary surrounded by a layer of tumour cells within a cystic space — resembling a primitive glomerulus. AFP is markedly elevated. Yolk sac tumours (endodermal sinus tumours) occur most commonly in girls and young women (median age 19 years), grow rapidly, and require BEP chemotherapy. Granulosa cell tumours (option B) have Call-Exner bodies (different!). Dysgerminoma (option C) has large cells with central nuclei and lymphocytic infiltrate. Dermoid (option D) has squamous epithelium with skin appendages. Call-Exner = GCT; Schiller-Duval = Yolk Sac: always pair these.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Meigs Syndrome (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q356 — Meigs syndrome consists of:**

- A. Ovarian fibroma + ascites + right hydrothorax
- B. Granulosa cell tumour + virilisation + ascites
- C. Dysgerminoma + hypercalcaemia + effusion
- D. Mucinous tumour + pseudomyxoma peritonei

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Ovarian fibroma + ascites + right hydrothorax** — Meigs syndrome is the classic triad. Importantly, (1) the hydrothorax is right-sided in 70%, bilateral in 10%, and left-sided in 20%; (2) complete resolution of both ascites and pleural effusion occurs after surgical removal of the fibroma; (3) CA-125 may be markedly elevated, mimicking malignancy — a diagnostic trap. Option B describes features of a virilising sex cord-stromal tumour, not Meigs. Option C (dysgerminoma + hypercalcaemia) is a feature of small cell carcinoma of the ovary (hypercalcaemic type), not Meigs. Option D is pseudomyxoma peritonei from mucinous rupture, not Meigs.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Krukenberg Tumour (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q355 — Krukenberg tumour most commonly metastasises from:**

- A. Lung
- B. Stomach (signet-ring cells)
- C. Breast
- D. Colorectal

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Gastric carcinoma (signet-ring cells)** — Krukenberg tumours are bilateral ovarian metastases characterised by signet-ring cells (mucin displaces the nucleus peripherally) embedded within a cellular spindle-cell stroma. The stomach is the most common primary site in Asian patients, while breast and colon are next. Lung (option A) very rarely metastasises to the ovary. The key histological feature is signet-ring cells in cellular stroma — not seen in primary ovarian tumours. Krukenberg = Bilateral ovarian mass in a woman with GI symptoms → always ask about the stomach and colon.

</details>

---

### 12.5 FIGO Staging of Ovarian Cancer

| Stage | Definition |
|---|---|
| IA | One ovary, capsule intact, no surface growth, negative washings |
| IB | Both ovaries, capsule intact |
| IC | As above + capsule rupture / surface involvement / positive washings |
| IIA | Extension to uterus or tubes |
| IIB | Extension to other pelvic structures |
| IIIA | Microscopic peritoneal metastases beyond pelvis |
| IIIB | Macroscopic peritoneal metastases ≤ 2 cm |
| IIIC | Peritoneal metastases > 2 cm ± retroperitoneal LN |
| IVA | Pleural effusion with cytologically positive fluid |
| IVB | Parenchymal liver/spleen metastases; extra-abdominal LN |

**Note:** Ovarian cancer Stage IIIB and cervical cancer Stage IIIB are COMPLETELY DIFFERENT. Ovarian IIIB = peritoneal metastases ≤ 2 cm. Cervical IIIB = pelvic wall extension or hydronephrosis. This cross-chapter confusion is an exam trap.

**Most common stage at diagnosis:** Stage IIIC (~50% of ovarian cancers are Stage III at diagnosis — this is why it is the "silent killer").

**Treatment:** Surgery (TAH + BSO + omentectomy + peritoneal biopsies + LND = comprehensive staging/debulking) + platinum-taxane chemotherapy (carboplatin + paclitaxel).

---

### 12.6 Gestational Trophoblastic Disease — Classification

**GTD spectrum:**
```
GTD
├── BENIGN (Non-neoplastic)
│   ├── Complete hydatidiform mole
│   └── Partial hydatidiform mole
│
└── MALIGNANT (GTN = Gestational Trophoblastic Neoplasia)
    ├── Invasive mole (locally invasive, high HCG)
    ├── Choriocarcinoma (highly malignant, haematogenous spread)
    ├── Placental site trophoblastic tumour (PSTT)
    └── Epithelioid trophoblastic tumour (ETT)
```

---

### 12.7 Complete vs Partial Hydatidiform Mole — The Critical Comparison

This table is the single most tested GTD concept:

| Feature | Complete Mole | Partial Mole |
|---|---|---|
| **Karyotype** | **46XX (diploid, all paternal)** | **69XXX or 69XXY (triploid)** |
| **Fetal tissue** | **ABSENT** | **PRESENT (abnormal fetus/fetal RBCs)** |
| **Trophoblastic hyperplasia** | Diffuse, marked | Focal, mild |
| **Villous hydrops** | Diffuse | Focal ("Swiss-cheese placenta") |
| **Uterine size** | Large for dates (in 50%) | Small or appropriate for dates |
| **HCG level** | **Very high (> 100,000 mIU/mL)** | **Lower than complete** |
| **Ultrasound** | **Snowstorm, NO fetus** | **Swiss-cheese placenta + small fetus** |
| **Theca-lutein cysts** | **Present in 25–30%** | Rare |
| **Risk of GTN** | **15–20%** | **0.5–1%** |
| **Pre-eclampsia risk** | High (before 20 weeks — a classic finding) | Low |
| **Hyperemesis** | Severe | Mild |

**Genetics detail (important for NEET-PG):**
- Complete mole: typically formed by fertilisation of an empty ovum by **one sperm that duplicates itself** (monospermic, 46XX) OR by **two sperm** (dispermic, 46XX or 46XY — all paternal). Nuclear material is entirely paternal; maternal mitochondrial DNA is present but nuclear chromosomes are all paternal.
- Partial mole: fertilisation of a normal ovum by **two sperm** simultaneously → triploid 69XXX, 69XXY, or 69XYY.

---

### Exam Question — Complete Mole Ultrasound (NEET-PG repeated)

**Q20 — Classic ultrasound appearance of a complete hydatidiform mole:**

- A. Snowstorm pattern with echogenic foci filling the uterus
- B. Swiss-cheese placenta with a small fetus
- C. Anechoic intrauterine cyst
- D. Snowstorm pattern with a fetus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Snowstorm pattern with echogenic foci filling the uterus** — Complete mole shows multiple echogenic/hydropic villi filling the entire uterine cavity with NO fetal parts visible — the "snowstorm." Option B (Swiss-cheese placenta with a small fetus) describes a PARTIAL mole — the fetus is abnormal and triploid, the placenta shows focal hydropic change. Option D is the critical distractor: a snowstorm WITH a fetus = partial mole scenario, not complete mole. The complete mole has NO fetus whatsoever. Option C (anechoic cyst) suggests a simple ovarian cyst or early failed pregnancy — not a mole.

</details>

---

### 12.8 Presentation of Molar Pregnancy

**Classic clinical picture:**
- Amenorrhoea (missed periods) → patient thinks she is pregnant
- Vaginal bleeding (brownish, continuous) — most common presenting symptom
- Uterus larger than dates for gestational age (though may be normal or small in partial mole)
- **Passage of grape-like vesicles** — pathognomonic when present (not always seen)
- Hyperemesis gravidarum (worse than normal pregnancy due to very high HCG)
- Signs of hyperthyroidism (HCG has weak TSH-like activity → can cause biochemical hyperthyroidism at very high levels)
- Pre-eclampsia before 20 weeks — a classic sign; PIH before 20 weeks should always raise suspicion for molar pregnancy
- **Theca-lutein cysts** (bilateral large multi-locular ovarian cysts): HCG overstimulates ovaries → bilateral ovarian enlargement; **not to be treated** (they regress with falling HCG after mole evacuation)

---

### Scenario Question — Grape-like Vesicles Passage

**Scenario: A 26-year-old G2P1 at 16 weeks presents with brownish vaginal bleeding and passage of grape-like vesicles. Ultrasound shows snowstorm pattern, no fetal parts. HCG = 250,000 mIU/mL. Diagnosis, next step, and follow-up?**

**Diagnosis:** Complete hydatidiform mole (46XX, all paternal, no fetal parts, snowstorm USS, very high HCG, grape-like vesicles).

**Next step:** Suction evacuation (suction curettage) — the first-line treatment at any gestational age. NOT oxytocin induction, NOT prostaglandins (these cause trophoblastic dissemination). Hysterectomy is an alternative only in older women who desire sterilisation.

**Pre-evacuation workup:** HCG quantitative, blood group + crossmatch, CXR (pulmonary trophoblastic embolism?), coagulation screen, thyroid function.

**During evacuation:** Oxytocin infusion after suction starts (not before — to avoid trophoblastic dissemination), Anti-D immunoglobulin if Rh-negative.

**Post-evacuation follow-up (most tested aspect):**
- **Serial beta-hCG:** Weekly until normal → then monthly for 6 months (complete mole); 3 months (partial mole)
- **Use contraception** during the entire surveillance period (OCP is first choice — also reduces false HCG signals) — this is often incorrectly remembered as "no contraception"
- **No pregnancy** during surveillance (rising HCG from new pregnancy would be confused with GTN)
- **Normal HCG values:** Should normalise within 56–70 days of evacuation for complete mole

**GTN criteria (when to start chemotherapy):**
1. HCG plateau (< 10% change) for ≥ 3 consecutive weekly measurements
2. Rising HCG (≥ 10% rise) over 2 consecutive weekly measurements
3. HCG still elevated at 6 months after evacuation
4. Histological diagnosis of choriocarcinoma
5. Metastatic disease (lung, brain, liver)

---

### Exam Question — Molar Pregnancy Treatment (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q145 — A 29-year-old at 16 weeks with bleeding and snowstorm USS — most feasible treatment?**

- A. Misoprostol alone
- B. Hysterectomy
- C. Mifepristone
- D. Suction evacuation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Suction evacuation** — Standard definitive treatment for complete hydatidiform mole at any gestational age. Misoprostol (option A) is a prostaglandin used for termination of normal pregnancies — it causes uterine contractions that can disseminate trophoblastic tissue haematogenously, potentially promoting metastatic GTN. Mifepristone (option C) similarly induces contractions and is not appropriate for molar evacuation. Hysterectomy (option B) is reserved for women who have completed their family and wish simultaneous sterilisation; it eliminates the risk of invasive mole but is not first-line for a 29-year-old who may want future pregnancies.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — GTD Incidence Geography (NEET-PG)

**Q49 — The highest incidence of gestational trophoblastic disease (complete mole) is in:**

- A. Australia
- B. Southeast Asia
- C. North America
- D. Western Europe

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Southeast Asia** — Complete mole incidence: ~1 in 600–1000 pregnancies in Southeast Asia, Philippines, and Taiwan, compared to ~1 in 1500–2000 in Western countries. Risk factors include dietary deficiency of vitamin A/carotene, Asian ethnicity, extremes of reproductive age (< 15 or > 40 years), history of previous molar pregnancy (1–2% recurrence risk), and blood group A in husband with group O wife (proposed association). The high incidence in Asia makes GTD a relatively common clinical entity in Indian practice.

</details>

---

### 12.9 Choriocarcinoma — The Unique Trophoblastic Malignancy

**Key clinical facts:**
- Highly malignant tumour of trophoblastic cells (cytotrophoblast + syncytiotrophoblast)
- **Can follow ANY pregnancy:** Normal delivery (30–40%), hydatidiform mole (50%), spontaneous abortion (20%), ectopic pregnancy (rare)
- The interval between antecedent pregnancy and choriocarcinoma diagnosis can be months to years
- Classic presentation: irregular bleeding after any pregnancy + rising HCG + lung metastases (cannonball lesions)
- **No chorionic villi** histologically (distinguishes from invasive mole, which has villi)
- Spreads haematogenously (not lymphatically): lungs most common, then brain, liver
- **Extraordinarily chemosensitive:** 90–95% cure rate even with metastatic disease

**Scenario: Post-hysterectomy patient with rising HCG**
- Any woman with persistently or rising HCG after hysterectomy should be evaluated for GTN (PSTT or choriocarcinoma)
- Investigate: serum HCG, CXR, CT chest/abdomen/pelvis, brain MRI (if neuro symptoms)
- Treat: WHO/FIGO risk score → single-agent or multi-agent chemotherapy

---

### Exam Question — Choriocarcinoma Following Normal Pregnancy (NEET-PG 2021)

**Q217 — A woman underwent D&C for molar pregnancy 6 months ago and now has abdominal pain, persistently elevated beta-hCG, and cannonball lung lesions. Appropriate management?**

- A. Methotrexate with folinic acid
- B. Methotrexate alone
- C. EMA-CO regimen
- D. Hysterectomy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Methotrexate with folinic acid** — This question requires WHO/FIGO risk scoring before choosing the answer. Post-molar GTN with lung metastases needs risk scoring (see section 12.10). If the WHO score is < 7 (low-risk), single-agent methotrexate with folinic acid (leucovorin) rescue is the standard. EMA-CO (option C) is reserved for HIGH-risk GTN (WHO score ≥ 7). The distinguishing feature here is the risk score — cannonball lesions alone (1 metastatic site = lung) may not necessarily equal high-risk if other score parameters are low. Hysterectomy (option D) is not the primary treatment for metastatic GTN; chemotherapy cures even metastatic disease in the vast majority. Note: if the question specified WHO score ≥ 7 OR more than one metastatic site OR prior chemotherapy failure, the answer would be EMA-CO.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — EMA-CO for Resistant Choriocarcinoma (NEET-PG 2021)

**Q15 — Choriocarcinoma failing single-agent methotrexate. Next regimen?**

- A. Methotrexate + folinic acid
- B. Methotrexate alone (continue)
- C. EMACO regimen
- D. Hysterectomy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. EMA-CO regimen** — When single-agent chemotherapy fails (rising or plateau HCG despite adequate methotrexate courses), the patient is reclassified as high-risk and requires multi-agent EMA-CO: **E**toposide, **M**ethotrexate, **A**ctinomycin D (given alternating weeks) + **C**yclophosphamide + **O**ncovin (Vincristine). EMA-CO achieves cure in ~75–80% of high-risk GTN. Continuing methotrexate alone (option B) when resistance is documented is incorrect and delays effective treatment. Hysterectomy (option D) may be used as an adjunct in PSTT or to manage haemorrhage but is not the chemotherapy choice.

</details>

---

### 12.10 WHO/FIGO Risk Scoring for GTN — The Score That Determines Treatment

The WHO/FIGO risk score predicts response to single-agent chemotherapy. Score ≥ 7 = high-risk = multi-agent EMA-CO required.

| Prognostic Factor | Score 0 | Score 1 | Score 2 | Score 4 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Age | < 40 | ≥ 40 | — | — |
| Antecedent pregnancy | Mole | Abortion | Term pregnancy | — |
| Interval (months) | < 4 | 4–6 | 7–12 | > 12 |
| Pre-treatment HCG (mIU/mL) | < 1000 | 1000–9999 | 10,000–99,999 | ≥ 100,000 |
| Largest tumour size (cm) | < 3 | 3–4 | ≥ 5 | — |
| Sites of metastasis | Lung | Spleen, kidney | GI tract | Brain, liver |
| Number of metastases | 0 | 1–4 | 5–8 | > 8 |
| Previous failed chemotherapy | — | — | Single drug | ≥ 2 drugs |

**Score interpretation:**
- **0–6 = Low-risk:** Single-agent chemotherapy (methotrexate 50 mg/m² IM every 48h + folinic acid 15 mg PO 30h after each MTX, for 8 days; or actinomycin D)
- **≥ 7 = High-risk:** Multi-agent EMA-CO (or EMA-EP in resistant cases)

**Memory aid for WHO score parameters (8 rows):**
**"A² I² HT SM P" = Age, Antecedent pregnancy, Interval, HCG, Tumour size, Sites, Number of Metastases, Prior treatment**

**Key scoring facts for NEET-PG:**
- Term pregnancy as antecedent = score 2 (highest) → more aggressive disease expected
- Brain and liver metastases = score 4 (highest for metastatic sites) → high-risk almost automatically
- HCG ≥ 100,000 mIU/mL = score 4 → high-risk
- Prior failed chemotherapy = most dire situation → score 2–4

---

### Exam Question — GTN Marker for Monitoring (NEET-PG pattern)

**Q87 — In GTN, the most reliable marker for monitoring treatment response and detecting recurrence:**

- A. CA-125
- B. AFP
- C. β-hCG
- D. LDH

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. β-hCG** — Beta-hCG is secreted by all types of trophoblastic tissue (normal and malignant); it is the ONLY reliable marker for GTN of all types. Serial beta-hCG measurements document: (a) complete remission (three consecutive weekly normal values), (b) resistance to current therapy (plateau or rise during chemotherapy), and (c) recurrence after treatment. CA-125 (option A) is an ovarian cancer marker — it has no reliable role in GTN monitoring. AFP (option B) is elevated in yolk sac tumours but not GTN. LDH (option D) monitors dysgerminoma. Beta-hCG is the gold standard — any examination question about GTN monitoring will have beta-hCG as the answer.

</details>

---

### Exam Question — Beta-hCG Measurement Method (NEET-PG)

**Q36 — The gold standard test for measuring serum β-hCG levels (in GTD) is:**

- A. ELISA
- B. Bioassay
- C. Radioimmunoassay (RIA)
- D. Latex agglutination inhibition

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Radioimmunoassay (RIA)** — RIA is the most sensitive and specific quantitative technique for measuring serum beta-hCG, detecting levels as low as 1–5 mIU/mL. This precision is essential for GTD monitoring (where decisions to continue or stop chemotherapy depend on small changes in HCG). ELISA (option A) is used in home pregnancy tests and is adequate for qualitative detection but less precise for low-level quantification. Latex agglutination inhibition (option D) is a qualitative urine pregnancy test — wholly inadequate for GTN monitoring. Bioassay (option B) is historical and no longer used clinically.

</details>

---

### 12.11 Dysgerminoma — The Most Important Germ Cell Tumour

**Why it gets its own section:** Dysgerminoma is tested on almost every NEET-PG — typically in 2–3 questions per year across marker identification, histology, and treatment.

**Clinical features:**
- Most common **malignant** germ cell tumour of the ovary (~50% of malignant GCT)
- Ovarian counterpart of **testicular seminoma**
- Age: typically 10–30 years (young women, adolescents)
- Bilateral in 10–15% (more bilateral than other GCT)
- Solid tumour on USS; moderate size
- Associated with **gonadal dysgenesis** (45,X / 46,XY mosaics): dysgenetic gonads → gonadoblastoma → dysgerminoma

**Histology:**
- Large, vesicular cells with clear cytoplasm and central nuclei (similar to primordial germ cells)
- Lymphocytic infiltrate in the stroma (T-cell mediated immunity)
- Fibrous septa dividing tumour into lobules
- Syncytiotrophoblast giant cells may be present → HCG mildly elevated (NOT AFP)

**Tumour markers:**
- **LDH** (especially isoenzyme 1) = primary monitoring marker
- **PLAP** (Placental Alkaline Phosphatase) = secreted marker
- Beta-hCG: only mildly elevated if syncytiotrophoblast elements present; AFP = negative (absence of AFP distinguishes pure dysgerminoma from mixed GCT)

**Treatment:**
- **Radiosensitive AND chemosensitive** (unique advantage)
- Stage IA: Unilateral salpingo-oophorectomy (fertility-sparing) — excellent prognosis
- Advanced/bilateral: BEP chemotherapy (Bleomycin, Etoposide, Cisplatin) — 5-year survival > 90%
- Historically treated with radiotherapy; now chemotherapy preferred to preserve fertility

**Prognosis:** Excellent — even with metastatic disease, > 90% cure rate with chemotherapy.

---

### Exam Question — Most Common Pure Germ Cell Tumour (NEET-PG)

**Q121 — Most common pure germ cell tumour of the ovary:**

- A. Choriocarcinoma
- B. Dysgerminoma
- C. Embryonal cell tumour
- D. Malignant teratoma

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Dysgerminoma** — Dysgerminoma accounts for ~50% of all malignant ovarian germ cell tumours. Important to distinguish: the most common germ cell tumour OVERALL is the mature cystic teratoma (dermoid) — a BENIGN tumour. The most common MALIGNANT pure germ cell tumour = dysgerminoma. This distinction is an exam trap. Choriocarcinoma (option A) is rare in pure form; it usually occurs as a component of mixed GCT. Embryonal carcinoma (option C) is very rare. Malignant teratoma (option D) = immature teratoma, which is less common than dysgerminoma as a pure malignant GCT.

</details>

---

## Bias Removal — Avoiding the High-Frequency Exam Errors

**The Five Most Dangerous GTD/Ovarian Confusions:**

**1. Complete mole HCG vs Partial mole HCG:**
- "Falling HCG after suction evacuation = GOOD news" (trophoblastic tissue being eliminated)
- "Rising HCG after evacuation = BAD news" (GTN developing — invasive mole or choriocarcinoma)
- Complete mole: very high HCG (> 100,000 mIU/mL usually)
- Partial mole: lower HCG (often < 100,000 mIU/mL)
- Theca-lutein cysts = seen with complete mole (HCG overstimulates ovaries); do NOT aspirate them

**2. Contraception during mole surveillance:**
- The correct instruction is: "USE contraception during the entire surveillance period"
- The exam distractors often say "no contraception is required" — WRONG
- OCP is preferred (also suppresses LH which could interfere with HCG assays)
- IUD is contraindicated until HCG normalises (insertion risk of perforation in soft involuting uterus)

**3. Ovarian vs Cervical IIIB:**
- Cervical IIIB = hydronephrosis / pelvic wall extension
- Ovarian IIIB = peritoneal metastases ≤ 2 cm
- Never mix these staging definitions

**4. Granulosa cell tumour hormone vs Sertoli-Leydig hormone:**
- Granulosa = oestrogen → feminising (endometrial hyperplasia/cancer in post-menopausal woman)
- Sertoli-Leydig = androgen → virilising (hirsutism, clitoromegaly in young woman)
- Examiner favourite: "Young woman with irregular periods and hirsutism → which ovarian tumour?" = Sertoli-Leydig

**5. Clear cell carcinoma is NOT a germ cell tumour:**
- Clear cell (mesonephroid) carcinoma = EPITHELIAL tumour
- The name "mesonephroid" evokes mesonephric/germ cell thinking — resist this
- Germ cell = dysgerminoma, teratoma, yolk sac, embryonal, choriocarcinoma (non-gestational)

---

## Memory Engineering

**GTD Risk Score — AAIHTSNP (8 items, same order as the table):**
- **A**ge (< 40 = 0; ≥ 40 = 1)
- **A**ntecedent pregnancy (Mole=0, Abortion=1, Term=2)
- **I**nterval months (< 4 = 0; 4–6 = 1; 7–12 = 2; > 12 = 4)
- **H**CG level (< 1000 = 0 → ≥ 100,000 = 4)
- **T**umour size (< 3 cm = 0; 3–4 cm = 1; ≥ 5 cm = 2)
- **S**ites (Lung = 1; Spleen/kidney = 2; GI = 2; Brain/liver = 4)
- **N**umber of mets (0=0; 1–4=1; 5–8=2; >8=4)
- **P**rior treatment (Single drug=2; 2 drugs=4)

**Score ≥ 7 → High Risk → EMA-CO**

**Ovarian tumour marker mnemonic — "DYGAL":**
- **D**ysgerminoma = **L**DH (L for LDH)
- **Y**olk Sac = **A**FP (A for AFP)
- **G**ranulosa = Inhibin
- **A**denocarcinoma (epithelial) = CA-125
- Choriocarcinoma = **HCG**

## Ultra High-Yield Revision Layer

### Ovarian Tumour Marker Table (Final Revision)

| Tumour Type | Marker | Extra Point |
|---|---|---|
| Serous epithelial (most common) | CA-125 | BRCA1/2 association; psammoma bodies |
| Granulosa cell | Inhibin B | Call-Exner bodies; oestrogen production; LMP malignancy |
| Sertoli-Leydig | Testosterone | Virilisation; AFP if heterologous elements |
| Dysgerminoma | LDH (+ PLAP) | Most common malignant GCT; radiosensitive; bilateral 10% |
| Yolk sac tumour | AFP | Schiller-Duval bodies; in young girls |
| Choriocarcinoma (non-gestational) | beta-hCG | No chorionic villi; hematogenous spread |
| Embryonal carcinoma | AFP + beta-hCG | Both markers elevated |
| Mucinous | CEA + CA 19-9 | Largest tumour; pseudomyxoma if ruptured |
| Krukenberg (secondary) | CEA | Signet-ring cells; bilateral; stomach is #1 primary |

### Complete vs Partial Mole — Final Flash Card

| | Complete Mole | Partial Mole |
|---|---|---|
| Karyotype | 46XX (all paternal) | 69XXX (triploid) |
| Fetus | Absent | Present (abnormal) |
| USS | Snowstorm | Swiss-cheese + fetus |
| HCG | Very high | Moderate |
| GTN risk | 15–20% | 0.5–1% |
| Theca-lutein cysts | Yes (25%) | Rare |

### GTN WHO/FIGO Score — Threshold

- **Score 0–6 → Low-risk → Single-agent MTX (± folinic acid)**
- **Score ≥ 7 → High-risk → EMA-CO**
- Cannonball lung lesions alone: may be low-risk if score < 7
- Brain/liver metastases: score 4 for site → almost always high-risk

### Top 10 Exam Traps — Ovarian Cancer & GTD

1. Most common malignant germ cell tumour = dysgerminoma (NOT mature teratoma — teratoma is most common BENIGN GCT)
2. Dysgerminoma marker for MONITORING = LDH (PLAP is secreted, but LDH is monitored)
3. Clear cell carcinoma = EPITHELIAL, NOT germ cell
4. Call-Exner bodies = Granulosa cell tumour (NOT dysgerminoma — different histology)
5. Schiller-Duval bodies = Yolk sac tumour (not granulosa — pair these separately from Call-Exner)
6. Complete mole = 46XX all PATERNAL (NOT 46XX maternal); empty egg fertilised by sperm
7. Partial mole = TRIPLOID 69XXX (two sperm + one egg) = NOT diploid
8. Post-molar surveillance requires CONTRACEPTION (usually OCP) — not "no contraception"
9. Meigs syndrome = FIBROMA (not granulosa, not serous) + ascites + right hydrothorax
10. GTN WHO score ≥ 7 = EMA-CO; < 7 = methotrexate; choriocarcinoma failing MTX = always EMA-CO

### HPV Vaccine Recommendations for a Daughter of a Cervical Cancer Patient

**Q251 — A 54-year-old woman has advanced cervical cancer and a 14-year-old daughter. Advice for the daughter?**

- A. Advise HPV vaccine
- B. Screen for BRCA mutation
- C. Screen for PTEN mutation
- D. Perform cervical biopsy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Advise HPV vaccine** — Cervical cancer is caused by HPV, an environmentally acquired infection — it is NOT an inherited genetic cancer. BRCA screening (option B) applies to breast and ovarian cancer (hereditary risk). PTEN screening (option C) applies to endometrial/Cowden syndrome. Cervical biopsy in a 14-year-old (option D) without abnormal cytology or clinical abnormality would be unwarranted. The appropriate, evidence-based intervention for a 14-year-old at population-level risk of HPV exposure is HPV vaccination (2 doses at 0 and 6 months in the 9–14 age group, per WHO/India MoHFW guidelines). This question tests both the understanding that cervical cancer is viral (not genetic) in origin AND the practical vaccination schedule.

</details>
